#                 --device none  pp1419 259.50           tg64 39.08
#                 -ngl 99        pp1419 1781.44          tg64 56.55
#                 -ngl 0         pp110 741.57, the figure that says why the
#                                command names --device none
#
#   The share count asked again on the grouped job, three alternated rounds:
#   five reads 1.782 s generating for 9.25 s of processor time against four's
#   1.830 for 7.79. The gate for those six readings was a load under 1.10
#   rather than the usual 0.70, because another build had the machine for the
#   afternoon; alternated with every reading of one arm below every reading
#   of the other, which is what the comparison rests on.
#
#   The scaling group's figures come from `tests benchmark` in the same
#   sitting.
#
# 2026-09-03, the sitting six groups were retaken in, after the inline floor.
# Medians of three, load gated under 0.65 before each reading, llama-bench
# -r 3 on the same host, llama.cpp at 95b8e33e1, TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0.
#
#   The engine's own figures, twelve tokens from the six-token prompt:
#
#     0.376 s wall, 0.051 s evaluating, 0.324 s generating, 1.96 s of
#     processor time, loading 0.072 s
#     the same at --arith f32:  1.216 s for 9.54 s of processor time
#     the same at fifteen threads: 0.464 s for 2.62 s
#
#   The reference backend, four tokens:
#
#     cpu       0.052 s evaluating, 0.107 s generating
#     reference 5.973 s evaluating, 3.908 s generating
#
#   which is sixty-two times in total, a hundred and fifteen on the prompt
#   and thirty-six on the generation, the two printing the same digest. The
#   benchmark's serial comparison in the same sitting reads 2.35x for q8_0,
#   2.27x for q4_k and 2.94x for f32.
#
#   The device backend, the same twelve-token run and the 110-token one:
#
#     6 tokens, 12 generated   cpu 0.377 s wall, 0.051 evaluating, 0.326
#                              generating, 1.98 s of processor time
#                              device 0.262, 0.032, 0.229, 0.07
#     110 tokens, none generated  cpu 0.356 s for 2.75 s; device 0.102 for
#                              0.02
#
#   Drafting, twelve tokens each:
#
#     the model at eight bits          0.376 s
#     the same model at two bits       1.489 s
#     the first drafted by the second  3.138 s, 24 proposed, 7 accepted,
#                                      2.800 s of it generating
#
#   The llama.cpp comparison, both prompt lengths:
#
#     ours     110 tokens  processor 0.322 s   device 0.068 s
#              1419        processor 4.797     device 0.821
#              64 generated processor 1.748    device 1.238
#
#     llama-bench --device none  pp110 341.29 +/- 24.97  tg64 39.59
#                 -ngl 99        pp110 1583.34 +/- 54.83 tg64 56.75
#                 --device none  pp1419 261.87           tg64 39.04
#                 -ngl 99        pp1419 1883.19          tg64 56.73
#                 -ngl 0         pp110 756.38
#
#   Which gives, at 110 tokens: processor 341.6 t/s against 341.29, level;
#   device 1617.6 against 1583.34, ahead. At 1419: processor 295.8 against
#   261.87, ahead by 1.13; device 1728.4 against 1883.19, 1.09 behind.
#   Generating: processor 36.6 against 39.59, 1.08; device 51.7 against
#   56.75, 1.10.
#
#   The device rows swing between sittings and the file should say so rather
#   than pick the flattering one. llama-bench's device pp110 has read 1579.1,
#   1640.8 and 1583.3 on three afternoons and its pp1419 1823.2, 1781.4 and
#   1883.2; this program's have read 1571.4, 1549.3, 1617.6 and 1726.3,
#   1747.5, 1728.4. The claim those rows carry is that the two are close, not
#   which side of close either lands on.
#
#   The share-scaling figures, `tests benchmark`, pinned with
#   `taskset -c 0-7`: eight shares 13623 Me/s against seven at 12367 with one
#   vector a pass, ten per cent; batched, 23168 at eight against 21651 at
#   seven, seven per cent. Unpinned: 13037 against 12325 with one vector, six
#   per cent, and 22427 against 22746 batched, one and a half below.
#
# 2026-09-02, the pool is not woken for a job smaller than waking it -- and
# most of what this was aimed at was already not waking it.
#
#   THE PREMISE WAS HALF WRONG, which is the part worth keeping. The ranking
#   that led here said five per cent of a generated run is normalizing,
#   rotating, attending and joining, each of them a few microseconds of
#   arithmetic posted as a job of the pool. Four of those five were already
#   not posted: the layer's small work takes
#
#     Team : constant Workers_CPU.Pool_Reference :=
#       (if Count >= 16 then Item.Team else null);
#
#   and a null pool means the calling task does the whole of it. The comment
#   above that line says so in as many words, two lines above the call I had
#   decided to change. That is the fourth time this file has recorded a
#   ranked item whose premise was in the source and unread.
#
#   WHAT WAS LEFT is attention, which is shared by heads and reaches the pool
#   through Item.Team with no such test. A generated token early in a
#   conversation wakes five workers twenty-two times to divide a cache of
#   seventy positions between them.
#
#   So Dispatch_Shares takes a Cost -- the arithmetic of the whole job, in
#   elements -- and the submitting task does the job alone below a floor.
#   Bit-exact by construction: every item of these jobs is independent of
#   every other and a share is a range of them, so one task over the whole
#   range computes what the shares would have, element for element. The
#   single-token attention says its cost is Heads * Reserved * Head_Size:
#   143 thousand early in a conversation, 3.0 million after a long prompt.
#
#   Three alternated rounds, load under 0.90 before each, medians of three:
#
#     attending      0.027 s against 0.053     halved
#     generating     1.748  against 1.780      1.8 per cent
#     1419 prompt    4.811, 4.763 against 4.814, 4.813   level
#
#   THE FLOOR IS BRACKETED rather than reasoned. Generating sixty-four tokens
#   after the 1419-token prompt, where the cost is three million and the job
#   must not be taken inline:
#
#     floor of one million     1.953 s generating
#     floor of eight million   2.383
#     no floor at all          1.919
#
#   Eight million is twenty-four per cent worse, and one million is what no
#   floor is -- which is the point, since at that context the two run the
#   same code. The constant has to sit between the context a token wants
#   inline and the one it does not, and those three readings say it does.
#
#   AND A FIRST ATTEMPT THAT WAS WRONG, kept here because the shape of the
#   error is the useful part. The cost was passed at the five
#   position-shared sites as well, with the same floor. A prompt is read in
#   batches of a hundred and twenty-eight, and a batch's normalization is
#   128 * 2048 = 262 thousand elements -- under a floor of a million, so it
#   was taken inline, and that is real work rather than a job too small to
#   share. The long prompt read 5.011 s against 4.891. Narrowed to the one
#   site that needed it, and the position sites left as they were, because
#   the count test above already covers them.
#
#   Digests unchanged: 3248ac1bb7011de0 generating, 1a26d24d33b8957b at 1419,
#   7ec6b755e53e16b4 for the long prompt with sixty-four generated after it.
#
# load:  under 0.90 before every reading
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-09-03, where a generated token goes on the device, and four roads that
# are closed. Nothing in the program changed; this is a measurement and the
# reasons not to make four changes.
#
#   THE PREMISE WAS WRONG. The ranking said a generated token on the device
#   is mostly dispatch launches -- sixteen steps a layer, twenty-two layers,
#   about three hundred and fifty launches at the nine microseconds the
#   prompt's floor measurement gives. It is not.
#
#   The floor, every shader returning at once:
#
#     nothing changed      1.211 s for 64 tokens
#     every kernel voided  0.094
#
#   Seven and eight tenths per cent is the host, the submissions and the
#   launches -- and that is an upper bound on what they cost rather than
#   what they cost, because the host is only twenty-seven per cent busy
#   during a real run and its work overlaps the device's. The experiment
#   below settles which.
#
#   THE BUDGET, by removal, sixty-four generated tokens:
#
#     nothing changed          1.211 s
#     row_product voided       0.129      so the row product is 89.3 %
#     attention voided         1.221      nothing
#     norm voided              1.208      nothing
#     combine voided           1.210      nothing
#     rotate voided            1.211      nothing
#
#   A generated token on this device is one shader and a rounding error.
#   Everything the ranking proposed cutting -- the cache writes, the
#   rotations, the normalizations -- is inside the eleven per cent that is
#   not the row product, and most of that eleven is the floor.
#
#   FOUR ROADS, all closed by measurement.
#
#   One, the fence. The wait asks vkGetFenceStatus up to a thousand times
#   before it blocks, and on this driver every one of those is a system
#   call: strace counts 60,909 ioctls for sixty-four generated tokens, 86
#   per cent of them failed waits, which is 894 a token. Removing the spin
#   entirely takes that to 7,075 with none failing -- and the run gets
#   SLOWER, 1.249 s against 1.219. The spin is buying latency and paying for
#   it in system calls, and the trade is the right way round.
#
#   Two, throttling the spin. Two thousand and forty-eight pause
#   instructions between one asking and the next takes the ioctls from
#   60,909 to 28,126, and the run reads 1.214 s against 1.228 -- inside the
#   noise. Which is the same lesson twice: the system calls are not what the
#   waiting costs.
#
#   Three, the row shader's width. Eight lanes to a row is a constant with
#   an Ada twin, and neither had been swept:
#
#     four lanes    1.330 s
#     eight         1.140
#     sixteen       1.137
#
#   Eight and sixteen are level and four is much worse. The constant was
#   already right, and now it has been asked.
#
#   Four, the arithmetic in that shader. Keeping every load and doing a
#   quarter of the arithmetic -- one of the four bytes of each word decoded
#   and multiplied instead of all four -- reads 1.185 s against 1.212, two
#   and two tenths per cent. THE SHADER IS MEMORY-BOUND: three quarters of
#   what it computes is free. So an integer dot product, a wider fused
#   multiply-add, a cheaper decode -- none of them has anything to win.
#
#   WHAT IS LEFT. The row product moves 1.09 GiB a token in about seventeen
#   milliseconds, and llama.cpp does the same bytes in about fifteen and a
#   half. Seven per cent, on a shader that is waiting for memory, is not
#   about what it computes but about how the bytes reach it -- occupancy,
#   how much of a cache line a wave uses, how many rows are in flight. That
#   is a project rather than a constant, and it is the only thing left on
#   this row.
#
#   AND THE OTHER ITEM THIS SITTING WAS FOR. The device's 110-token prompt
#   was ranked at 1.06 behind and read ahead in the sitting of 2026-09-03 --
#   1617.6 against 1583.3. It is closed by measurement rather than by work,
#   and the honest reading is the one the README already carries: that row
#   swings between afternoons on both sides and what it says is that the two
#   are close.
#
# load:  under 0.80 before every reading
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-09-03, the row shader's lane sweep finished, and three ways of not
# paying for a misaligned block. Nothing in the program changed.
#
#   THE SWEEP, which stopped at sixteen last time and is now complete.
#   Sixty-four generated tokens on the device, alternated:
#
#     four lanes a row     1.330 s
#     eight                1.140 / 1.239
#     sixteen              1.137
#     thirty-two           1.257
#     sixty-four           1.262
#
#   The idea behind asking the wide end was that at sixty-four lanes a wave
#   holds exactly one row -- two thousand and forty-eight elements is
#   sixty-four blocks, so each lane takes one thirty-four-byte block and the
#   wave reads the row's 2176 bytes as one contiguous run. It is not faster.
#   Eight and sixteen are the plateau, four is much worse, and the wide end
#   is one to two per cent behind: what a wave asks for in one instruction is
#   the same set of addresses either way, and what changes is the reduction
#   at the end of the row and how many workgroups there are.
#
#   THE MISALIGNMENT IS REAL AND WORTH FIVE PER CENT. A Q8_0 block is
#   thirty-four bytes, so half the blocks of a row begin two bytes into a
#   word and the eight words each of those wants are made of two words each.
#   Pretending every block is word-aligned -- wrong answers, right shape --
#   reads 1.170 s against 1.235, five and three tenths per cent. That is most
#   of the seven per cent this row is behind llama.cpp.
#
#   THREE WAYS OF COLLECTING IT, all measured, none kept.
#
#   Carrying the word across the loop. The word a turn takes second is the
#   one the next turn takes first, so eight words should cost nine loads and
#   not sixteen. Written explicitly, five alternated rounds read 1.260 s
#   against 1.253 -- level. The compiler was already doing it, which is what
#   this measurement was for.
#
#   Deciding the alignment once a lane rather than once a block. A lane takes
#   every eighth block and thirty-four times eight is a whole number of
#   words, so every block a lane touches begins at the same offset into its
#   word: the alternation is between lanes and not along a lane. Hoisted into
#   two specialized loops it reads 1.533 s against 1.236 -- a quarter slower.
#   The body is eight accumulations a word where the batch reads eight
#   vectors, and two copies of it cost more in what the shader will hold than
#   the test costs to make. Left as one loop with the test invariant, it is
#   level.
#
#   So the five per cent is the ninth load and the shifting together, and
#   neither can be taken out of the shader without changing what it reads.
#   What would take it out is a layout: the device copies every matrix on
#   upload anyway, and a copy that put a row's scales in one run and its
#   quants in another would give the same bytes with every word aligned. That
#   is the next thing on this row, and it is a change to the upload and to
#   both shaders rather than a constant.
#
# load:  under 1.20 before every reading, alternated with every reading of
#          one arm beside the other
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-09-03, the split layout built, measured and reverted. The five per
# cent the alignment probe promised is not collectable, and this is why.
#
#   THE CORRECTION THAT MADE IT WORTH BUILDING. The entry before this one
#   said the layout change would cost the zero-copy import -- reading the
#   weights where the file has them. It would not: Device_Share is False by
#   default and sharing is opt-in, documented as slower and there for a
#   machine that cannot hold the model twice. By default the device copies
#   every matrix it uses, so rearranging during that copy is free of memory
#   and nearly free of time.
#
#   WHAT WAS BUILT. Per row rather than per tensor, which is what keeps it
#   small: a row's scales in front of its quants, two bytes a block and then
#   thirty-two. Where the block count is even the scales take a whole number
#   of words, so the row is the same length it was and nothing about how much
#   room a matrix needs changes -- and every quant word begins on a word. A
#   push constant tells the shaders which arrangement they have, so a shared
#   matrix still reads the file's own. About a hundred lines across the
#   engine and both shaders, and every digest held: 448c2ed68ec342ee
#   generating, 1a26d24d33b8957b and cbf29ce484222325 on the prompts.
#
#   WHAT IT MEASURED. Four alternated rounds each, medians of three:
#
#     generating 64   split 1.202 s   as it was 1.226   2.0 per cent faster
#     1419 prompt     split 0.860     as it was 0.836   2.9 per cent slower
#
#   Two per cent of the five on the row shader, and a loss on the tile that
#   is larger. Net worse, and worse on the row that is further behind, so it
#   goes back.
#
#   WHY THE FIVE PER CENT DOES NOT ARRIVE. The probe that promised it -- read
#   every block as though it were word-aligned -- removed a load and added
#   nothing. The layout removes that load and adds another: the scale is no
#   longer in the same thirty-four bytes as the quants it belongs to, so
#   every block now touches two streams instead of one. On the row shader the
#   two roughly cancel and two per cent is what is left. On the tile shader
#   there was nothing to win in the first place -- it was measured at
#   0.843 s against 0.814 with the misalignment pretended away, which is to
#   say the aligned version is SLOWER there -- and the second stream is a
#   straight cost.
#
#   So the thirty-four-byte block costs what it costs and the cost is not
#   recoverable by moving the scale. What would recover it is a scale that
#   does not have to be read separately at all, which is a different
#   quantization rather than a different arrangement of this one.
#
#   AND THE TILE SHADER, ASKED THE OTHER QUESTION. Keeping every load and
#   doing a sixteenth of the decode reads 0.786 s against 0.813, three and
#   three tenths per cent. So the tile is not arithmetic-bound either: at
#   3.85 teraflops a second against the part's sixteen and a half it is at
#   twenty-three per cent of peak, and llama.cpp is at twenty-five, so
#   neither is anywhere near it and the difference between them is not the
#   decode.
#
# load:  under 1.10 before every reading, alternated
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-09-03, what the registers say, and two more roads closed. The guess
# this file has been carrying -- that what is left on the device rows is
# "occupancy, how much of a cache line a wave uses, how many rows are in
# flight" -- is now measured, and the first of the three is wrong.
#
#   READ OUT OF THE DISASSEMBLY. RADV_DEBUG=asm prints every pipeline this
#   program compiles; splitting the dump and taking the highest vector
#   register each shader names gives what each of them costs:
#
#     the tile product, both format sets     152 registers
#     the row product, one vector a pass     128
#     the row product, eight a pass          256
#     attention by matrix                    128
#
#   On this part a wave64 at a hundred and twenty-eight registers gets about
#   four waves to a SIMD where the file allows eight, so the row shader that
#   generates a token is at half the occupancy the hardware would give it.
#   That was the suspicion; here is what it is worth.
#
#   OCCUPANCY IS NOT THE LIMIT. The tile is compiled twice, once for the six
#   formats a published model is usually made of and once for the eight
#   others, and the row product is not -- it carries all fifteen in one
#   shader, and a register allocation is made for the worst branch and used
#   by every branch. Cutting it to the four formats this model needs takes
#   it from a hundred and twenty-eight registers to twenty-nine -- four
#   times the waves -- and sixty-four generated tokens read
#
#     twenty-nine registers   1.689 s
#     a hundred and twenty-eight  1.242
#
#   THIRTY-SIX PER CENT SLOWER with four times the occupancy. The registers
#   were not idle: they were holding loads the shader had issued and not yet
#   needed, and a wave with fewer of them has fewer reads outstanding. More
#   waves each asking for less is worse here than fewer waves each asking
#   for more, which is the opposite of the guess.
#
#   NOR IS MORE OF IT BY HAND. Two blocks a turn -- the same blocks in the
#   same order, so no digest moves, with the second block's loads not
#   waiting on the first's -- reads 1.351 s against 1.237, nine per cent
#   slower. The compiler's own arrangement at a hundred and twenty-eight
#   registers is a local optimum in both directions: it cannot be improved
#   by giving it fewer registers or by writing more parallelism into the
#   source.
#
#   So what is left on this row is not reachable from the shader's text. The
#   remaining candidates are the ones this entry did not test -- how much of
#   a cache line a wave uses, and what the driver's own scheduling does with
#   a workgroup this shape -- and neither is a change to the program.
#
# load:  under 1.20, all readings against the same binary in the same
#          minute
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-03, the share count asked a third time, on a pool that has changed
# twice since it was chosen. Nothing in the program changed.

#   Two structural things have landed since the sweep that picked five: a
#   layer's projections became one job of the pool rather than one each, and
#   the small work below a floor stopped being posted at all. Both change

#   was one and two tenths better and is now a tenth WORSE. Five today reads
#   better than eight did yesterday, for two thirds of the processor time
#   eight wanted.

#   What took it is the grouped job. A job is three matrices where it was
#   one, so the chunk counter has three times the tiles to hand out and the
#   straggler at the end of a job is a smaller share of what the job was.
#   That is what the sixth and the seventh and the eighth share were buying,
#   and it is now bought once for everybody.

#   longer seven eighths of what there is to take, it is nearly all of it.

#   The prompt is the control and is untouched, as it has to be -- the share
#   count caps the single-vector path only, and a batch keeps every share.

#   than the pool.

#   eight shares 12877 Me/s against seven at 12273 with one vector a pass,

# 2026-09-03, a share walks its own stretch of a job, and the pool's own
# structure turns out to cost one per cent rather than eight.
#
#   THE CORRECTION FIRST. The entry before this one ended by saying the
#   processor's remaining gap is "our barrier-per-product structure against
#   llama.cpp's single parallel region", which is a claim about size as well
#   as about shape and the size was never measured. Measured now, by removal:
#   with every product's share doing nothing at all and everything else left
#   standing,
#
#     sixty-four generated tokens   1.754 s -> 0.112
#     the 1419-token prompt         4.994   -> 1.268
#
#   Of the 0.112, the phase account says attention, the normalizations, the
#   rotations and the joins are about 0.09, so THE POOL'S OWN STRUCTURE IS
#   ABOUT ONE PER CENT of a generated token. A redesign of it would buy that
#   and no more. The prompt's 1.268 is the same story at a different scale:
#   attention is 0.918 of it and the structure is again a rounding error.
#
#   So the eight per cent is inside the products, and the products are the
#   memory, and what is left to change about the memory is what the workers
#   ask it for.
#
#   WHAT CHANGED. The chunk counter hands out tile numbers in order, so the
#   first workers to arrive all start at the front of a job and walk it a
#   tile apart -- one narrow stream of reads for the memory to serve. The
#   tiles are now numbered so that counter value v is tile
#   (v mod Runs) * Per + v / Runs, with Runs the share count: worker w takes
#   values w, w + Runs, w + 2 Runs, which are consecutive tiles of stretch w.
#   Each worker walks a contiguous stretch of its own and the memory sees as
#   many streams as there are workers, while the counter still balances --
#   a worker that finishes its stretch takes whatever is left wherever it is.
#
#   Nothing about a value changes: a chunk computes the same rows from the
#   same weights whenever it is taken, and no chunk is taken twice. Every
#   digest holds.
#
#   Four alternated rounds, medians of three, every reading of one arm below
#   every reading of the other:
#
#     generating 64   1.7365 s against 1.747   0.6 per cent
#     1419 prompt     4.831   against 4.837    level
#
#   And where it shows most is where there are most workers to spread: the
#   twelve-token run at fifteen threads goes from 0.464 s to 0.401 and its
#   processor time from 2.62 s to 2.44, which is fourteen per cent of the
#   case this program has always been worst at.
#
#   Two stretches a worker rather than one -- Runs twice the share count, so
#   a worker alternates between two -- is level: 1.743 s against 1.740. One
#   stretch is the answer, which is what the two-process reading predicted:
#   what the memory wants is a stream a core, not more streams than cores.
#
# load:  under 1.10 before every reading, alternated
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-09-03, the chunk grain re-asked after the stretches, and it stands.
#
#   The grain is how many rows a worker takes from the counter at a time. It
#   was swept once and set to the kernel's row tile, thirty-two, and what it
#   meant then was only how evenly the last worker finishes: every worker was
#   a tile apart from every other, so the run each of them walked was one
#   tile whatever this said. Since a share began walking a stretch of its
#   own the grain also decides how long that run is, which is the quantity
#   the memory turned out to care about. So it was asked again.
#
#   Alternated, medians of three, sixty-four generated tokens and the
#   1419-token prompt:
#
#     grain    generating   prompt
#     thirty-two   1.741 s   4.848 s
#     sixty-four   1.743     5.066
#     a hundred and twenty-eight  1.771   5.465
#
#   Thirty-two and sixty-four are level generating and thirty-two wins the
#   prompt outright: four and a half per cent at sixty-four and thirteen at a
#   hundred and twenty-eight. The longer run is worth less than the balance
#   it costs, and it costs most where there are most shares -- a batch keeps
#   every worker, so a coarse chunk leaves the last of eight holding a tenth
#   of the job.
#
#   THE READING NOT TO TAKE. One of the three at sixty-four read 1.672 s,
#   four per cent below both of its own arm's other two and below everything
#   else measured that afternoon. It is a window and not a figure, and it is
#   written down here because this file has taken one of those before and
#   published it.
#
#   WHAT CHANGED IN THE SOURCE, AND WHY NO FIGURE MOVES. The grain now has a
#   name of its own -- Chunk_Grain -- rather than being the row tile read at
#   four call sites. The two are still the same number, and they have to be a
#   multiple of each other, because a share boundary that did not fall where
#   the tile does would make the answer depend on how the work was divided.
#   Naming it is what keeps somebody changing the tiling from silently
#   changing the grain.
#
#   That is a rename and a comment, and it moves the fingerprint of six
#   groups without moving anything a figure could see. Checked rather than
#   asserted: the two binaries disassemble to 1,523,805 instructions each,
#   and with addresses normalised every instruction and every operand is
#   identical -- the only differences in the raw disassembly are the
#   displacements of string constants that moved because the source text got
#   longer. So the groups are restamped and not re-measured, which is the
#   second of the two things this file's rule allows.
#
# load:  under 1.10 before every reading, alternated
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-09-03, the ceiling re-measured and the non-temporal prefetch, which is
# flat. Nothing in the program changed.
#
#   THE CEILING, asked again after a share began walking its own stretch:
#
#     one run alone      1.733 s for 64 tokens, 40.2 GB/s of the model
#     two runs at once   3.013 s each, 46.3 GB/s between them
#
#   It was 38.6 against 43.1 before the stretches. Both moved up and the gap
#   between them did not close: a single run still leaves about fifteen per
#   cent of what the part will deliver, and ten threads across two processes
#   still take it where five in one do not.
#
#   THE PREFETCH. A generated token reads the whole model once and reuses
#   none of it, so every one of those gigabytes evicts something that will be
#   wanted -- the activations, the cache of keys and values, the scales built
#   a moment ago. prefetcht0 was tried and was flat, and that instruction
#   fills every level; prefetchnta asks for the line and asks for it not to
#   be kept, which is a different question rather than the same one again.
#
#   Four alternated rounds, five hundred and twelve bytes ahead of the
#   cursor:
#
#     generating   1.7405 s against 1.741   level
#     1419 prompt  4.851   against 4.839    level
#
#   Flat both ways, so the prefetch question is closed from both sides: this
#   loop is not short of lines, it is short of somewhere to put the ones it
#   has asked for. Not kept.
#
# load:  under 1.10 before every reading, alternated
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-09-03, the driver will say what the occupancy is, and the claim in the
# entry two above is wrong.
#
#   RADV_DEBUG=shaderstats prints, for every pipeline this program builds,
#   what the entry two above tried to work out by hand from the highest
#   register a disassembly names. It refused to print the first time it was
#   asked and the reason was a wrong working directory, not the driver.
#
#     shader                      VGPRs  waves a SIMD  LDS   VMEM clauses
#     row product, eight a pass     256       4        8192      308
#     row product, one a pass       128       8        1024      540
#     attention by matrix           128       8        8192       72
#     the two tile products         168       6        3072    89, 104
#     the small kernels            8-80     12-32    0-3072     2-20
#
#   Nothing spills anywhere.
#
#   THE CORRECTION. That entry said "a wave64 at a hundred and twenty-eight
#   registers gets about four waves to a SIMD where the file would allow
#   eight, so the shader that generates a token runs at half the occupancy
#   the hardware offers". It runs at EIGHT of eight. The arithmetic was mine
#   and it was wrong, and the driver was willing to be asked the whole time.
#
#   What that entry measured is unaffected and its conclusion is unchanged,
#   but the reason moves. Cutting the row shader to four formats took it from
#   a hundred and twenty-eight registers to twenty-nine and made it
#   thirty-six per cent slower; the entry read that as "more waves each
#   asking for less is worse than fewer waves each asking for more". It is
#   simpler than that: it bought NO waves at all, because there were already
#   as many as a SIMD will hold, and it spent registers that were holding
#   loads. Occupancy was never the thing to buy, which is the same
#   conclusion by a shorter road.
#
#   And the tile products sit at six of eight, which is the one place a
#   register saved could still buy a wave. Sixteen registers fewer would do
#   it. That is the only occupancy left on this device and it is worth
#   something like a twelfth of the shader's waves.
#
#   This is also the answer to the item that said the device needs a
#   profiling capture. It does not: the driver reports the occupancy, the
#   register and memory pressure, the instruction mix, the memory clauses and
#   an estimated latency and throughput a wave, and none of that needs a
#   trace file or a tool to read one.
#
# 2026-09-03, the share count asked after the stretches too, and the fifteen
# per cent named. Nothing in the program changed.
#
#   The stretches gave every share a contiguous run of its own, which is the
#   one thing that could have made a sixth or a seventh worth having: before
#   them an added share was another worker a tile apart from the rest, and
#   after them it is another stream. So the count was asked once more, on a
#   finer grid than before.
#
#   Three alternated rounds, medians of three, sixty-four generated tokens:
#
#     shares   generating   processor time
#     five        1.738 s      8.94 s
#     six         1.736       10.56
#     seven       1.7345      12.16
#     eight       1.753       13.92
#
#   Five, six and seven are within two tenths of a per cent of each other and
#   eight is nine tenths worse. The processor time rises in a straight line
#   -- one and six tenths of a second a share -- so the added workers do work
#   and buy no wall time at all. Beyond five shares an added worker consumes
#   the bandwidth rather than adding to it, and the stretches did not change
#   that. Five stands, for the fourth time and now against a grid with no gap
#   in it.
#
#   AND THAT NAMES THE FIFTEEN PER CENT. The part will deliver 46.3 GB/s --
#   two runs at once take it -- and one run takes 40.2 however its five to
#   eight workers are arranged. Every arrangement inside one process has now
#   been asked: more shares, more streams a share, a longer run each, the
#   chunks in a different order, the small work off the pool, the projections
#   in one job, both kinds of prefetch, two rows a turn in the kernel. The
#   ones that paid are recorded above and they add to about three per cent.
#
#   What two processes have that one does not is two dependency chains. A
#   token is a hundred and fifty-five products in a fixed order and a share
#   cannot start the next until every share has finished this one, so at each
#   of eighty-nine job boundaries the memory system drains and refills. Two
#   processes interleave two of those chains and keep it fed. That is not a
#   constant to tune or a loop to rewrite; it is what a single sequence of
#   dependent products is worth on this part, and the way to have the other
#   fifteen per cent is to have a second sequence -- another session served at
#   the same time, or a draft model checked beside the target, both of which
#   this program can already hold.
#
# load:  under 1.10 before every reading, alternated
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-09-03, what a second sequence is actually worth. Nothing in the
# program changed; this is the measurement the entry before this one argued
# for, and it is worth much more than the entry guessed.
#
#   That entry ended: the way to have the other fifteen per cent is to have a
#   second sequence. It is not fifteen per cent. A second sequence in the
#   same pass does not use the bandwidth better -- it NEEDS LESS OF IT, because
#   two tokens come out of one reading of the weights.
#
#   Measured with --batch-size, which is a real option of the command and of
#   the speed tool, on the 110-token prompt so that a batch of one is
#   affordable. A batch of one is a token at a time and reads 27.7
#   milliseconds a token, which is the generating figure to a tenth.
#
#     batch   processor            device
#         1   27.7 ms a token      18.8 ms a token
#         2   17.8   1.56x         13.6   1.38x
#         3   13.5   2.06
#         4    8.6   3.23           7.6   2.48x
#         6    8.9   3.10
#         8    5.8   4.79           3.8   4.92x
#        16    4.1   6.75
#        32    3.6   7.63           2.1   9.02x
#       128    3.0   9.30           0.65 29.2x
#
#   So two sequences served in one pass produce tokens at one and a half
#   times the rate of two served in turn on the processor, and four sequences
#   at three and a quarter. On the device the same four are two and a half
#   times and eight are nearly five.
#
#   THE KINK AT THREE AND SIX. A batch of three costs nearly what four does
#   and a batch of six costs MORE in total than four (0.984 s against 0.944).
#   The strip kernel takes vectors in fours, so a batch that is not a
#   multiple of four leaves lanes idle for the whole pass, and six is one
#   full strip and a half-empty one where four is one full strip. A scheduler
#   that gathers sequences should gather them in fours.
#
#   Every one of these prints the same digest, 8ca534de63ff96ac, at nine
#   batch sizes on the processor and six on the device. Batching changes no
#   answer, which this page has claimed for a long time on one comparison and
#   which is now shown across fifteen.
#
#   WHAT THE PROGRAM HAS AND WHAT IT LACKS. It can hold any number of
#   sessions on one prepared model -- a model carries no per-evaluation
#   state, and there is a test that interleaves two sessions a token at a
#   time and checks each gets what it would have got alone. What nothing does
#   is put two sessions' next tokens into one pass. That is the feature these
#   figures price, and they price it at half again as many tokens a second
#   for two callers and three and a quarter times for four.
#
#   It is a scheduler, a batch whose rows come from different sessions at
#   different positions, and a sampler run per row. None of that is a
#   constant to tune, and all of it is worth more than everything this file
#   has measured this week put together.
#
# load:  under 1.10, medians of three
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-09-03, the sitting five groups were retaken in, after the round.
# Medians of three, load gated, llama-bench -r 3 on the same host, llama.cpp
# at 95b8e33e1, TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0.
#
#   The engine's own figures, twelve tokens from the six-token prompt:
#     0.373 s wall, 0.050 s evaluating, 0.322 s generating, 1.96 s of
#     processor time, loading 0.076 s
#     at --arith f32:  1.202 s for 9.43 s of processor time
#     at fifteen threads: 0.442 s for 2.68 s
#
#   The reference backend, four tokens: cpu 0.052 s evaluating and 0.108 s
#   generating; reference 5.957 and 3.925. Sixty-two times in total, a
#   hundred and fifteen on the prompt and thirty-six on the generation.
#
#   The device backend:
#     6 tokens, 12 generated   cpu 0.371 s wall, 0.051 evaluating, 0.320
#                              generating, 1.96 s of processor time
#                              device 0.281, 0.032, 0.244, 0.08
#     110 tokens, none generated  cpu 0.359 s for 2.77 s; device 0.100 for
#                              0.02
#
#   Drafting, twelve tokens each: the model at eight bits 0.373 s, the same
#   model at two bits 1.486, the first drafted by the second 3.138 with 24
#   proposed and 7 accepted, 2.805 s of it generating.
#
#   The llama.cpp comparison:
#     ours     110 tokens  processor 0.321 s   device 0.069 s
#              1419        processor 5.130     device 0.815
#              64 generated processor 1.747    device 1.235
#
#     llama-bench --device none  pp110 351.55 +/- 16.67  tg64 39.30
#                 -ngl 99        pp110 1647.11           tg64 56.33
#                 --device none  pp1419 263.43           tg64 39.35
#                 -ngl 99        pp1419 1803.68          tg64 56.74
#                 -ngl 0         pp110 741.11
#
#   Which gives, at 110 tokens: processor 342.7 t/s against 351.55, 1.03
#   behind; device 1594.2 against 1647.11, 1.03. At 1419: processor 276.6
#   against 263.43, ahead by 1.05; device 1741.1 against 1803.68, 1.04.
#   Generating: processor 36.6 against 39.30, 1.07; device 51.8 against
#   56.33, 1.09.
#
#   The 1419-token processor row is lower than the sitting before -- 276.6
#   against 294.6 -- and the reading behind it carried a load that rose to
#   1.77 during the run where the one before it settled at 0.72. It is a
#   window rather than the round, which touches nothing a batch of one
#   session's positions does: the same procedure with Beside empty is the
#   same code, and the prompt digests are unchanged. Worth retaking before
#   anything is concluded from it.
#
# 2026-09-03, stage one of the round: several sequences in one pass, on the
# processor, and it delivers more than it was priced at.
#
#   Sixty-four rounds from a seven-token prompt, one token a member a round:
#
#     members   a token   tokens a second   against one
#          1     29.3 ms       34.1            1.00x
#          2     17.4          57.3            1.68x
#          3     13.5          73.8            2.16x
#          4      8.7         114.7            3.36x
#          6      9.3         107.3            3.14x
#          8      5.4         185.0            5.42x
#
#   Priced at 1.56x for two and 3.23x for four from the batch-size sweep; it
#   pays 1.68 and 3.36. The sweep was a prompt's own positions and a round is
#   different sequences, and the round is slightly the better of the two --
#   a prompt's rows attend a growing history where a round's attend their
#   own, so a round's attention is the same work a row every time.
#
#   AND THE KINK IS IN THE THING ITSELF. Six members read 107.3 tokens a
#   second where four read 114.7: SIX MEMBERS ARE SLOWER THAN FOUR. That is
#   the strip kernel taking vectors in fours, predicted from the batch-size
#   sweep before the round existed and now measured in the round. A scheduler
#   gathers in fours because of this table.
#
#   For scale, llama.cpp generates one sequence at 39.3 tokens a second on
#   this host. Four members is two and nine tenths of that and eight is four
#   and seven tenths -- which is not a comparison of like with like, and is
#   the point: nobody serving several callers should be doing it one at a
#   time.
#
#   WHAT IT COST TO BUILD. The primitive is Evaluate_Batch with two answers
#   made per row instead of once -- which cache a row attends and where in it
#   the row sits -- and the four substitutions the design named. The rest of
#   that procedure is untouched: the products were already over the whole
#   batch and the normalizations, the rotation, the gated middle and the
#   joins were already a row at a time. One addition beyond the design: the
#   final projection over the vocabulary is done for all rows at once for a
#   round, because a row at a time there would give back a fifteenth of what
#   the layers just saved.
#
#   THE GATE. Every member of a round produces, bit for bit, the logits it
#   would have produced alone -- two sequences that differ, stepped together,
#   compared step for step against themselves run alone. The suite is 295
#   tests and the sweep 28344 sequences with none outside tolerance.
#
#   Three error codes came off the reserved list, because a round refuses a
#   member that is closed, a member that has failed, and a shape whose tokens
#   and members do not agree. Two of the three are reached by tests; the
#   third wants a failure part way through a forward pass, which nothing here
#   can arrange, and it is on the unreached list with that reason.
#
#   Not on a device: the sequence a device layer is built from names one
#   cache and one range of positions for the whole batch, so a round there
#   would have every row attend the first member's history. Refused by name.
#   That is stage two.
#
#   WHY NO FIGURE MOVES. The fix is inside Speed_Run.Round, lines 576 to 835
#   of the measuring tool, and nothing published is taken with --round: every
#   figure in this file comes from Speed_Run.Run or from the benchmark. The
#   five groups are restamped rather than re-measured for that reason, which
#   is the second of the two things this file's rule allows -- and the
#   argument is checkable, since the whole diff is twenty-two lines inside
#   one procedure that only --round reaches.
#
# load:  under 0.90 before every reading
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-09-03, the sitting five groups were retaken in, after stage two.
# Medians of three, load gated, llama-bench -r 3, llama.cpp at 95b8e33e1.
#
#   The engine, twelve tokens from the six-token prompt: 0.376 s wall,
#   0.052 evaluating, 0.324 generating, 1.95 s of processor time, loading
#   0.065 s. At --arith f32: 1.194 s for 9.40 s. At fifteen threads: 0.426 s
#   for 2.55 s.
#
#   The reference backend, four tokens: cpu 0.053 s and 0.107 s; reference
#   5.951 and 3.929. Sixty-one times in total, a hundred and twelve on the
#   prompt and thirty-seven on the generation.
#
#   The device backend: six tokens and twelve generated, cpu 0.384 s wall,
#   0.059 evaluating, 0.323 generating, 1.97 s of processor time; device
#   0.262, 0.034, 0.229, 0.08. A 110-token prompt with nothing generated,
#   cpu 0.357 s for 2.72 s; device 0.097 for 0.02.
#
#   Drafting: the model at eight bits 0.376 s, at two bits 1.496, the first
#   drafted by the second 3.152 with 24 proposed and 7 accepted, 2.823 s of
#   it generating.
#
#   The llama.cpp comparison:
#     ours  110 tokens  processor 0.307 s  device 0.073 s
#           1419        processor 5.070    device 0.817
#           64 generated processor 1.761   device 1.202
#
#     llama-bench --device none  pp110 332.57 +/- 30.92  tg64 39.58
#                 -ngl 99        pp110 1660.25          tg64 56.92
#                 --device none  pp1419 262.34          tg64 38.85
#                 -ngl 99        pp1419 1824.14         tg64 56.88
#                 -ngl 0         pp110 730.33
#
#   Which gives, at 110 tokens: processor 358.3 t/s against 332.57, ahead by
#   1.08; device 1506.8 against 1660.25, 1.10 behind. At 1419: processor
#   279.9 against 262.34, ahead by 1.07; device 1736.8 against 1824.14, 1.05
#   behind. Generating: processor 36.3 against 39.58, 1.09; device 53.2
#   against 56.92, 1.07.
#
#   The 110-token processor row is ahead for the second time in five
#   sittings and behind in the other three; llama-bench's own spread on that
#   row is thirty tokens a second. The rows that repeat are the long prompt
#   and the two generating ones.
#
# 2026-09-03, stage two of the round: a device runs its products and not its
# attention, and it is the faster of the two at every count.
#
#   What changed is two refusals. The fused half-layer -- the one that
#   normalizes, projects, rotates, writes the cache and attends in a single
#   submission -- names one cache and one run of positions for the whole
#   batch, so a round taking it would have every row attend the first
#   member's history. A round takes the unfused path instead: every product
#   goes to the device, the device's own copy of the cache is not written,
#   and attention runs on the host where each row already has its own. That
#   is what stage two was designed to be, and attention is three per cent of
#   a generated token, so most of the saving survives the arrangement.
#
#   Sixty-four rounds from a seven-token prompt, both backends:
#
#     members   processor            device
#           1   29.4 ms   34.1 t/s   20.2 ms   49.5 t/s
#           2   17.6      56.9       17.2      58.0
#           3   13.4      74.9       12.0      83.4
#           4    8.4     119.1        9.6     103.9
#           6    8.9     111.9        7.7     130.1
#           8    5.4     183.6        5.0     198.8
#
#   The device is ahead at every count and gains less from each added member
#   -- four times over one at eight members against the processor's five and
#   a half -- which is what putting attention on the host costs: a device
#   round pays it once a member on a processor that is otherwise idle, and
#   the more members there are the more of the round it is.
#
#   A single member on the device reads 20.2 ms a token where the fused path
#   reads 19.3, so the unfused arrangement costs about five per cent for one
#   sequence. Two members already pay for it twice over.
#
#   THE CHECK THAT MATTERS, AND IT IS A NEW ONE. The round driver digests
#   every token every member chose, in order. Greedy from the same prompt is
#   the same text, so the two backends must print the same mark -- and they
#   do, at every count measured: bf82006c92ec1fa3 at one member,
#   09bc06356d355071 at two, 3e2394729fe53cb7 at three, 32adae0fef628b85 at
#   four, 895b17fc753f5bc1 at six and e581a08b8bf861d5 at eight. A device
#   round says exactly what a processor round says, which is the property
#   the whole thing would be worthless without and which nothing else in the
#   suite would have caught: the conformance sweep runs evaluations, not
#   rounds.
#
#   Stage three is the per-row cache table in the attention step, which
#   would let the fused half-layer come back and take the host out of a
#   device round entirely.
#
# load:  under 0.90 before every reading
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-09-03, what stage three is worth, and the long context that says so.
# The round figures published so far were taken at a context of seventy-one
# positions, and they look very different at fourteen hundred.
#
#   A BUG FOUND ON THE WAY. The round driver handed a whole prompt to one
#   call of the batched evaluation. A batch is bounded by Max_Batch, which is
#   five hundred and twelve, so a 1419-token prompt was past it and a round
#   with a long prompt refused with a shape mismatch. It reads the prompt in
#   batches of a hundred and twenty-eight now, which is what generation does.
#   Nothing published was measured with it: every round figure before this
#   used a seven-token prompt.
#
#   THE SAME ROUND AT A LONG CONTEXT. Thirty-two rounds after a 1419-token
#   prompt, on the device:
#
#     members   a token   tokens a second   against one
#           1    21.9 ms      45.7            1.00x
#           2    20.6         48.6            1.06x
#           4    14.5         69.0            1.51x
#           8    12.2         82.1            1.80x
#
#   Against 1.17x, 2.10x and 4.02x at seventy-one positions. The round's
#   advantage more than halves, and the reason is the arrangement stage two
#   chose: a device round runs its products on the device and its attention
#   on the host, and attention grows with the square of the context while the
#   products do not grow at all.
#
#   PRICED BY REMOVAL. A round's attention made to do nothing -- wrong
#   answers, right shape -- at the same long context:
#
#     members   as it is   attention voided   attention's share
#           2    1.316 s        1.105              16 per cent
#           4    1.855          1.230              34
#           8    3.117          1.567              50
#
#   AT EIGHT MEMBERS AND A LONG CONTEXT, HALF THE ROUND IS HOST ATTENTION.
#   That is stage three's ceiling: eight members would go from 82.1 tokens a
#   second to about 163. At the short context the same measurement reads
#   nothing, nothing and ten per cent -- so stage two's arrangement is free
#   where the context is short and costs half where it is long, and that is
#   the honest qualification of the figures above it.
#
#   AND WHAT STAGE THREE HAS TO BE, which this measurement also settled.
#
#   A decode round never reaches the matrix attention shader: that one wants
#   sixteen rows and a round has as many rows as members. So stage three is
#   one shader, attention.comp, in its three compilations -- not two.
#
#   The per-row numbers are three and two of them derive. In a decode round
#   the row IS the member, so a row's cache base is its row number times a
#   stride; and the window's start derives from the last position and the
#   window's width. Only the last position is genuinely a table.
#
#   There is room for it in the push constants. The attention block is
#   sixty-four bytes and every device offers a hundred and twenty-eight, so a
#   stride and eight last-positions fit -- which caps a device round's
#   attention at eight members, and eight is where the win is.
#
#   What is left is the device's cache: it is reserved for one session's keys
#   and values, and a round needs it reserved for the members and written
#   with a member's offset. That and Attend_And_Feed's interface are the
#   whole of the work.
#
# load:  under 0.90 before every reading
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-09-04, stage three of the round: a device runs a round's attention as
# well as its products, and eight members at a long context double.
#
#   WHAT WAS BUILT. The device holds one cache buffer laid out as one
#   session's keys and values. It is dealt out in blocks of one session's
#   worth now, and row i of a round reads block i -- which is all the kernel
#   has to be told, since it multiplies a row's number by the block width.
#   The attention kernel's push block carries that width and a table of eight
#   last positions; it went from sixty-four bytes to a hundred and
#   twenty-eight, and the pipeline layout's declared push range with it. A
#   round takes the compilation of attention.comp whose block is one query,
#   because a block of more reads a cached key once and dots it into every
#   query of the block, and rows of a round do not share a cache.
#
#   THE PUSH BLOCK. This file records that widening that block by one field
#   once corrupted this device's answers and that nobody explained it. It was
#   widened here deliberately, and the declared range was raised with it --
#   the older note about pushing sixty bytes past a declared range of
#   thirty-two is the likeliest account of the corruption, and this change is
#   the test of that reading. Every digest in the suite is unmoved, the
#   conformance sweep is at zero outside tolerance, and the round's mark is
#   the same on both backends at every count.
#
#   ALTERNATED WITH THE ARRANGEMENT IT REPLACES, medians of three each way,
#   on the device, both binaries built from the same tree:
#
#     1419-token prompt, 32 rounds     host      device
#       2 members                      2.024 s   1.572 s   1.29x
#       4 members                      2.915     1.969     1.48x
#       8 members                      5.658     2.802     2.02x
#
#     7-token prompt, 64 rounds
#       2 members                      2.756 s   2.477 s   1.11x
#       4 members                      3.343     2.776     1.20x
#       8 members                      4.571     3.271     1.40x
#
#   Eight members at fourteen hundred positions read 91.4 tokens a second
#   against 45.2. THE REMOVAL PRICED THAT ROW AT FIFTY PER CENT AND TAKING
#   IT AWAY DOUBLED THE ROUND, which is the rarest kind of entry here: a
#   price named before the work and met by it. The two rows beside it were
#   priced at sixteen and thirty-four per cent and pay twenty-nine and
#   forty-eight, so the prediction was low where it did not matter.
#
#   NOTHING OUTSIDE A ROUND MOVED. The ordinary device prompt reads 0.098 s
#   either way in three alternated pairs, and every published digest is what
#   it was. The extra sixty-four bytes a dispatch pushes are below what any
#   figure here resolves.
#
#   AND A BUG THE BUILDING FOUND. Blocks are asked for furthest-first. A
#   wider cache buffer is a new one and an empty one, so asking for block
#   zero and then block seven turns every row seated so far out of its block;
#   the layer loop repaired it by re-uploading each member's whole cache
#   every round, which was right and read a third slower -- eight members at
#   the long context 4.078 s against 2.802. Asking for the most first means
#   the growing happens before anything is seated.
#
#   Two sessions on one device also used to write over each other's keys in
#   that buffer, which nothing did and nothing caught. Blocks are the answer
#   to that as well: a session takes one and keeps it, and one turned out of
#   its block loses a copy and nothing else, because what the host holds is
#   the copy of record.
#
#   THE ROUND TABLES RETAKEN, one sitting, 64 rounds from a seven-token
#   prompt:
#
#     members   processor            device
#           1    29.7 ms   33.7 t/s   19.9 ms   50.3 t/s
#           2    18.6      53.7       17.7      56.6
#           3    13.9      72.0       12.4      80.5
#           4     8.8     113.3       10.0     100.1
#           6     9.7     103.4        7.7     130.4
#           8     5.7     175.6        6.6     151.6
#
#   The device column read 5.0 ms at eight members in the sitting stage two
#   was published from and would not go below 6.4 in this one -- on the
#   binary that produced it as well as on the one that replaced it. That is
#   a sitting, not a change, and it is why the alternated pairs above are
#   the evidence and this table is only the shape. At fourteen hundred
#   positions, on the device: one member 22.2 ms and 45.1 t/s, two 24.6 and
#   40.7, four 15.4 and 65.0, eight 10.9 and 91.4. One member is not a round
#   -- it takes the fused whole-layer path -- and two members do not beat it
#   at that context.
#
# load:  under 0.60 before each alternated set
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# Medians of three, load gated, llama-bench -r 3, llama.cpp at 95b8e33e1.

#   5.958 and 3.801. Sixty-one times in total, a hundred and fifteen on the

#

#   0.250, 0.035, 0.214, 0.07. A 110-token prompt with nothing generated,
#   cpu 0.355 s for 2.69 s; device 0.099 for 0.02.
#
#   Drafting: the model at eight bits 0.377 s, at two bits 1.397, the first
#   drafted by the second 3.228 with 24 proposed and 7 accepted, 2.903 s of
#   it generating and the draft's own generation 1.119 s.
#
#   The llama.cpp comparison:
#     ours  110 tokens  processor 0.307 s  device 0.070 s

#           64 generated processor 1.746   device 1.218

#   Which gives, at 110 tokens: processor 358.3 t/s against 371.34, 1.04
#   behind; device 1571.4 against 1538.30, ahead by 1.02. At 1419: processor
#   276.7 against 261.40, ahead by 1.06; device 1707.6 against 1760.44, 1.03
#   behind. Generating: processor 36.7 against 39.56, 1.08; device 52.5
#   against 56.18, 1.07.
#
#   The device's short prompt is ahead of llama.cpp for the first time and
#   the processor's is behind for the fourth time in six sittings. Neither is
#   a change to the code -- the device row of both tables is what stage three
#   left alone -- and llama-bench's own spread on that row is between sixteen
#   and thirty-five tokens a second. The rows that repeat are the long prompt
#   and the two generating ones.

# load:  under 0.60 before the first of them; the machine had settled at
#     57 C and llama-bench ran on the same pass, so the short prompt was not

# 2026-09-04, where a device round's time was going, and the block a session
# keeps. Two entries in one, because the second is what the first found.
#
#   THE QUESTION. A device round was behind a processor round at a long
#   context at every member count -- 91.3 tokens a second against 117.6 at
#   eight members, 65.5 against 88.0 at four -- with attention on the device
#   in both. A single sequence goes the other way, 52.5 against 36.7, so the
#   crossover was somewhere in the round and nothing said where.
#
#   THE PHASE CLOCK, which `tests speed --round N --budget` now reports as
#   the single-sequence measurement always has. Eight members, 32 rounds,
#   1419 positions, the round alone and not the prompt each member read:
#
#     processor              device
#       FEEDING     0.975 s    ATTENDING    1.302 s
#       ATTENDING   0.636      NORMALIZING  1.008
#       PROJECTING  0.323      PROJECTING   0.284
#       everything  0.204      everything   0.106
#
#   The device's FEEDING and JOINING are zero because the layer's whole
#   second half is one submission there, which ATTENDING covers. What is not
#   explained is NORMALIZING at thirty-seven per cent, where the processor's
#   reads 0.036 s of the same host code.
#
#   WHERE IT ACTUALLY WAS. Four probes, each a charge moved: the window is
#   not the normalization (0.032 s), not the layer boundary (0.000 s), not
#   the preamble (0.001 s), and not the attention's tail. It is the loop that
#   seats a round's rows, and IT DOES NOT GROW WITH THE ROUND COUNT: 0.976 s
#   at 32 rounds, 0.999 at 64, 0.976 at 128.
#
#   So it is a one-off, paid the first time a round forms, and it is every
#   member's whole cache written into the block its row number named -- a
#   hundred and thirty-eight megabytes a member, of which the conversion to
#   half precision is most of the cost. A member prefills alone, takes the
#   first block, and is then moved to the block its row wants.
#
#   Once seated, the device round was ahead all along: 55.7 ms a round
#   against the processor's 69.3 at eight members, which the 128-round
#   reading shows without any subtraction -- 8.273 s against 8.865.
#
#   WHAT WAS BUILT. A session takes a block the first time it writes to the
#   device's cache and keeps it until it closes. Rows read the blocks their
#   sessions were already in, so forming a round writes a table of two words
#   a row and nothing else. The table moved out of the push constants and
#   into the end of the cache, which is a buffer the kernel has bound
#   already: the push block held eight rows, and what bounds a round now is
#   memory -- sixteen blocks, two gigabytes for this model at two thousand
#   positions.
#
#   ALTERNATED, medians of three each way, device, 1419 positions, 32 rounds:
#
#     members   host attention   pushed table   blocks
#           2       2.024 s        1.587 s      1.292 s
#           4       2.915          1.965        1.477
#           8       5.658          2.777        1.783
#          16       --             8.115        3.444
#
#   Eight members read 143.6 tokens a second against 45.2 with attention on
#   the host. Sixteen read 148.7 against the pushed table's 62.8, which is
#   the cap: a round of more than eight attended on the host and was slower
#   than the same device at eight.
#
#   THE ROUND TABLES, one sitting, medians of three, milliseconds a token:
#
#     members   1419 positions        71 positions
#               processor  device    processor  device
#           1     32.0       22.1      29.9       20.6
#           2     21.4       20.0      18.3       18.5
#           4     11.3       11.3       8.9        9.9
#           8      8.6        7.0       5.8        5.2
#          16      7.1        6.5       4.7        4.6
#
#   TWO REPAIRS the building needed. A wider cache buffer came up empty and
#   turned every seated session out of its block; it carries its contents
#   over now, values and half-precision copy alike. And every place that
#   addresses the device's cache for a session adds that session's block --
#   the fused whole layer, the fused half layer, the single-call attention,
#   the readback -- which a cache with one tenant did not need. A session at
#   the sixteenth block also overflowed a Natural where the half-precision
#   copy's base is added; that addition is in sixty-four bits now.
#
#   The marks agree on both backends at every count from one to twenty-four,
#   above the block limit as well as below it: a round of seventeen attends
#   on the host and says what a round of sixteen says on the device.
#
# load:  under 0.60 before each alternated set
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

#   0.069 s. At --arith f32: 1.217 s for 9.64 s. At fifteen threads: 0.465 s
#   for 2.75 s.
#
#   The reference backend, four tokens: cpu 0.054 s and 0.107 s; reference
#   6.020 and 4.032. Sixty-two times in total, a hundred and eleven on the
#   prompt and thirty-eight on the generation.
#
#   The device backend: six tokens and twelve generated, cpu 0.376 s wall,
#   0.052 evaluating, 0.323 generating, 1.97 s of processor time; device
#   0.260, 0.033, 0.227, 0.07. A 110-token prompt with nothing generated,
#   cpu 0.356 s for 2.75 s; device 0.098 for 0.02.
#
#   Drafting: the model at eight bits 0.374 s, at two bits 1.508, the first
#   drafted by the second 3.380 with 24 proposed and 7 accepted, 3.028 s of
#   it generating and the draft's own generation 1.218 s.
#
#   The llama.cpp comparison:
#     ours  110 tokens  processor 0.336 s  device 0.070 s
#           1419        processor 5.104    device 0.829

#   behind; device 1571.4 against 1645.51, 1.05 behind. At 1419: processor
#   278.0 against 260.41, ahead by 1.07; device 1711.7 against 1758.24, 1.03
#   behind. Generating: processor 36.3 against 39.21, 1.08; device 52.6
#   against 56.16, 1.07.

# 2026-09-04, the scheduler over the round, and five fingerprints that moved
# for a procedure nothing published runs through.
#
#   WHAT WAS BUILT. Model_Runner.Serving: a queue of callers, a round taken
#   from the front of it, a sampler and a stop and a limit a member, and a
#   seat given back when one finishes. tests speed --serve N --callers M is
#   what exercises it, and speed_run.adb is where that lives -- which is why
#   every group listing the measuring tool went stale.
#
#   NO FIGURE MOVED AND NONE COULD. What changed in speed_run.adb is one new
#   procedure, Serve, reached only by a command-line option that did not
#   exist. Round, Measure and everything the published figures run through
#   are untouched, and the twelve-token, reference, device, drafting and
#   llama.cpp numbers were taken four hours before this on the same tree
#   minus the new procedure. Restamped with that as the reason rather than
#   re-measured, which is the choice this file's header offers and the one
#   an unreachable addition earns.
#
#   WHAT THE SERVER MEASURES, which is not a published figure and is here
#   because it is the argument for the next thing. Sixteen callers through
#   eight seats, each from the 110-token prompt, held to about thirty-two
#   tokens:
#
#     served/callers   processor   device
#           4 / 8       22 ms       15 ms
#           8 / 16      22          13
#          16 / 32      23          15
#
#   The processor's column is flat and the device's is not, and the reason
#   is the arriving: eight members steady on the device read 7.0 ms a token
#   and eight turning over read 13. A caller's prompt is read on its own, so
#   a server whose members turn over spends part of every round's worth of
#   time on prefill that no round divides. THAT IS WHAT MIXING PREFILL ROWS
#   INTO A ROUND WOULD CLOSE, and it is the largest thing left in this part
#   of the program.
#
#   With a seven-token prompt the two backends swap and the processor is
#   ahead at every count, because a round that short is per-call cost on the
#   device. A server is worth putting on a device when its callers have
#   something to say.
#
#   THE CHECK. Every token every member is handed is digested in the order
#   it was handed out. The digest repeats run to run on a backend. Between
#   backends it repeats for one member and diverges after twenty or thirty
#   tokens for two or more, which is the products' own drift at two rows
#   against one and not the serving: tests speed --round shows the same
#   divergence at the same lengths, and the conformance sweep's tolerance is
#   what bounds it.
#
# load:  under 1.30
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-04, what a caller's arrival costs, and a host that moved under a
# binary that did not.
#
#   THE MEASUREMENT THAT STARTED IT. The serving driver times the arriving
#   apart from the rounds now, which nothing had. Eight seats, thirty-two
#   callers, thirty-two tokens each:
#
#     prompt   backend    total     joining      a caller
#       7      processor  7.298 s   2.246 s      70.2 ms
#       7      device     7.435     2.817        88.0
#     110      processor 18.846    13.212       412.9
#     110      device     8.838     4.083       127.6
#
#   THIRTY-ONE TO SEVENTY PER CENT OF A SERVING RUN WAS THE ARRIVING, and a
#   round costs eight milliseconds. Two things were wrong.
#
#   ONE: A SEAT DID NOT KEEP ITS SESSION. Admitting a caller opened one and
#   retiring it closed one -- a whole context's keys and values allocated,
#   zeroed and freed, a hundred and thirty-eight megabytes here -- and the
#   seat's block of the device's cache went back with it for the next caller
#   to take and re-fill. The seat rewinds instead. Alternated, device, 110
#   tokens: 10.170 s against 8.880, and at a seven-token prompt 9.0 against
#   7.40.
#
#   TWO: A PROMPT WAS READ IN A PASS OF ITS OWN. It is rows of a round now.
#   Evaluate_Round takes a share list -- how many rows each member
#   contributes, one apiece being a decode round -- and a row is a member and
#   a position, so nothing in an evaluation cares which of the two kinds it
#   is. The map from row to member is built once a call and everything reads
#   it through the two functions that were already there.
#
#   BOTH TOGETHER, alternated with the commit before them, medians of three,
#   eight seats and thirty-two callers:
#
#     prompt   backend     before     after
#       7      processor   8.817 s    6.827 s   1.29x
#       7      device      9.559      7.502     1.27x
#     110      processor  19.971     16.444     1.21x
#     110      device     10.754      9.292     1.16x
#
#   Admitting a caller costs ten milliseconds, and what is left of it is a
#   copy of the prompt into the seat.
#
#   ONE STRETCH IS STILL READ ALONE. A device answers sixteen query
#   positions at once out of one cache and a round's rows do not share a
#   cache, so a round gives that kernel up. Measured: prompts of a hundred
#   and ten in rounds read 9.861 s where the same prompts read alone read
#   8.838. The rule is that kernel's own threshold -- sixteen positions --
#   and below it a round is free. Teaching the kernel to take one tile per
#   member is what would remove the exception.
#
#   A BUG THE ROWS FOUND. The fused half-layer held its queries and its
#   residual in a stack array sized by the rows of the call -- sixteen
#   kilobytes a row for this model. A decode round of eight is a hundred and
#   twenty-eight kilobytes and nobody noticed; a round carrying four hundred
#   rows of prompt is seven megabytes, and the stack said so. It is room the
#   backend keeps and grows now, which is one allocation for a run.
#
#   AND A CONTROL THAT FAILED, which is the part to keep. The five groups
#   were re-measured for this change and every processor figure came back
#   where it was -- twelve tokens 0.374 s against 0.374, the reference ratio
#   62 against 62, drafting 0.374/1.570/3.397 against 0.374/1.508/3.380 --
#   and so did the device's GENERATING rows: twelve tokens 0.264 s against
#   0.260, sixty-four generated 1.245 against 1.216.
#
#   The device's PROMPT rows did not. The 1419-token prompt read 1.141 s
#   against the published 0.829, and the 110-token one 0.087 against 0.070.
#   THE BINARY THAT PRODUCED THE PUBLISHED FIGURES THIS MORNING PRODUCES THE
#   NEW ONES THIS EVENING: 1.155 s from the same file, at a load of 0.27 and
#   a part at 58 C. Three binaries -- the morning's, the afternoon's and
#   this one -- read within one per cent of each other on the same
#   measurement. So the host moved and the program did not, and the device's
#   top clock state is where to look: a prompt is a long dispatch and a
#   generated token is not, which is exactly the pair that split.
#
#   The published device figures are left where they were and this is
#   recorded instead. Publishing an evening's device prompt rows over a
#   morning's, when the morning's binary disagrees with itself by forty per
#   cent between the two, would be publishing an afternoon.
#
# load:  under 0.60 before each alternated set; part at 58 C
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-04, the clock a device figure was taken at, which none of them
# carried.
#
#   WHY. The entry above records a control that failed: the same binary read
#   a 1419-token prompt at 0.829 s in the morning and 1.155 in the evening,
#   quiet machine, settled part, three binaries agreeing with each other and
#   disagreeing with the morning. Sampling pp_dpm_sclk by hand during a run
#   showed the part swinging 800 to 2700 MHz and spending a third of the
#   prompt at the bottom. Nothing in a published figure said which had
#   happened.
#
#   WHAT IT DOES NOW. tests speed samples the clock and the power every
#   twentieth of a second through the region the wall time is taken around
#   and prints the mean, the range and the watts beside the load. The round
#   and the server carry it too. A host that keeps no such numbers says
#   nothing and is left where it was.
#
#   WHAT THE PUBLISHED DEVICE ROWS READ, one sitting, load under 0.71:
#
#     measurement                        mean   range        watts
#     110-token prompt, generating none   951   800 - 1539   22.8
#     12 generated                       1695   800 - 2542   16.4
#     110-token file, 12 generated       1710   800 - 2601   24.8
#     1419-token prompt                  1761   800 - 2675   28.6
#     64 generated                       2066   800 - 2541   30.4
#
#   A TENTH OF A SECOND OF WORK NEVER GETS THE PART ABOVE 1539 MHz, and a
#   second and a quarter of it holds two thirds of the top state. The row
#   this file has explained twice -- the short prompt that generates nothing
#   -- is the one at 951, and it is the only measurement here that does not
#   reach half. What was inferred from two hand-samplings is now a number on
#   the line.
#
#   REPORTED AND NOT GATED ON, which a measurement decided rather than a
#   preference. The load has a bound and refusing above it is right. A clock
#   has none: a threshold high enough to catch the slow sitting would refuse
#   the 951 row, and that row is a published figure this file keeps on
#   purpose.
#
#   THE INSTRUMENT'S OWN COST, measured rather than assumed, because a
#   sampler that perturbs what it samples is worse than no sampler. Reading
#   pp_dpm_sclk is not a file read -- the driver asks the part -- and at a
#   hundredth of a second, with the kernel's directories searched at every
#   sample, a 1419-token prompt read 1.492 s against 1.477: one per cent,
#   consistently, in three alternated pairs. Resolving the paths once at
#   elaboration took it to 0.7 per cent and a twentieth of a second took it
#   under what the measurement resolves -- 1.173 s against 1.167, with the
#   third of three pairs level. A run on a processor samples nothing at all:
#   the watcher is told whether to look, because a task nobody starts is a
#   block nobody leaves.
#
#   The five groups moved because speed_run.adb is in every one of their
#   source lists. Restamped with the measurement above as the reason: no
#   processor figure can have moved, because nothing samples on that path,
#   and the device perturbation is under half a per cent and inside what
#   three alternated pairs resolve.
#
# load:  under 0.71
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-04, where a server's time goes, and the fused half a round was
# still refused.
#
#   THE INSTRUMENT. tests speed --serve --budget reports the phases, summed
#   across the seats: a round charges them to the session the call was made
#   on and which seat that is changes with who is in the round, so one seat's
#   clock is one seat's rounds and the sum is the server's.
#
#   WHAT IT SAID. Eight seats, thirty-two callers, the 110-token prompt:
#
#     phase                  processor   device
#       FEEDING              10.268 s     0.000 s
#       PROJECTING            3.095       4.829
#       ATTENDING             0.517       3.153
#       everything else       1.402       0.750
#       accounted            15.282       8.732 of 9.213
#
#   The device's feeding is zero because its whole second half is one
#   submission and is counted under attending. What the first row says is
#   that FIFTY-FIVE PER CENT OF A DEVICE SERVING RUN WAS THE THREE MATRICES
#   BEFORE ATTENTION -- and a round was doing them one call at a time, on a
#   path a batch of one sequence had not used since the layer's first half
#   went over as one submission.
#
#   THE EXCLUSION WAS OVER-BROAD. Normalize_And_Project names no cache and
#   no run of positions: it reads the layer's input, normalizes each row,
#   multiplies the batch by three matrices, and turns the queries and keys
#   by angles the caller tabulates a row at a time. Every one of those was
#   already a row at a time. What does name a cache is Whole_Layer below it,
#   and that one still refuses a round -- it would have every row attend the
#   first member's history and write over the first member's cache.
#
#   ALTERNATED, medians of three, device, marks and digests unmoved:
#
#     1419 positions, 32 rounds, 8 members    1.775 s -> 1.656 s   1.07x
#     1419 positions, 32 rounds, 16 members   3.428   -> 3.192     1.07x
#     8 seats, 32 callers, 110-token prompt   9.328   -> 8.777     1.06x
#
#   AND THE EXCEPTION RE-PRICED. A stretch of sixteen positions or more is
#   read on its own on a device, because a round cannot use the matrix
#   attention kernel. Asked again with the fused half in place: reading
#   everything in rounds reads 9.2 s against 8.6, so the exception still
#   earns its keep and teaching that kernel one tile per member is still
#   worth about seven per cent. Not built.
#
#   AND WHAT DID NOT MOVE, which is why the five groups are restamped
#   rather than retaken. For a call that is not a round the change is
#   nothing at all: the condition it removed from the outer test is the one
#   it added to the inner choice, so a batch of one sequence takes the same
#   two branches it took before. Asked anyway, alternated: twelve tokens on
#   the device 0.264 s against 0.265, digest 5abff916f9d83ca6 either way,
#   and the clock 1705 MHz against 1688 -- which is the first pair of
#   figures in this file to say that the two sides were measured at the same
#   clock rather than to hope so.
#
# load:  under 1.10
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-04, the matrix attention kernel for a round: built, measured, not
# kept -- and what it found instead.
#
#   THE IDEA. A device answers sixteen query positions at once out of one
#   cache, and a round's rows do not share a cache, so a stretch of sixteen
#   positions or more is read on its own. But a MEMBER's own rows do share
#   one: they are one sequence, one cache and consecutive positions, which
#   is a batch in everything but name. Dispatch a round a member at a time
#   and each dispatch can have the kernel a batch has.
#
#   WHAT WAS BUILT. A row base in both attention shaders, so a dispatch can
#   answer a slice of the rows the caller laid out rather than all of them.
#   A per-member list on the attention step: one push and one dispatch a
#   member, each with its own cache base, its own first and last position
#   and its own row count. The kernel choice then falls out of the row count
#   as it does for a batch -- sixteen rows or more and the matrix kernel
#   runs. A round whose rows are one apiece keeps the table and its single
#   dispatch, because a push and a dispatch a member would cost more than
#   the kernel could save on one row.
#
#   WHAT IT MEASURED, device, eight seats and thirty-two callers:
#
#     110-token prompts, read in rounds, before   9.2 s
#     110-token prompts, read in rounds, after    8.9
#     110-token prompts, read on their own        8.70
#
#     7-token prompts, in rounds, without it      6.860
#     7-token prompts, in rounds, with it         6.961
#
#   It is better than it was and still behind reading alone, and on short
#   prompts it is worse than not having it: eight rows a member never reach
#   the tile the matrix kernel wants, so all the change adds there is eight
#   pushes and eight dispatches where there was one.
#
#   WHY IT LOSES, WHICH IS THE PART TO KEEP. The matrix kernel was not the
#   whole reason a lone prompt is fast on a device. A prompt read on its own
#   takes Whole_Layer -- the entire layer in one submission, the cache write
#   included -- and a round cannot, because that sequence names one cache
#   and one run of positions for every row. So the spread hands a round the
#   attention kernel and leaves it paying normalize-and-project, a rotation
#   on the host, a cache write on the host and a fused second half: four
#   submissions a layer against one.
#
#   THE NEXT THING IS THEREFORE NOT THIS. It is Whole_Layer for a round --
