#   per-member cache writes and per-member attention inside one sequence --
#   and the per-member dispatch above is about a third of it. Reverted
#   rather than kept, because a path nothing reaches is a path nothing
#   checks, and the measurement is worth more than the code.
#
# load:  under 1.10; device 1519 to 1677 MHz mean of 2700 across the
#     alternated sets, which is what says the two sides were compared at the
#     same clock.
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-04, the fused whole layer for a round: built, worth twelve per
# cent, and wrong. Not kept.
#
#   THE IDEA, which the entry above named as the next thing. A prompt read
#   on its own takes Whole_Layer -- the entire layer in one submission, the
#   cache write included -- and a round could not, because that sequence
#   names one cache and one run of positions for every row. But a round
#   already has a per-row table in the cache, two words a row: where the row
#   has got to and where its block begins. Give the step that writes the
#   cache the same table the step that attends already reads, and the
#   sequence stops naming one of anything.
#
#   WHAT WAS BUILT. place.comp reads the table and writes each row into its
#   own member's block at its own position rather than one stride after the
#   row before it. Add_Place and Whole_Layer carry the table through; the
#   product push block went from twenty-eight bytes to thirty-two to hold
#   it. The readback was generalised: a round's rows come back into the
#   member whose cache they belong to, a row at a time. And the round was
#   let into the path.
#
#   WHAT IT MEASURED, alternated, medians of three, on the device:
#
#     1419 positions, 32 rounds, 8 members    1.655 s -> 1.466 s   1.13x
#     1419 positions, 32 rounds, 16 members   3.160   -> 2.809     1.12x
#     8 seats, 32 callers, 110-token prompts  8.786   -> 7.533     1.17x
#     8 seats, 32 callers, 7-token prompts    6.938   -> 6.329     1.10x
#
#   AND IT REMOVED THE EXCEPTION. With the whole layer in reach, prompts
#   read as rows of a round beat prompts read on their own -- 7.43 s against
#   8.21 -- so the sixteen-position threshold and the pass it protects could
#   both go.
#
#   AND THE ANSWERS ARE WRONG. Members of a round disagree, which is the
#   check the round driver exists for: two members with the same prompt and
#   the same rule must say the same text, and they do not.
#
#   THE ERROR HIDES AT A LONG CONTEXT, which is what made it worth writing
#   down. A wrongly placed new position is one part in fourteen hundred of a
#   softmax: a 1419-token round agreed with the processor for thirty-two
#   tokens and disagreed at sixty-four, while a seven-token round disagreed
#   at ten. The first measurements taken of this change were the long ones,
#   and they said it was right.
#
#   WHAT THE BISECT ESTABLISHED, for whoever takes it up:
#
#     The table does reach both shaders. Passing zero for it changes the
#     answer, so it is read and used.
#
#     One whole layer is already wrong. Letting a round take Whole_Layer for
#     the first layer alone moves the mark off the processor's, so it is not
#     an interaction between layers.
#
#     It is not the carry -- turning the carry off for a round changes
#     nothing -- and not the aliasing of the cache buffer in the place
#     shader: reading the table through the read-only binding that shader
#     already has, rather than through the writable one, changes nothing.
#
#   So the suspect is the fused layer's own cache write or its attention's
#   cache base: the two things the table is supposed to redirect and the two
#   the unfused path gets right. The measurement to take next is a round of
#   two members with the same prompt, with the layer's blend read back for
#   each of them and compared -- the collision says one member reads or
#   writes the other's cache, and that would say which.
#
#   Reverted. The prize is twelve per cent of a device round and a serving
#   run that needs no exception, and it is still there.
#
# load:  under 0.60; device 1933 to 2085 MHz mean of 2700 across the
#     alternated sets
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-04, a round's members now sit at different positions, and two
# things that came out of making them.
#
#   THE CHECK WAS WEAKER THAN IT LOOKED. `tests speed --round N` gave every
#   member the same prompt, so every member sat at the same position -- and
#   a round whose rows read each other's positions would have passed. Every
#   round figure in this file was taken that way. The driver gives each
#   member after the first a prompt one token shorter now, so the members
#   sit at different positions, which is what a server's members do.
#
#   THE SHIPPED ROUND PASSES IT. Processor and device agree at two, four
#   and eight members at one token and at four: 69a1504ba5575dfe,
#   b756687e5f5ee4ef, 997a64034590b0e8, 0f7b467c6c67777a. At thirty-two
#   tokens they part, which is the drift this file already records for two
#   rows or more against one and not a fault of the round.
#
#   WHAT IT SAID ABOUT THE WHOLE LAYER. The attempt above -- the fused whole
#   layer for a round -- fails the check, and the bisect it allowed got
#   further than the last one:
#
#     The placement is not the fault. Making the host write the cache over
#     what the fused layer's place step wrote leaves the answer wrong.
#
#     The table is read and used. Setting a row's reach to zero changes the
#     answer; setting its block to zero changes it again.
#
#     And one reading could not be reconciled: with the whole layer turned
#     off for rounds -- which leaves a round on exactly the shipped path --
#     the tree still answered differently from the processor. Either a
#     build in that sequence was stale or something in the shared plumbing
#     reaches a round, and the write-up says so rather than guessing.
#
#   AND A GHOST NOT REPRODUCED. This file records that widening the
#   attention push block by one field "once corrupted this device's answers
#   and nobody has explained" it. The product push block was grown by one
#   word here -- twenty-eight bytes to thirty-two, a spare word nothing
#   reads, every aggregate filled, nothing else touched -- and the round
#   agreed with the processor at ten and thirty-two tokens and the
#   twelve-token digest was 5abff916f9d83ca6 as always. SIZE ALONE IS NOT
#   IT. Whatever that note is about, it is not a block that got bigger.
#
#   The five groups moved because speed_run.adb is in their source lists.
#   The change is to the round driver's prefill, which no published figure
#   runs through: Measure is what takes them and it is untouched. The round
#   tables in the README were taken with the members level and are left
#   as they were, with this entry saying they were.
#
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-04, the whole layer for a round, hunted with the stronger check.
# What it is not, and one thing that should not be possible.
#
#   THE COLLISION WAS THE WRONG SIGNAL. The round driver prints each
#   member's first choice now, and in the failing tree BOTH members choose
#   the same wrong token: 275 at a logit of 17.13, where the processor and
#   the shipped device both say 29889 at 12.55 and 29892 at 12.60. Nothing
#   is leaking between members. The round's per-row table is not implicated
#   at all -- the whole round is wrong, equally, for every row.
#
#   THE PLUMBING IS NOT IT EITHER, bisected a piece at a time against the
#   committed llama.adb, each built and run:
#
#     the product push block grown by a word, every aggregate filled   ok
#     place.comp's two new fields, its table read, its binding renamed  ok
#     Add_Place carrying a table through to that push                   ok
#     Whole_Layer carrying a table through to its steps                 ok
#
#   AND THE THING THAT SHOULD NOT BE POSSIBLE. What breaks it is the
#   rewriting of Whole_Layer's arguments in terms of a constant --
#   Whole_Seat, which is Block_Base for a batch and zero for a round -- and
#   THAT CALL IS NOT REACHED FOR A ROUND. It was guarded with
#
#     if Rounding then raise Program_Error; end if;
#
#   as the first statement inside the branch. The exception does not fire.
#   The answers are still wrong.
#
#   What was ruled out around it:
#
#     Not the optimiser. It fails at -O0 with -gnatn removed, and the
#     failure is byte-identical to the -O3 one.
#
#     Not Ada's own checks missing something: this crate compiles without
#     -gnatp, so range and index checks are on and none fires.
#
#     Not stack layout by itself. A live local of the same type in the same
#     declarative part, written and read, changes nothing. Declaring the
#     two constants and marking them unreferenced changes nothing. It is
#     declaring one AND using it in the unreachable argument list.
#
#     Not the shaders drifting under us. After reverting, the regenerated
#     model_runner-shaders.ads is byte-identical to the committed one, so
#     the compiled words are a function of their sources.
#
#   THAT IS THE SAME SIGNATURE AS THE NOTE THIS FILE HAS CARRIED FOR WEEKS
#   -- "widening the attention push block by one field once corrupted this
#   device's answers and nobody has explained it". The entry above proved
#   the size is not it. This one says what the shape of the thing actually
#   is: a change with no execution path to the failing computation moves
#   the answer, deterministically, at every optimisation level. Something
#   in this program reads memory it does not own, and both notes are that
#   thing seen twice.
#
#   The whole layer for a round stays out. What is worth more than the
#   twelve per cent is the reproducer: `tests speed --round 2 --max-tokens 1
#   --backend device` against the same on the processor, with the two
#   constants and the argument rewrite applied to llama.adb, is four seconds
#   and shows it every time.
#
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-04, the whole layer for a round: found, fixed, kept. And the
# entry above corrected.
#
#   THE BUG WAS MINE AND IT WAS ONE LINE. The rewrite that broke it hoisted
#   Block_Base (Item) -- where this session's block of the device's cache
#   begins -- into a constant declared at the top of the block:
#
#     Whole_Seat : constant Element_Count :=
#       (if Rounding then 0 else Block_Base (Item));
#
#   Take_Block, which is what gives a session its block, runs a dozen lines
#   BELOW that declaration, inside the same block. So the constant held the
#   seat the session had before it had one -- zero -- and a batch wrote its
#   whole layer's cache into somebody else's block.
#
#   WHICH EXPLAINS EVERY SYMPTOM. Both members equally wrong: their
#   prefills, which are batches, wrote to the same wrong block. The round's
#   per-row table not implicated: it was right all along. The branch not
#   being entered for a round: the damage was done before the round, by the
#   prefill that does enter it. Surviving -O0 and showing no memory error:
#   the addresses were in range, just wrong.
#
#   AND IT CORRECTS THE ENTRY ABOVE. That one says "a change with no
#   execution path to the failing computation moves the answer" and draws a
#   parallel with the note about widening the attention push block. THE
#   PARALLEL IS WITHDRAWN: there was an execution path, through the
#   prefill, and I did not see it because I was watching the round. The
#   push-block note is unexplained again and this is not another sighting
#   of it. What that entry does still establish is that the push block's
#   SIZE is not the cause, which was measured directly.
#
#   WHAT FOUND IT, after ASan found nothing and -O0 changed nothing: asking
#   the program whether the two forms were equal, inside the branch that
#   uses them --
#
#     if <new form> /= <old form> then raise Program_Error; end if;
#
#   It fired on the first run. The rule that follows is worth more than the
#   fix: WHEN A REWRITE IS SUPPOSED TO BE ARITHMETICALLY IDENTICAL, ASK THE
#   PROGRAM, DO NOT READ IT AGAIN.
#
#   THE FIX. The block is read where it is used rather than hoisted, and
#   the comment beside it says why.
#
#   WHAT IT IS WORTH, alternated against the commit before it, medians of
#   three, on the device, every pair at a comparable clock:
#
#     1419 positions, 32 rounds, 8 members     1.667 s -> 1.473 s   1.13x
#     1419 positions, 32 rounds, 16 members    3.191   -> 2.809     1.14x
#     8 seats, 32 callers, 7-token prompts     6.993   -> 6.283     1.11x
#     8 seats, 32 callers, 110-token prompts   8.782   -> 7.489     1.17x
#
#   AND THE EXCEPTION IS GONE. A stretch of sixteen positions or more was
#   read on its own on a device, because a round could not use the matrix
#   attention kernel. With the whole layer in reach a round beats reading
#   alone -- 8.184 s against 7.496 for the same server -- so the threshold,
#   the pass it protected and the procedure that made it are removed. The
#   serving path agrees with the processor at one, two and four tokens with
#   it gone.
#
#   NOTHING OUTSIDE A ROUND MOVED: twelve tokens on the device 0.252 s
#   against 0.255, digest 5abff916f9d83ca6 either way, at 1698 MHz against
#   1676. The batch's own arithmetic is unchanged -- the seat and the
#   position are added at the call as they always were -- so the five groups
#   are restamped on that pair rather than retaken.
#
#   The round marks agree with the processor at two, four, eight and
#   sixteen members with the members sitting at different positions, which
#   is the check the entry before last put in.
#
# load:  under 0.60 before each alternated set
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-04, the first comparison of several sequences at once. This
# program's rounds against llama.cpp's batched decoding.
#
#   WHY IT WAS MISSING. Every figure in the llama.cpp table is one sequence
#   against one sequence, and most of this month's work was about serving
#   several. llama-batched-bench is the counterpart to tests speed --round:
#   -npl B decodes B sequences together, each with its own prompt
#   (is_pp_shared 0), and reports generated tokens over decode time, which
#   is the quantity the round driver reports.
#
#   THE RUNS:
#
#     llama-batched-bench -m M -c 24576 -npp 1419 -ntg 32 \
#       -npl 1,2,4,8,16 -ngl 99
#     llama-batched-bench -m M -c 24576 -npp 1419 -ntg 32 \
#       -npl 1,2,4,8,16 --device none -t 8
#     llama-batched-bench -m M -c 4096 -npp 7 -ntg 64 -npl 1,2,4,8,16 ...
#
#     tests speed --model M --prompt-file speed-prompt-long.txt \
#       --max-tokens 32 --round N --backend device|cpu
#     tests speed --model M --prompt-file speed-prompt-short.txt \
#       --max-tokens 64 --round N --backend device|cpu
#
#   GENERATING, TOKENS A SECOND, 1419-token prompts:
#
#     seqs   ours cpu  llama cpu   ours dev  llama dev
#        1      31.1      33.8       45.2      55.1
#        2      47.8      57.5       64.3      98.1
#        4      89.5      92.8      111.2     155.9
#        8     116.6     119.9      175.3     217.5
#       16     142.5     128.7      182.0     445.6
#
#   AND WITH SEVEN-TOKEN PROMPTS, sixty-four generated:
#
#     seqs   ours cpu  llama cpu   ours dev  llama dev
#        1      34.0      39.5       49.8      57.6
#        2      55.6      71.1       71.1     108.0
#        4     112.8     135.9      136.4     196.8
#        8     172.7     221.9      250.6     259.1
#       16     216.6     242.1      266.7     613.9
#
#   THE PROCESSOR HOLDS UP AND THE DEVICE DOES NOT. On the processor this
#   program is 1.03 to 1.28 behind and AHEAD AT SIXTEEN SEQUENCES WITH A
#   LONG CONTEXT -- 142.5 against 128.7, where llama.cpp's own curve turns
#   down. On the device it is behind everywhere: 1.2 at one, 1.4 to 1.5 at
#   two and four, 1.03 to 1.24 at eight, and 2.3 to 2.5 at sixteen.
#
#   THE SHAPE IS THE FINDING. A curve that is worse at two, level at eight
#   and twice as bad at sixteen is not a slower program but one whose
#   thresholds sit in different places. Ours stops scaling between eight and
#   sixteen -- 250.6 to 266.7 short, 175.3 to 182.0 long -- while
#   llama.cpp's roughly doubles over the same step, its decode time barely
#   moving (1.177 s to 1.149 at 1419 positions).
#
#   TWO THINGS TO LOOK AT, neither measured:
#
#     Sixteen rows is where a batch switches to the matrix attention kernel
#     here, and a round is excluded from that kernel. We lose it at exactly
#     the count where the comparison worsens most.
#
#     llama.cpp's own step from eight to sixteen is 2.37x, which is more
#     than the rows. Something switches on their side too, and what it is
#     would say whether the target here is a kernel or an arrangement.
#
#   WHAT IS FAIR. Each side gives every sequence its own prompt, both decode
#   with a full cache behind them, both report the decode alone. Ours
#   staggers the members' prompts by a token each so they sit at different
#   positions and llama.cpp's does not, which is too little work to see.
#   Prompt-reading is not compared here; both read sixteen prompts at the
#   rate they read one, and those numbers are in the single-sequence table.
#
# load:  under 0.60 before each set
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0; llama.cpp at 95b8e33e1

# 2026-09-04, the pass over the weights a tenth row cost. GROUP in
# row_product.comp, and a third compilation of it.
#
#   THE PROFILE THAT NAMED IT. A device round of N sequences, 64 generated
#   after 7-token prompts, is a step and not a slope:
#
#     8 rows   2.005 s
#     9 rows   3.557 s     78 % more time for 12 % more rows
#    10 rows   3.614 s
#    16 rows   3.830 s
#
#   Nine to sixteen all cost the same thing. row_product.comp's GROUP was
#   eight -- an invocation carries GROUP accumulators and reads each weight
#   once for all of them -- and the dispatch loop steps First by Batch_Group
#   until it covers the batch, so a batch of nine is two dispatches and the
#   second reads every weight in the model again.
#
#   The phase clock says attention is 0.0 per cent of a round's accounted
#   time, because a whole layer goes down as one submission; the matrix
#   attention kernel a round is excluded from, which the entry above named
#   as the candidate, is not what this was.
#
#   THE CHANGE. A third compilation of the same source with WIDER, GROUP at
#   sixteen, bound by Row_Line when the batch is above Batch_Group; Row_Group
#   makes the dispatch loop step by what the bound kernel carries. Below nine
#   the eight-wide kernel stays; at Tile_Least (32) the matrix product takes
#   over, and its tile is 128 vectors wide, so sixteen rows through it would
#   be seven eighths invented zeros.
#
#     glslangValidator -V -DWIDER src/shaders/row_product.comp row_wide.spv
#
#   WHAT IT IS WORTH, alternated, three rounds each, load 0.56 before the
#   first, TinyLlama-1.1B Q8_0 on the device:
#
#     9 seqs, 7-token prompts, 64 generated
#       before  3.573, 3.611, 3.632 s
#       after   2.810, 2.827, 2.859 s      1.28
#
#     16 seqs, same
#       before  3.799, 3.861, 3.874 s
#       after   3.059, 3.077, 3.088 s      1.26
#
#     9 seqs, 1419-token prompts, 32 generated
#       before  2.299, 2.319, 2.337 s
#       after   1.943, 1.944, 1.964 s      1.19
#
#     16 seqs, same
#       before  2.870, 2.872, 2.853 s
#       after   2.505, 2.483, 2.505 s      1.15
#
#   THE STEP AT NINE IS SMALLER AND STILL THERE: 2.002 s at eight against
#   2.810 at nine. One sixteen-wide pass is not one eight-wide pass -- it
#   does sixteen multiply-adds a weight rather than eight and holds sixteen
#   kilobytes of shared memory rather than eight, which halves how many
#   workgroups a compute unit keeps in flight. Reading the weights once
#   rather than twice buys more than that costs and not the whole
#   difference.
#
#   NOTHING OUTSIDE A ROUND MOVED, checked and not argued. A generated token
#   is one vector and takes the narrow kernel; a prompt is read 128 tokens
#   at a time and takes the matrix one. Alternated in this sitting:
#
#     1419-token device prompt   before 1.162, 1.176, 1.164 s
#                                after  1.173, 1.176, 1.186 s
#      110-token device prompt   before 0.123, 0.123 s
#                                after  0.123, 0.122 s
#
#   Digests unchanged: cbf29ce484222325 for the 110-token prompt,
#   448c2ed68ec342ee for sixty-four generated on the device.
#
#   THE ROUND TABLE'S DEVICE COLUMN, re-measured whole in this sitting, as
#   tokens a second:
#
#     seqs   short, was    is      long, was    is
#        1        49.8    50.0          45.2   44.6
#        2        71.1    71.3          64.3   63.2
#        4       136.4   136.6         111.2  109.8
#        8       250.6   253.8         175.3  173.2
#       16       266.7   332.9         182.0  208.9
#
#   THE TWO FINGERPRINT GROUPS THIS SITTING RESTAMPS.
#
#     The device backend's six cells, from idle, back to back, load 0.34 to
#     0.47:
#
#       6-token prompt, 12 generated  cpu 0.373 s   device 0.263 s
#         evaluating the prompt           0.051         0.034
#         generating                      0.322         0.229
#         processor time                  1.95          0.08
#       110-token prompt, none generated  0.367         0.122
#         processor time                  2.75          0.03
#
#     THE 110-TOKEN DEVICE CELL READ 0.098 s LAST SITTING AND READS 0.122
#     HERE, AND THE CODE DID NOT MOVE. It is the row "a prompt too short to
#     wake the machine" is about: the part never left its low band (974 MHz
#     mean of 2700 here) and where inside that band it sits decides the
#     cell. The before binary reads 0.123, 0.123 and the after 0.123, 0.122
#     in this same sitting.
#
#     The llama.cpp comparison, one sitting, load 0.52 before the first,
#     llama.cpp at 95b8e33e1:
#
#       110 tokens        prompt    generating 64
#         ours cpu        353.7       36.8
#         llama cpu       369.3       39.6
#         ours device    1182.8       49.5
#         llama device   1543.3       55.8
#
#       1419 tokens       prompt    generating 64
#         ours cpu        281.9       36.8
#         llama cpu       259.4       39.3
#         ours device    1211.8       49.5
#         llama device   1747.3       55.5
#
#     BOTH DEVICE PROMPT ROWS FELL AND NO DEVICE CODE CHANGED -- 1571.4 to
#     1182.8 and 1711.7 to 1211.8. The alternated readings above say the
#     binaries agree to a thousandth on that run. The part's clock in this
#     sitting was a mean of 1755 of 2700 MHz where earlier sittings on this
#     row held nearer two thousand, and the same binary has read 0.829 s and
#     1.155 s on it six hours apart. Published as measured, with the reason
#     beside it.
#
#     seconds behind the rates: cpu 110 in 0.311 s, 64 in 1.741;
#     device 0.093 s and 1.292. llama-bench at -ngl 0 with a device
#     present reads 759.1 t/s where --device none reads 369.3.
#
# load:  0.56 for the A/B, 0.34 to 0.47 for the device table, 0.52 for
#          the comparison
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-04, where a round of seventeen belongs, and three ways of
# reading a batch that were measured and not kept.
#
#   THE BAND THE ENTRY ABOVE LEFT BEHIND. Between Wide_Group (16) and
#   Tile_Least (32) a batch was served by neither kernel well, and a round
#   of seventeen cost more than a round of thirty-two:
#
#     seqs   before    after
#       16   3.153 s   3.112 s
#       17   5.431     4.155
#       24   5.704     4.336
#       31   5.982     4.482
#       32   4.348     4.348
#
#   Seventeen went down as two dispatches of the sixteen-wide row product,
#   which is two passes over every weight; thirty-two went to the matrix
#   product, whose tile is 128 vectors wide whether it is handed seventeen
#   or thirty-two, so it costs the same across that whole span. The
#   boundary belongs immediately above the row group and is now written
#   Tile_Least : constant := Wide_Group + 1, so the two cannot drift.
#
#   With a 1419-token context: 2.849, 2.986, 3.094 s before against 2.140,
#   2.183, 2.245 after -- 1.33 to 1.38. Sixteen untouched. Conformance
#   0 outside tolerance; the matrix kernel is half precision, so batches of
#   17 to 31 answer within tolerance rather than identically.
#
#   THE CEILING, MEASURED BEFORE ANYTHING WAS BUILT. The wide kernel reads
#   sixteen activations per decoded weight, one vector-length (8 KB) apart.
#   Two wrong-answer builds, alternated against the right one, 64 rounds of
#   sixteen sequences:
#
#     as it stands, sixteen loads sixteen lines apart   3.08 s
#     the same sixteen loads inside one line            2.16 to 2.45 s
#     one load, sixteen multiply-adds                   1.78 s
#
#   So the spread is worth about 1.34 and the loads about as much again.
#
#   THREE THINGS BUILT TO CHASE IT, ALL SLOWER.
#
#     A TRANSPOSED BATCH -- x[col * GROUP + k], the sixteen contiguous --
#     reads 4.02 s, a third worse. Contiguity across k costs contiguity
#     across lanes: eight lanes read eight consecutive columns today, one
#     32-byte fetch, and under a transpose they read eight blocks sixteen
#     floats apart. The layout cannot have both.
#
#     FOUR COLUMNS TO A LOAD, vec4 instead of four scalars, reads the same
#     3.10 s. The compiler did not merge them and cannot: the base is
#     vector * columns and nothing says columns is a multiple of four.
#
#     A WINDOW IN SHARED MEMORY, 256 columns at a time in the array the
#     reduction uses later, reads 12.9 s as first written and 5.3 s with
#     the bank conflicts out of it -- eight lanes a block apart land on one
#     bank of thirty-two, and giving each lane one word of every block
#     rather than every word of one block fixes that and makes the weight
#     read contiguous besides. Still 1.7 times slower than doing nothing.
#     The same answer this file recorded once before for a shared-memory
#     window; the cache is already serving these out of the nearest level.
#
#   AND A FOURTH, ABOUT THE ARITHMETIC RATHER THAN THE READS. Bounding the
#   accumulate loop by what the dispatch holds rather than by GROUP reads
#   5.72 s against 2.85 at nine sequences, 2.5 times worse. A dynamically
#   bounded loop is not unrolled and an array indexed by a variable in a
#   loop that is not unrolled goes to scratch memory. The shader's note
#   says the trip count has to be constant to unroll; it is right.
#
#   THE TWO FINGERPRINT GROUPS, RE-MEASURED, AND A CORRECTION.
#
#     THE ENTRY ABOVE PUBLISHED 1182.8 AND 1211.8 t/s FOR THE DEVICE PROMPT
#     ROWS AND SAID THEY WERE A CLOCK STATE RATHER THAN THE CODE. THREE
#     HOURS LATER THE SAME BINARIES SAY SO OUTRIGHT: alternated, four
#     readings each on the 1419-token device prompt, 0.812 to 0.841 s here
#     against 1.162 to 1.186 there, with the part at 1889 to 1944 MHz
#     rather than 1755. The rows below are the second reading. Both are
#     kept because the pair is the finding: the same binary, host and
#     command, 1.41 times apart on a clock state.
#
#     llama.cpp comparison, one sitting, load 0.49 before the first,
#     llama.cpp at 95b8e33e1:
#
#       110 tokens        prompt    generating 64
#         ours cpu        347.0       36.6
#         llama cpu       375.3       39.5
#         ours device    1506.8       50.3
#         llama device   1560.0       55.5
#
#       1419 tokens       prompt    generating 64
#         ours cpu        277.0       36.6
#         llama cpu       255.4       39.2
#         ours device    1648.1       50.3
#         llama device   1734.7       55.7
#
#     seconds behind the rates: cpu 110 in 0.317 s, 64 in 1.748; device
#     0.073 s and 1.273. llama-bench at -ngl 0 with a device present reads
#     724.2 t/s where --device none reads 375.3.
#
#     The device backend's six cells, load 0.49 to 0.57:
#
#       6-token prompt, 12 generated  cpu 0.378 s   device 0.260 s
#         evaluating the prompt           0.054         0.034
#         generating                      0.323         0.228
#         processor time                  1.98          0.08
#       110-token prompt, none generated  0.362         0.100
#         processor time                  2.79          0.01
#
#     THE 110-TOKEN DEVICE CELL HAS NOW READ 0.098, 0.122 AND 0.100 s IN
#     ONE DAY with the code unmoved between the second and third. The
#     middle sitting was the odd one; alternated binaries read 0.123 and
#     0.122 there and 0.100 here. A median of three inside one window is
#     not a defence against a window that is itself unusual, which is the
#     second time this had to be said today.
#
# load:  0.93 for the A/B, 0.49 to 0.57 for both published groups
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-04, a tile the size of the batch. matrix_product.comp compiled
# twice more with NARROW, and two items closed without building them.
#
#   THE PREMISE, MEASURED FIRST. A tile costs what its width costs whether
#   the batch fills it or not. With Tile_Least dropped to nine as a probe,
#   the 128-wide tile reads 3.983 s at nine rows and 4.139 at sixteen --
#   flat, because both round to one tile -- against 1.28 s for a batch of
#   one, which is a weight pass and nothing else. Most of the rest is
#   arithmetic on the zeros the rounding invented.
#
#   THE CHANGE. matrix_product.comp compiled again with NARROW (TILE_V 32
#   rather than 128) and again with NARROW beside MORE_FORMATS, so all four
#   format-by-width combinations exist. AV goes from eight accumulator
#   fragments to two; the weight tile is unchanged. Tile_Pipeline picks one
#   of the four and Uses_Matrix refuses the matrix path when the one this
#   batch needs was refused by the device, because Whole_Tiles decides the
#   room before the pipeline is bound.
#
#     glslangValidator -V --target-env vulkan1.3 -DNARROW \
#       src/shaders/matrix_product.comp matrix_narrow.spv
#     glslangValidator -V --target-env vulkan1.3 -DNARROW -DMORE_FORMATS \
#       src/shaders/matrix_product.comp matrix_narrow_extra.spv
#
#   WHAT IT IS WORTH, alternated three rounds each, load 2.25 before the
#   first -- above the usual gate, which is why every reading is given:
#
#     seqs   64 rounds, 7-token prompts, 64 generated
#        9   before 2.885, 2.865, 2.950   after 2.046, 2.003, 2.080   1.41
#       16   before 3.081, 3.116, 3.215   after 2.132, 2.175, 2.104   1.46
#       32   before 4.480, 4.551, 4.543   after 2.280, 2.298, 2.281   1.99
#       64   before 5.248, 5.169, 5.109   after 4.261, 4.193, 4.266   1.21
#
#     and 1419-token prompts, 32 generated, two rounds each:
#        9   1.953 -> 1.515   1.29
#       16   2.503 -> 2.000   1.25
#       32   2.268 -> 1.258   1.80
#       64   2.676 -> 2.248   1.19
#
#   BOTH BOUNDARIES WERE FOUND AND NOT CHOSEN.
#
#     Tile_Least is Batch_Group + 1. From two to eight the row product and
#     the narrow tile measure the same -- 1.812 against 1.817 at two, 2.048
#     against 2.009 at eight -- and the row product is exact where the tile
#     is half precision, so the exact one keeps what it does not lose on.
#
#     Narrow_Limit is 64 and is its own constant rather than the tile's
#     width reused. Above the narrow tile's width a batch is several narrow
#     tiles: that wins at 33, 48 and 64 (3.604, 3.932, 4.267 against 4.340,
#     4.743, 5.135) and loses at 128 (8.731 against 6.199).
#
#   THE WIDE ROW KERNEL OF THIS MORNING IS NOW UNREACHABLE ON THIS HOST.
#   Nine to thirty-one vectors was its band and the tile has it. A build
#   with it switched off alternates 2.091 and 2.398 against 2.370 and 2.080
#   -- noise on both sides. It stays for a device without the instruction.
#
#   TWO ITEMS CLOSED WITHOUT BUILDING THEM.
#
#     HALF-PRECISION ACTIVATIONS FOR THE WIDE ROW KERNEL. The kernel is no
#     longer reached here, so the change would be measurable by no figure
#     this repository publishes. Not built. Worth what it is worth on a
#     device without the matrix instruction, which is where to measure it.
#
#     THE PROCESSOR'S ROUND PATH HAS NO DEFECT OF ITS OWN. One sequence
#     33.9 t/s against llama.cpp's 39.5, sixteen 218.7 against 242.1 -- so
#     sixteen amortize 6.4 times here and 6.1 there. It amortizes better and
#     starts further back; the round is 1.11 behind because a token is 1.17
#     behind. The phase clock puts FEEDING at 64 per cent and PROJECTING at
#     20 at every count from one to sixteen. A perf profile of the round:
#
#       rows_by_strips           52.8 %
#       rows (the setup around it)  20.9
#       worker (the pool waiting)   10.4
#       mat_mul_range_packed         1.4
#       quantize_blocks              1.3
#
#     The 20.9 per cent is the next processor question and it is not a
#     question about rounds.
#
#   THE ROUND TABLE'S DEVICE COLUMN, re-measured whole, tokens a second:
#
#     seqs   short, was    is      long, was    is
#        1        50.0    49.8          44.6   44.5
#        2        71.3    71.4          63.2   64.2
#        4       136.6   137.6         109.8  110.3
#        8       253.8   254.0         173.2  175.2
#       16       332.9   491.6         208.9  255.1
#
#   THE TWO FINGERPRINT GROUPS, one sitting, load 0.46 to 0.57.
#
#     llama.cpp at 95b8e33e1:
#
#       110 tokens        prompt    generating 64
#         ours cpu        359.5       36.6
#         llama cpu       367.9       39.6
#         ours device    1549.3       50.6
#         llama device   1647.3       55.9
#
#       1419 tokens       prompt    generating 64
#         ours cpu        276.6       36.6
#         llama cpu       256.0       38.9
#         ours device    1605.2       50.6
#         llama device   1753.0       55.6
#
#     seconds behind the rates: cpu 110 in 0.306 s, 64 in 1.751; device
#     0.071 s and 1.264. llama-bench at -ngl 0 with a device present reads
#     752.8 t/s where --device none reads 367.9.
#
#     The device backend's six cells:
#
#       6-token prompt, 12 generated  cpu 0.379 s   device 0.260 s
#         evaluating the prompt           0.057         0.033
#         generating                      0.321         0.227
#         processor time                  1.98          0.07
#       110-token prompt, none generated  0.358         0.099
#         processor time                  2.75          0.01
#
#     The 110-token device cell has now read 0.098, 0.122, 0.100 and 0.099
#     in one day; the second was the odd one and the rest agree.
#
#   conformance: 0 outside tolerance. The tile is half precision, so every
#   batch from nine up now answers within tolerance rather than identically.
#
# load:  2.25 for the A/B, 0.46 to 0.57 for both published groups
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-04, the cache a round reads twice the size of. attention.comp
# compiled a fourth time with HALVED, and one more item the ablation closed.
#
#   WHERE THE LAST OF THE ROUND'S GAP WAS. A round of sixteen split by
#   context length, per round-step on the device:
#
#                                       ours      llama.cpp
#     after a 7-token prompt           32.5 ms      26.1 ms
#     after a 1419-token prompt        62.7         35.9
#     so attention over 1420 positions 30.2          9.8
#
#   Everything but attention was 1.25 behind and attention was 3.1 behind.
#   attention.comp declared its cache binding as float kv[]. place.comp has
#   written every position twice for some time -- once as it is and once as
#   half precision, in a copy after the cache proper, indexed the same way
#   -- and attention_matrix.comp was the only reader. A round cannot use
#   that kernel: it takes a batch of queries against one cache and a round
#   is one query against each of several.
#
#   The rate said the same before anything was built: a gigabyte of cache a
#   step in 30.2 ms is 34 GB/s, and llama.cpp moving half that in 9.8 ms is
#   53. A rate, not a different algorithm.
#
#     glslangValidator -V --target-env vulkan1.1 -DSUBGROUPS -DWIDE \
#       -DHALVED src/shaders/attention.comp attention_halved.spv
#
#   THE TABLE IS THE ONE THING NOT IN THE COPY, which is why this is a
#   compilation and not a binding change. It lives at the end of the cache
#   proper, the same buffer, so under HALVED its words are read as the two
#   halves they were written as, low half first.
#
#   WHAT IT IS WORTH, alternated three rounds each, 1419-token prompts,
#   32 generated, load about 1.0:
#
#      1 seq   before 0.698, 0.719, 0.721   after 0.725, 0.721, 0.719
#      8 seqs  before 1.453, 1.472, 1.478   after 1.335, 1.347, 1.359  1.09
#     16 seqs  before 1.996, 2.025, 2.045   after 1.750, 1.762, 1.749  1.16
#
#   A short context is a wash and should be: 8 seqs 1.999, 2.021, 2.017
#   against 2.013, 2.023, 2.026, and 16 seqs 2.155, 2.124, 2.101 against
#   2.129, 2.098, 2.129. Seventy-one positions is not where a cache costs.
#
#   BOUND FOR A ROUND AND NOTHING ELSE, which was a decision. The kernel
#   would serve any case the matrix and tiled ones leave alone, the largest
#   being one token generated with a long cache behind it. Tried that way it
#   reads 1.362 and 1.375 s against 1.374 and 1.415 -- a wash, because one
#   query against thirty-two heads is short of work rather than bandwidth.
#   Half precision for nothing gained is a bad bargain, so that case keeps
#   the cache proper and its exact answer, and the published generating
#   digest with it.
#
#   THE SETUP AROUND THE STRIP KERNEL IS NOT WHAT IT LOOKED LIKE. A profile
#   of a processor round: 52.8 per cent in Rows_By_Strips, 20.9 in Rows.
#   Annotating the 20.9 shows a scalar loop converting half-precision weight
#   scales one at a time -- two byte loads and a vcvtph2ps each -- reading
#   two bytes out of every thirty-four-byte block of the tile, which is a
#   second pass over the weights, built once per product where three
#   products share an activation.
#
#   THE ABLATION REFUTES IT. A build that fills the scale table with a
#   constant -- wrong answer, none of the conversion -- reads 4.872 and
#   4.687 s against 4.453 and 4.836 on a round of sixteen. There is nothing
#   there to take. Either the tile is small enough that the second pass is
#   served from cache and the conversion hides under the dot product, or the
#   twenty-one per cent is inlined strip work attributed to the caller.
#
#   THE ROUND TABLE'S DEVICE COLUMN, re-measured whole, tokens a second:
#
#     seqs   short, was    is      long, was    is
#        1        49.8    49.4          44.5   44.7
#        2        71.4    70.8          64.2   66.3
#        4       137.6   137.8         110.3  115.7
#        8       254.0   254.1         175.2  189.8
#       16       491.6   490.4         255.1  295.4
#
#   THE TWO FINGERPRINT GROUPS: NOT RE-MEASURED, AND WHY.
#
#     The change binds a kernel only when Rounding, and every figure in both
#     groups is a single sequence, so neither can move. That was checked
#     rather than argued: the binary before and the binary after, alternated
#     on the two device runs, read
#
#       6-token, 12 generated     before 0.225, 0.231 s   after 0.231, 0.233
#       1419-token prompt         before 1.163, 1.163     after 1.176, 1.166
#
#     The host was not quiet enough for absolute figures in this window --
#     load 1.0 to 1.5 from the desktop, and the part at 1700 to 1790 MHz
#     against the 1900 and better it holds when quiet -- and the sitting
#     three hours earlier, which was properly gated, is what the published
#     numbers still are. Publishing a second low-clock sitting today to
#     satisfy a check would be worse than saying this.
#
#     The alternated readings above are the evidence the check is really
#     asking for; the restamp records that it was given.
#
#   conformance: 0 outside tolerance.
#
# load:  about 1.0 for the A/B, 1.0 to 1.5 for the alternation
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-04, two more ways of narrowing, both measured and neither kept,
# and the redundancy the arithmetic found instead.
#
#   A TILE OF SIXTEEN. Whole_Tiles (16) is thirty-two, so a round of sixteen
#   still sends half a tile of invented zeros through the instruction. TILE_V
#   at sixteen takes that half back and is slower at every count:
#
#     seqs   tile of 32   tile of 16
#        9      1.922 s      2.060 s
#       16      2.083        2.176
#       24      2.127        3.349
#       32      2.269        3.494
#       48      3.779        5.044
#       64      4.035        6.975
#
#   AV is TILE_V / 16, so sixteen makes it one and the weight tile staged in
#   shared memory is used once per load rather than twice. That is the trade
#   matrix_product.comp's own note pinned when it chose eight against two
#   over two against eight -- 1709 against 328 gigaflops -- and thirty-two is
#   the floor for that reason and not for how full the tile is.
#
#   THE HALVED CACHE READ AS WORDS. Halving the bytes bought a third of the
#   time and not a half, which says the loads stayed eight where the bytes
#   halved. Eight consecutive key components are four uint words, so the
#   HALVED binding was declared uint and unpacked with unpackHalf2x16 -- one
#   fetch instead of eight, and the per-row table read as one word each
#   rather than recombined from two halves. Alternated, 1419-token prompts:
#
#      8 seqs   before 1.341, 1.356 s   after 1.380, 1.377
#     16 seqs   before 1.721, 1.779     after 1.813, 1.787
#
#   No better. The unpack pays back what the fetch saves. The values cannot
#   be read that way in any case: their eight are eight positions of one
#   component, a row of the cache apart.
#
#   WHAT THE ARITHMETIC FOUND INSTEAD, and it is worth more than either.
#   Attention still moves about 24 GB/s where llama.cpp moves 53. This model
#   has 32 attention heads and 4 key-value heads (attention.head_count 32,
#   attention.head_count_kv 4), so group_size is eight: eight query heads
#   share one group of keys and values. attention.comp dispatches ONE
#   WORKGROUP PER QUERY HEAD, so each group's slice of the cache is read
#   eight times over, once by each workgroup that wants it. Whether those
#   eight land close enough together to share a cache is the device's
#   business; a kernel that gave a group's eight heads to one workgroup
#   would read it once and not have to hope.
#
#   That is a kernel change rather than a constant, and it is the next
#   thing.
#
# load:  0.73 for the tile sweep, 1.42 for the word alternation
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-04, four heads to a workgroup. attention.comp compiled a fifth
# time with GROUPED.
#
#   THE REDUNDANCY. This model has 32 attention heads and 4 key-value heads,
#   so eight query heads share one group of keys and values, and
#   attention.comp dispatched one workgroup per query head. Each of the
#   eight read that group's slice of the cache for itself.
#
#   HOW MUCH IT WAS COSTING, MEASURED BY TAKING AWAY WHAT HID IT. The
#   workgroups of a group are neighbours down the dispatch's first axis, so
#   a cache serves them. Dispatching heads down the second axis instead --
#   one line in the shader, one in the engine, and the same change in
#   attention_matrix.comp so the prompt stays right -- took a round of
#   sixteen from 1.75 s to 8.9, and the mark was unchanged. A factor of five
#   from nothing but where the workgroups sat.
#
#   THE CHANGE. HEADS is four under GROUPED; the loops that were over the
#   block's queries are over the bundle's heads as well, so a key component
#   crosses the bus once and does QUERIES times HEADS multiply-adds, and a
#   value likewise. Attend_Heads gives the first axis one workgroup a bundle
#   rather than one a head, and the two are one decision as the second axis
#   already was.
#
#     glslangValidator -V --target-env vulkan1.1 -DSUBGROUPS -DWIDE \
#       -DHALVED -DGROUPED src/shaders/attention.comp attention_bundled.spv
#
#   THE BARRIERS ARE WHAT A BUNDLE WOULD OTHERWISE COST. A workgroup's
#   running maximum and running sum are reductions across its invocations,
#   and four heads reduced one at a time pay for four pairs of barriers.
#   whole_max_all and whole_sum_all reduce the bundle together: one pair of
#   barriers and four subgroup operations, which are not barriers. Written
#   the naive way this change would have lost.
#
#   WHAT IT IS WORTH, alternated three rounds each, 1419-token prompts,
#   32 generated:
#
#      8 seqs  before 1.375, 1.385, 1.388 s  after 1.217, 1.213, 1.225  1.14
#     16 seqs  before 1.755, 1.733, 1.810    after 1.430, 1.424, 1.429  1.23
#
#   And a short context gains a little where the entry before this gained
#   nothing -- 2.151 and 2.321 s against 2.071 and 2.070 at sixteen --
#   because a bundle saves reads of a cache of any length. A round of one is
#   a wash: 1.316 and 1.326 against 1.314 and 1.347.
#
#   Marks unchanged: 1eebfd0a51979065 at eight, e7c8984445611f65 at sixteen.
#   Conformance 0 outside tolerance.
#
#   FOUR RATHER THAN EIGHT WAS MEASURED. Eight matches this model's group
#   exactly and reads the same -- 1.192, 1.207 s against 1.220, 1.222 at
#   eight sequences and 1.411, 1.401 against 1.397, 1.433 at sixteen -- so
#   four is chosen because it also serves a model whose group is four, which
#   is the commoner shape. The engine binds this only where the group
#   divides by the bundle.
#
#   THE ROUND TABLE'S DEVICE COLUMN, re-measured whole, tokens a second:
#
#     seqs   short, was    is      long, was    is
#        1        49.4    49.8          44.7   44.8
#        2        70.8    70.9          66.3   68.6
#        4       137.8   137.9         115.7  124.3
#        8       254.1   254.2         189.8  209.2
#       16       490.4   480.1         295.4  359.8
#
#   A round of sixteen is now 1.24 behind llama.cpp at a long context and
#   1.28 at a short one, where this morning it was 2.45 and 2.30. The two
#   are level for the first time.
#
#   THE TWO FINGERPRINT GROUPS: NOT RE-MEASURED, AND WHY. As the entry
#   before this. The kernel is bound only when Rounding and every figure in
#   both groups is a single sequence, which was checked rather than argued:
#
#     6-token, 12 generated   before 0.236, 0.231 s   after 0.232, 0.229
#     1419-token prompt       before 1.175, 1.171     after 1.162, 1.165
#
#   The part sat at 1675 to 1762 MHz through this window against the 1900
#   and better it holds when it has been kept busy, which is the same band
#   the note above describes, so the absolute figures published stand from
#   the sitting that was properly gated.
#
# load:  0.92 to 1.87 for the A/B, 0.39 to 1.15 for the alternation
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-05, a word at a time in the k-quants, and the format nobody had
# measured.
#
#   EVERY FIGURE IN THIS FILE IS Q8_0, AND THAT WAS HIDING SOMETHING.
#   Generating 64 tokens on the device, one sitting, against llama.cpp on
#   the same files:
#
#     format    file      generating   ours    llama.cpp   behind
#     Q4_K_M    637 MB    1.668 s      38.4     86.2        2.25
#     Q5_K_M    746 MB    1.833 s      34.9     76.9        2.20
#     Q8_0     1116 MB    1.275 s      50.2     56.1        1.12
#
#   The k-quants move sixty per cent of the bytes and take a third more
#   time. llama.cpp is twice as fast with a four-bit format as with the
#   eight-bit one, which is what half the bytes should buy; this program is
#   slower. The gap on the format most published models use is 2.2 where
#   the format every figure here is taken on is 1.12.
#
#   AND IT SETTLES A THING ASSUMED THE OTHER WAY: generating on the device
#   is not bandwidth-bound. Half the bytes could not take more time if it
#   were.
#
#   THE CHANGE. row_product.comp reads a quantized weight through byte_at,
#   which is a word load, a shift and a mask -- so a format read one byte at
#   a time pays four loads where one would do. The Q8_0 branch has read
#   words since it was written, including the shift for a run that does not
#   begin on a word boundary; Q4_K and Q5_K had not. word_of is that shift
#   written once, and both now read words.
#
#     Q4_K_M, 64 generated   before 1.882, 1.904 s   after 1.690, 1.690  1.11
#     Q5_K_M, 64 generated   before 1.821, 1.811     after 1.836, 1.841  --
#
#   Bit-exact: bacbf063a3f69bca for Q4_K, 0bad6a3dd2acab3e for Q5_K and
#   Q8_0, e990d2a22a08b4d1 for Q2_K, before and after. The elements are
#   taken in the same order and each is the same number.
#
#   Q5_K MEASURED FLAT AND HOW IT GOT THERE IS THE INTERESTING PART. Read
#   naively -- both runs of words fetched inside the group loop -- it was
#   WORSE, 2.486 and 2.473 s against 1.821 and 1.811. Its thirty-two
#   fifth-bit bytes are the same for all four groups of a block, two bits of
#   each serving one pair of sub-blocks, so fetching them per group is three
#   quarters waste, and the byte-at-a-time form had been paying that too.
#   Read once for the block they come out level.
#
#   1.11 OF 2.25 IS NOT THE ANSWER. The byte loads were a real cost and not
#   the main one. What is left is the decode -- a sub-block scale unpacked
#   from twelve packed bytes, a nibble split, an offset -- against
#   llama.cpp's per-format specialised kernels. A kernel change rather than
#   a read pattern, and the next thing.
#
#   THE TWO FINGERPRINT GROUPS, one sitting, load 0.43 before the first,
#   llama.cpp at 95b8e33e1. The device was in its high band this time --
#   1881 MHz on the long prompt, against the 1675 to 1790 the two sittings
#   before it had:
#
#       110 tokens        prompt    generating 64
#         ours cpu        363.0       36.6
#         llama cpu       369.2       39.5
#         ours device    1486.5       50.5
#         llama device   1539.3       56.1
#
#       1419 tokens       prompt    generating 64
#         ours cpu        278.9       36.6
#         llama cpu       255.7       39.3
#         ours device    1695.3       50.5
#         llama device   1748.5       55.8
#
#     seconds behind the rates: cpu 110 in 0.303 s, 64 in 1.748; device
#     0.074 s and 1.267. llama-bench at -ngl 0 with a device present reads
#     700.9 t/s where --device none reads 369.2.
#
#     The device backend's six cells:
#
#       6-token prompt, 12 generated  cpu 0.376 s   device 0.262 s
#         evaluating the prompt           0.053         0.034
#         generating                      0.323         0.227
#         processor time                  1.98          0.06
#       110-token prompt, none generated  0.354         0.102
#         processor time                  2.72          0.02
#
#   conformance: 0 outside tolerance.
#
# load:  0.50 for the k-quant A/B, 0.43 for the published groups
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat, Q4_K_M, Q5_K_M, Q2_K and Q8_0

# 2026-09-05, a lane a block where the block is two hundred bytes.
#
#   WHERE THE REST OF THE K-QUANT GAP WAS. The entry above got a ninth of it
#   from word reads and said the byte loads were not the main cost. An
#   ablation said where the rest was: a Q4_K build with its weight reads
#   removed -- wrong answer, none of the loads -- generated in 1.231 s
#   against 1.677. Twenty-seven per cent, for a format that fits in nine per
#   cent of the file's bytes.
#
#   Two other ablations on the same run, for the record: no activation load
#   1.386 s (the x reads are 17 per cent), and no scale or offset arithmetic
#   1.459 (11 per cent).
#
#   THE ARRANGEMENT, NOT THE READS. Eight invocations share a row and each
#   took a super-block of its own -- 144 bytes for Q4_K, 176 for Q5_K, 210
#   for Q6_K -- so the eight lanes fetched from eight addresses that far
#   apart. They now share the block and take consecutive words out of it,
#   dividing it along lines the format already has: Q4_K and Q5_K as four
#   groups of two halves, Q6_K as four unit-runs of two.
#
#   WHAT IT IS WORTH, alternated three rounds each, 64 generated after a
#   110-token prompt, load 0.86:
#
#     Q4_K_M  before 1.819, 1.884, 1.909 s  after 0.996, 1.003, 0.996  1.89
#     Q5_K_M  before 1.818, 1.822, 1.829    after 1.076, 1.084, 1.087  1.68
#     Q8_0    before 1.275, 1.280, 1.292    after 1.282, 1.278, 1.279   --
#
#   Q4_K generates 64.3 tokens a second where it generated 34.0, and is 1.36
#   behind llama.cpp where it was 2.56. Digests unchanged: 40c7b92c21dea698,
#   90f72ad934240ff0, 0fbf5ccfc25ad073. Conformance 0 outside tolerance.
#
#   Q6_K IS MOST OF IT AND NOT WHERE I EXPECTED. A "_M" file is a mixture:
#   Q4_K_M and Q5_K_M keep their output projection and some attention
#   tensors in Q6_K, and giving that branch the same treatment was worth a
#   further 1.37 on both files after the other two were done -- 1.333 to
#   0.976 s and 1.469 to 1.073. A mixture's slowest format decides it.
#
#   IT DOES NOT WORK FOR A THIRTY-TWO ELEMENT BLOCK. Q8_0's block is
#   thirty-four bytes; sharing it gives a lane one word and four elements,
#   and the per-block setup is then done eight times over for that. It reads
#   1.441, 1.441 s against 1.251, 1.277 -- fourteen per cent worse. The
#   trade is the scatter a lane-a-block causes against the setup a shared
#   block repeats, and two hundred elements is on one side of it while
#   thirty-two is on the other.
#
#   AND TWO FORMATS ARE LEFT OUT. Q2_K and Q3_K take the same treatment and
#   get a second saving with it: their four groups are four two-bit fields
#   of the same sixteen bytes, so a lane-a-group read them four times over.
#   Done, they made Q8_0 TEN PER CENT SLOWER with its own branch
#   byte-identical -- 1.300, 1.315, 1.317 s against 1.151, 1.193, 1.195 --
#   because a shader is one module and its registers are allocated for the
#   worst branch in it. Two branches holding four words across a loop cost
#   every other branch its occupancy. That is what matrix_product.comp was
#   split in two for, measured at twenty-one per cent there. Reverting the
#   two put Q8_0 back to a wash: 1.172, 1.192 against 1.197, 1.192.
#   Splitting the row product by format group is what would let them in.
#
#   THE TWO FINGERPRINT GROUPS, one sitting, load 0.48 before the first,
#   llama.cpp at 95b8e33e1, the device in its high band (1922 MHz on the
#   long prompt):
#
#       110 tokens        prompt    generating 64
#         ours cpu        361.8       36.7
#         llama cpu       378.9       39.6
#         ours device    1527.8       50.2
#         llama device   1643.9       55.5
#
#       1419 tokens       prompt    generating 64
#         ours cpu        275.6       36.7
#         llama cpu       254.5       39.2
#         ours device    1693.3       50.2
#         llama device   1760.5       55.8
#
#     seconds behind the rates: cpu 110 in 0.304 s, 64 in 1.742; device
#     0.072 s and 1.276. llama-bench at -ngl 0 with a device present reads
#     625.9 t/s where --device none reads 378.9.
#
#     The device backend's six cells:
#
#       6-token prompt, 12 generated  cpu 0.377 s   device 0.258 s
#         evaluating the prompt           0.054         0.032
#         generating                      0.322         0.227
#         processor time                  1.97          0.06
#       110-token prompt, none generated  0.361         0.103
#         processor time                  2.75          0.02
#
#     And the k-quants against llama.cpp in the same sitting: Q4_K_M 64.3
#     against 87.1, Q5_K_M 59.0 against 76.6, Q8_0 50.2 against 55.5.
#
# load:  0.86 for the A/B, 0.44 to 0.48 for the published groups
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat, Q2_K, Q4_K_M, Q5_K_M and Q8_0

# 2026-09-05, two ways at the rest of the k-quant gap, both measured and
# neither kept, and an ablation that over-promised.
#
#   WHERE Q4_K STANDS after the block sharing: 0.963 s generating 64 tokens
#   against llama.cpp's 0.735. Ablating the current code:
#
#     as it stands                        0.963 s
#     with the weight loads removed       0.830      14 %
#     with the activation loads removed   0.902       6 %
#
#   So about eighty per cent is arithmetic and loop, and removing every load
#   in the kernel would not reach llama.cpp.
#
#   A SPLIT SHADER IS NOT THE LEVER THE ENTRY ABOVE NAMED. A row product
#   trimmed to Q8_0 and the three floating-point formats -- the best case a
#   split by format group could give that branch -- reads 1.759, 1.754,
#   1.759 s against 1.235, 1.270, 1.273, with the digest unchanged. Thirty-
#   nine per cent slower. Register allocation is not monotone in branch
#   count: a compiler with fewer branches to fit may unroll harder and spend
#   what it saved. Q2_K and Q3_K stay out on weaker grounds than they were
#   left on.
#
#   FOLDING THE SCALE OUT OF THE ELEMENT LOOP IS A WASH. A k-quant's weight
#   is scale * q - offset and both are constant across a sub-block, so a
#   sub-block's contribution is scale * sum(q * x) - offset * sum(x) -- two
#   operations for the sub-block where there were two for each element.
#   Alternated: 0.965, 0.981, 0.984 s against 0.946, 0.989, 0.984.
#
#   IT OVER-PROMISED BECAUSE scale * q - offset WAS ALREADY ONE INSTRUCTION.
#   A multiply and a subtract of that shape fuse, so the element loop paid
#   one operation and not two, and the folded form's running sum of x costs
#   an add that puts it back exactly. The earlier ablation -- 11 per cent
#   from removing the scale and offset outright -- measured what it costs to
#   not need them at all, which is not the same quantity as what folding
#   them out would save. An ablation bounds a change from above and this is
#   the distance between the two.
#
# load:  0.64 to 0.86
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q4_K_M and Q8_0

# 2026-09-05, a tile twice as tall, measured and not kept.
#
#   THE ARGUMENT. Accumulators are AR times AV in 16x16 fragments. The wide
#   tile spends that budget on AV -- eight vector matrices against two of
#   weights, which is the way round it measured fastest -- and the narrow
#   tile, at AV two, has six of those eight back. What rows would buy is the
#   vector side of the staging: every workgroup loads the same TILE_V
#   vectors for its own rows, so half as many workgroups is half as much of
#   that, and at a narrow tile the vectors are a comparable weight to the
#   matrix rather than a small thing beside it.
#
#   TILE_R 64 under NARROW, AR 4, and the staging loop giving one row to
#   each of the sixty-four invocations in two halves rather than half a row
#   each. Dispatch height from Tile_Height (Count), and Uses_Matrix asking
#   Rows mod Tile_Height rather than mod Tile_Rows.
#
#   IT READS 1.3 TIMES WORSE, marks unchanged:
#
#     round of 16   tall 2.620, 2.751 s   as it is 2.053, 2.107
#     round of 32   tall 2.884, 2.997     as it is 2.253, 2.294
#
#   Two things are on the other side of the trade and both are about a
#   twelve-unit part. Doubling TILE_R doubles the accumulators a workgroup
#   holds, four fragments to eight, which is sixty-four registers before
#   anything else. And it halves the workgroups -- sixty-four for a
#   2048-row matrix to thirty-two, under three to a compute unit -- so a
#   tile that is too tall stops being able to hide anything: there is no
#   second workgroup on the unit to run while the first waits.
#
#   Third arrangement of this tile measured and not kept: narrower loses on
#   AV, taller loses on occupancy. The sizes it has are a local best rather
#   than an untested default.
#
# load:  0.92
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-05, one of the two formats left out, and what the other was
# really costing.
#
#   Two formats were left out of the block sharing because together they
#   made Q8_0 ten per cent slower with its own branch byte-identical, and
#   the entry recording it named a split shader as the way in. Neither half
#   of that survives being taken apart.
#
#   IT IS Q3_K ON ITS OWN. With only Q2_K given the treatment:
#
#     Q8_0   before 1.258, 1.278, 1.279 s   after 1.251, 1.285, 1.274
#
#   a wash. Adding Q3_K:
#
#     Q8_0   Q2_K only 1.251, 1.285, 1.274  with Q3_K 1.400, 1.398, 1.399
#
#   nine and a half per cent, every reading on the same side. Q2_K holds two
#   words live across its group loop and Q3_K holds four -- its quants and
#   the mask bytes carrying the third bit. Four is what pushes the
#   allocation over. No split is needed: Q2_K is in on the same module as
#   everything else and Q3_K stays out.
#
#   AND Q2_K WAS NEVER WORTH WHAT IT LOOKED WORTH. The earlier reading had
#   it at 1.27 -- 1.016, 1.058 s against 0.818, 0.811 -- measured against a
#   build with none of the three super-block formats done. A Q2_K file is a
#   mixture and carries Q6_K tensors, so most of that was Q6_K's, which is
#   now in. Against a build that already has the three:
#
#     Q2_K   before 0.940, 0.937, 0.941 s   after 0.923, 0.924, 0.925
#
#   about one and a half per cent. Consistent across three alternated pairs,
#   free, bit-exact (0fbf5ccfc25ad073 before and after), and kept for that
#   -- but not a quarter of anything.
#
#   THE LESSON IS THE ONE ABOUT MIXTURES, POINTED THE OTHER WAY. The entry
#   above learned that a mixture's slowest format decides it. This is the
#   same fact used carelessly: measuring a format by running a file named
#   after it credits that format with whatever else the file holds. Both
#   times the answer was Q6_K.
#
#   THE TWO FINGERPRINT GROUPS: NOT RE-MEASURED. The change is in the Q2_K
#   branch and every figure in both groups is Q8_0, whose own branch is
#   untouched -- and the one thing that could have carried across, the
#   module's register allocation, was measured and did not:
#
#     6-token, 12 generated   before 0.224, 0.227 s   after 0.230, 0.230
#     1419-token prompt       before 0.819, 0.857     after 0.805, 0.840
#
#   The figures published stand from this morning's sitting.
#
#   conformance: 0 outside tolerance.
#
# load:  0.59 for the A/B, 1.03 for the alternation
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q2_K and Q8_0

# 2026-09-05, five things llama.cpp does, and the one worth anything here.
#
#   Read ggml/src/ggml-vulkan for what its matrix-vector path does that this
#   one does not, and tried each. Four are worth nothing on this part.
#
#   1. AN INTEGER DOT PRODUCT. llama.cpp has a second family of kernels --
#      mul_mat_vecq against mul_mat_vec -- quantizing the activation to
#      eight bits and using dotPacked4x8EXT, four integer multiply-adds in
#      one instruction. This device advertises
#      integerDotProduct4x8BitPackedSignedAccelerated. Our processor backend
#      has used the trick for months.
#
#      IT IS WORTH 1.5 PER CENT TO THEM, and they ship the knob:
#
#        GGML_VK_DISABLE_INTEGER_DOT_PRODUCT=1
#          Q8_0    with 57.62 t/s   without 56.24
#          Q4_K_M  with 88.40       without 87.54
#
#      Their float kernel alone is still 87.5 against our 64. Half a day's
#      measurement against a fortnight's implementation, and the knob was in
#      their source.
#
#   2. THE BATCH BOUND AS vec4 (data_b_v4). Implemented -- the binding
#      declared vec4, not four scalar reads hoping to merge, which is what
#      an earlier attempt here got wrong. A wash: 1.287 s against 1.281 on
#      Q8_0, 1.004 against 0.991 on Q4_K. The ablation says why: the
#      activation loads are six per cent of the run.
#
#   3. THIRTY-TWO THREADS TO A ROW against our eight. Swept: eight and
#      sixteen identical (1.276, 1.281 against 1.274, 1.279), thirty-two one
#      per cent worse (1.311, 1.312). Row_Lanes was chosen once for
#      coalescing and never swept; it is right.
#
#   4. TWO OUTPUT ROWS TO A WORKGROUP (rm_kq = 2 here). What it amortizes is
#      the activation reads, which are the six per cent above, so it is
#      bounded by a number already measured. Not built.
#
#   5. THE WORKGROUP WIDTH IS NOT ONE NUMBER FOR EVERY FORMAT. llama.cpp
#      builds its matrix-vector pipelines at two widths and picks per case.
#      Swept here:
#
#                    256 wide   128 wide
#        Q4_K_M      0.992 s    0.941
#        Q5_K_M      1.046      1.036
#        Q8_0        1.258      1.320
#        Q2_K        0.894      1.053
#
#      A wide group gives a row's lanes more neighbours to hide behind and a
#      narrow one leaves more groups to schedule; which a format wants
#      depends on how long its decode is. Q2_K is sixteen per cent the wrong
#      way, so this is a list and not a rule.
#
#      KEPT: two pipelines, Q4_K and Q5_K bound to the narrower. Alternated,
#      four rounds, load 0.29:
#
#        Q4_K_M  before 1.028, 1.024, 1.017 s  after 0.969, 0.980, 0.981  1.05
#        Q5_K_M  before 0.995, 1.030, 1.107    after 0.979, 1.020, 1.073  1.02
#        Q8_0    before 1.290, 1.294, 1.292    after 1.300, 1.298, 1.302  0.99
#
#      Digests unchanged; conformance 0 outside tolerance.
#
#   TWO PIPELINES AND NOT TWO COMPILATIONS. A second copy of the words put
#   model_runner-shaders.ads at 1,070,384 bytes against the megabyte this
#   repository allows a file. The width is a specialization constant instead
#   -- local_size_x_id, which is how llama.cpp declares its own -- so one
#   module serves both. That costs Q8_0 six tenths of a per cent, every
#   reading on the same side: a workgroup size the compiler cannot see as a
#   literal is one it optimizes a little less well. Inside the spread that
#   row has carried for a dozen sittings, and stated rather than buried.
#
#   THE TWO FINGERPRINT GROUPS: NOT RE-MEASURED. The published figures are
#   Q8_0 and the alternated readings are
#
#     6-token, 12 generated   before 1.225, 1.287 s   after 1.271, 1.279
#     1419-token prompt       before 1.161, 1.183     after 1.193, 1.187
#     110-token prompt        before 0.117, 0.119     after 0.117, 0.119
#
#   The machine would not settle below a load of 0.8 for long enough to take
#   absolute figures, and the part sat in its low band (1673 to 1757 MHz on
#   the long prompt). The figures published stand from this morning.
#
# load:  0.29 for the A/B, 0.44 to 0.80 for the alternation
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q2_K, Q4_K_M, Q5_K_M and Q8_0

# 2026-09-05, an eighth of a generated token is not in a kernel.
#
#   WHICH OF LLAMA.CPP'S FACTORS IS WORTH MOST TO LLAMA.CPP, asked with
#   their own knobs, 64 generated on Q8_0:
#
#     nothing off                            57.67 t/s
#     GGML_VK_DISABLE_ASYNC                  53.52
#     GGML_VK_DISABLE_DOT2                   55.95
#     GGML_VK_DISABLE_HOST_VISIBLE_VIDMEM    55.95
#     GGML_VK_DISABLE_GRAPH_OPTIMIZE         56.55
#     GGML_VK_DISABLE_COOPMAT                56.78
#
#   Their largest single factor is how the work is handed to the device --
#   seven and a half per cent -- and not a kernel.
#
#   THE SAME QUESTION HERE, as an ablation: a build whose layer records no
#   work at all and submits anyway.
#
#     as it stands   1.260 s      records nothing   0.160 s
#
#   Sixty-four tokens, twenty-two layers each, fourteen hundred submissions
#   with nothing in them. AN EIGHTH OF A GENERATED TOKEN IS THE RECORDING
#   AND THE HANDING OVER, and none of it is a shader.
#
#   CORROBORATED BY A PUBLISHED FIGURE. The same fixed cost falls on a
#   prompt, where a layer's work is 128 tokens' worth rather than one, so it
#   is a much smaller share -- and the device prompt is 1.04 behind
#   llama.cpp where generating is 1.11. The gap is widest exactly where the
#   fixed cost is the larger fraction.
#
#   ONE CHEAP EXPLANATION TESTED AND REFUTED. Every Vulkan entry point in
#   the recording path is resolved by name at the point of use -- a heap
#   allocation, a loader call and a free -- eight a layer and one more a
#   step, twenty thousand for sixty-four tokens. Resolved once at open:
#
#     Q8_0    before 1.241, 1.296, 1.299 s   after 1.294, 1.299, 1.302
#     Q4_K_M  before 0.927, 0.970, 0.963     after 0.982, 0.975, 0.984
#
#   A wash. The cost is in the driver's calls, not in finding them.
#
#   WHAT WOULD COLLECT IT is one submission a token rather than one a layer,
#   which is what llama.cpp does. This engine already hands over without
#   waiting and already keeps two command-buffer slots and two banks of
#   descriptor sets. What it does not have is a bank a layer: a step's
#   descriptor names that step's weight buffer, so twenty-two layers
#   recorded before any of them runs need twenty-two banks. That is the
#   change and it is the largest thing left.
#
# load: 1.01 for the entry-point A/B, 0.32 for the knobs
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q4_K_M and Q8_0

# 2026-09-05, one submission a token, built, and it is a wash. The entry
# above inferred wrongly from a good ablation.
#
#   BUILT AND WORKING. A layer's descriptors are a bank of their own, eight
#   banks to a command buffer, a barrier between the sequences sharing one,
#   the hand-over deferred until the banks run out or a sequence wants its
#   answer on the host, and Swap_Slots moved from every sequence to every
#   opening -- which is what made it wrong the first time: a sequence
#   joining an open buffer swapped out from under the ones already in it,
#   every submission succeeded and every answer was noise.
#
#   Instrumented, a submission carried six to eight layers where it carried
#   one: three a token against twenty-two. Digests unchanged.
#
#   AND IT READS THE SAME, alternated three rounds:
#
#     Q8_0     before 1.246, 1.296, 1.308 s   after 1.302, 1.303, 1.294
#     Q4_K_M   before 0.936, 0.978, 0.968     after 0.964, 0.982, 0.979
#
#   THE MISTAKE WAS THE INFERENCE, NOT THE MEASUREMENT. A build that records
#   no work and submits anyway does take 0.160 s of 1.260. That is what the
#   host and the driver cost when there is nothing else to do. It is not
#   what they cost when there is: the device is busy about eight hundred
#   microseconds a layer and the host needs a hundred, so the host finishes
#   first and waits. An ablation that removes the work measures overhead in
#   isolation, and overhead in isolation is not overhead in place.
#
#   The same error is recorded three entries above against a different
#   quantity -- an arithmetic saving an ablation put at eleven per cent that
#   was worth nothing because the multiply was already fused. An ablation
#   bounds a change from above; it is not the change.
#
#   AND THE CORROBORATION DID NOT SAVE ME. The prompt is 1.04 behind
#   llama.cpp and generating 1.11, and a fixed cost is a smaller share of a
#   prompt -- which fits the theory and would fit a dozen others. A ratio
#   that agrees with a hypothesis is not evidence for it before the
#   hypothesis has been tried.
#
#   NOT KEPT. Eight banks is five hundred and twelve descriptor sets and a
#   state machine across four procedures, for nothing measurable, and a
#   device whose kernels ran faster would still find the overhead hidden
#   behind them.
#
# load: 0.65 to 1.26
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q4_K_M and Q8_0

# 2026-09-05, four nibbles in one unpack.
#
#   THE IDEA, from llama.cpp's k-quant matrix-vector kernels: a four-bit
#   quant taken out one at a time is a shift, a mask and a convert each;
#   taken four at once it is one mask on the word, one unpack8 and one
#   convert of the u8vec4.
#
#     const uint32_t qs0_u32_lo4 = qs0_u32 & 0x0F0F0F0F;
#     const vec4 qs0_lo4 = vec4(unpack8(qs0_u32_lo4));
#
#   The Q4_K and Q6_K branches do the same now. Needs
#   GL_EXT_shader_explicit_arithmetic_types_int8.
#
#   WHAT IT IS WORTH, alternated three rounds, 64 generated after a
#   110-token prompt:
#
#     Q4_K_M  before 0.936, 0.957, 0.967 s  after 0.930, 0.941, 0.927  1.03
#     Q5_K_M  before 1.063, 1.067, 1.078    after 1.056, 1.076, 1.058  1.01
#     Q8_0    before 1.302, 1.299           after 1.293, 1.302          --
#
#   Bit-exact: bacbf063a3f69bca, 0bad6a3dd2acab3e and e990d2a22a08b4d1
#   before and after. Conformance 0 outside tolerance.
#
#   Q5_K WAS TRIED THE SAME WAY AND PUT BACK. Its inner loop needs three
#   sets of four -- low nibbles, high nibbles, and the bytes carrying the
#   fifth bit -- and holding all three as vectors read 1.059, 1.062, 1.049
#   against 1.005, 1.048, 1.072. Three vectors live where there were three
#   scalars is three times the registers for the same arithmetic.
#
#   THREE PER CENT IS WHAT IT IS, and it is the only thing three readings of
#   their source have transferred. The others, all measured here: an integer
#   dot product (worth 1.5 per cent to them, by their own knob), a vec4
#   batch binding, the threads a row is divided across, the rows a workgroup
#   answers, and one submission a token.
#
#   THE TWO FINGERPRINT GROUPS: NOT RE-MEASURED. Both are Q8_0 and its
#   branch is untouched; the one thing that could carry across is the
#   module's register allocation, and it did not:
#
#     6-token, 12 generated   before 0.225, 0.229 s   after 0.232, 0.248
#     1419-token prompt       before 1.154, 1.164     after 1.169, 1.167
#     64 generated            before 1.302, 1.299     after 1.293, 1.302
#
#   The host would not settle below a load of two while these were taken.
#
# load: 0.88 to 2.04
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q2_K, Q4_K_M, Q5_K_M and Q8_0

# 2026-09-05, the scale a block applies once, and where the search stopped.
#
#   llama.cpp's eight-bit kernel dots four quants against four activations
#   and applies the block scale at the end of eight elements; this program
#   applied it per element. Unlike Q4_K, where scale * q - offset fuses into
#   one instruction, a bare scale * q fuses with nothing -- one operation an
#   element in a loop of about six.
#
#     Q8_0   before 1.297, 1.296 s   after 1.296, 1.297
#
#   A wash, digest unchanged. The eight-bit generating path is not short of
#   arithmetic, which is the fifth way of asking that has come back so.
#
#   THEIR KNOBS SAY THE SAME OF Q4_K, where this program is furthest behind.
#   64 generated on Q4_K_M:
#
#     nothing off             89.12 t/s
#     async                   81.92
#     graph optimize          86.54
#     bfloat16                86.67
#     f16                     87.21
#     host-visible vidmem     87.48
#     dot2                    87.54
#     integer dot product     87.87
#     cooperative matrix      87.96
#
#   Nothing they can turn off is worth more than eight per cent to them, and
#   the largest of those -- how the work is handed over -- was built here and
#   found hidden behind the kernels. Their advantage on Q4_K is not a
#   feature that can be named and copied.
#
#   ONE STRUCTURAL DIFFERENCE REMAINS UNTESTED, named rather than guessed
#   at: their kernel answers two output rows to a workgroup where this one
#   answers one, halving the activation traffic and doubling the arithmetic
#   behind each load. It was passed over on an ablation bounding it at six
#   per cent, and this file has twice recorded that an ablation bounds a
#   change from above and is not the change. Building it means a row axis
#   through every format branch and a dispatch a fraction of the size.
#
# load: 0.66 to 1.54
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q4_K_M and Q8_0

# 2026-09-05, two rows to an invocation, both ways, and the search closes.
#
#   THE LAST IDEA from llama.cpp's matrix-vector kernels: two output rows to
#   a workgroup where this program answers one. A row's weights are read
#   once and multiplied into the batch; the batch is read once for each row,
#   so two rows to an invocation reads it once for both -- GROUP's trade,
#   made on the row side.
#
#   BUILT. A row axis through the kernel: sum twice as long, at_row at file
#   scope so accumulate needs no extra parameter, the format chain in a loop
#   whose trip count is constant so the copies' activation reads are the
#   same addresses, the reduction and the write-out over rows, and
#   Row_Groups asking for half as many workgroups. Digests unchanged.
#
#     Q8_0     one row 1.286, 1.301 s   two rows 1.412, 1.404
#     Q4_K_M   one row 0.927, 0.928     two rows 0.993, 0.988
#
#   Seven to nine per cent worse, and for the reason the tall matrix tile
#   found: half as many workgroups is thirty-two for a 2048-row matrix,
#   under three to a compute unit on a part with twelve, and there is no
#   second workgroup to run while the first waits. Third arrangement
#   measured here that loses on occupancy rather than arithmetic.
#
#   BUILT AGAIN THE OTHER WAY, with the lanes sharing a row doubled from
#   eight to sixteen so the invocation count is exactly what it was and the
#   sharing is kept:
#
#     Q8_0     before 1.293, 1.298 s   after 1.290, 1.286
#
#   A wash. So the sharing itself is worth about nothing here -- the
#   activation reads it saves were not costing anything -- and the naive
#   form was worse than that only because it disturbed the occupancy.
#
#   WHICH SETTLES THE METHOD TOO. The ablation that bounded this at six per
#   cent was right. An ablation bounds the thing it removes and says nothing
#   about what an implementation of it would disturb, which is sharper than
#   the two statements about ablations recorded above it.
#
#   THAT IS EVERY DIFFERENCE four readings could find between the two
#   matrix-vector paths: the integer dot product, the vec4 batch binding,
#   the threads a row is divided across, the rows a workgroup answers, the
#   workgroup width, one submission a token, the block scale applied once,
#   and four nibbles in one unpack. Two of the eight were worth anything
#   here -- the width, five per cent on two formats, and the unpack, three
#   on one.
#
# load: 0.57 to 0.80
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q4_K_M and Q8_0

# 2026-09-05, the unaligned word, which costs nothing.
#
#   llama.cpp indexes its quant data through a correctly-typed alias of the
#   buffer -- data_a_packed32[...].qs[...] -- and loads a word directly.
#   This program's word_of carries a shift, and where the shift is not zero
#   it reads two words and combines them, because a block boundary need not
#   be a word boundary.
#
#   WHICH BLOCKS MISALIGN, from the block lengths: Q6_K at 210 bytes and
#   Q3_K at 110 are not multiples of four and alternate; Q4_K at 144, Q5_K
#   at 176 and Q2_K at 84 are, and misalign only if the tensor's own base
#   does.
#
#   IT DOES NOT. Reading Q4_K's words directly, with no shift at all, leaves
#   the digest at 40c7b92c21dea698 -- so that format's runs are word-aligned
#   in this model, and the shift was never doing anything for it.
#
#   AND REMOVING IT IS A WASH: 0.935, 0.924 s against 0.925, 0.936. The
#   branch is uniform across the workgroup and the device was already
#   skipping the second load. An earlier probe that assumed alignment
#   everywhere read two to five per cent faster and changed two digests,
#   which is what it looks like when a saving is really a format being
#   computed wrongly.
#
# load: 1.29
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q4_K_M and Q5_K_M

# 2026-09-05, the twelve bytes a sub-block scale is packed into.
#
#   THE HALF NOBODY HAD LOOKED AT. Five readings of llama.cpp went over the
#   device backend; the widest gap in this file is on the processor:
#   generating with Q4_K_M reads 28.1 tokens a second against llama.cpp's
#   65.8, which is 2.3 behind, where Q8_0 is 1.09. The same shape the device
#   had -- they run the four-bit format nearly twice as fast as the
#   eight-bit one and this program ran it slower.
#
#   A profile: 67 per cent in Rows, 13 in rows_by_strips_q4k. What is in
#   Rows is the prologue that unpacks every sub-block's scale from twelve
#   packed bytes -- six-bit fields, four whole and four split between a
#   nibble and two bits held elsewhere -- sixty-four sub-blocks a row at
#   about ten scalar operations each.
#
