# And the llama.cpp comparison, both programs in one sitting, llama.cpp at
# 95b8e33e1 build 10597, three rounds. The engine's rows are 110 tokens in
# 0.779 s and 64 generated in 2.079 s on the processor, 0.169 s and 1.540 s
# on the device.
#
#   model_runner, processor    141.2 t/s prompt, 30.8 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp, processor       352.0 t/s prompt, 40.0 t/s generating
#   model_runner, device       650.9 t/s prompt, 41.6 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp, device         1670.5 t/s prompt, 56.2 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp at -ngl 0        769.4 t/s, which is the device answering
#
# The gap is 2.5 and 1.3 on the processor, 2.6 and 1.4 on the device.
#
# **The processor prompt row moved the wrong way**: 141.2 against 153.4 in
# the sitting before, for a change three alternated rounds put at four per
# cent faster. The A/B is the measurement and the row is a sitting; this
# file has said so before and here is the plainest instance of it yet. The
# device row reads 650.9 against 670.7 for a change that did not touch it.

# 2026-08-27, attention loaded every value four times. A device prompt is
# 1.14 times faster for swapping two loops.
#
# The item this answers was attention's uncoalesced key reads, and the entry
# above retired it: the tile is short of arithmetic and not of memory, and
# attention agrees -- confining its reads to a cache-resident window bought
# 1.18 times, so its whole memory cost is fifteen per cent and the fix
# wanted shared-memory staging, which is nought for four on this device.
# Same target, corrected mechanism. Attention is thirty-five per cent of a
# device prompt and does 182 gigaflops of arithmetic in about a second,
# which is 175 gigaflops a second on a part that does 3520 on a product.
#
# What was wrong. The weighted sum of values ran the query loop outside the
# position loop:
#
#   for each query i:
#      for each position j:  got += tile[i][j] * kv[value(j, c)]
#
# so kv[value(j, c)] -- one address, one value -- was loaded once for every
# query of the block. Four loads for four multiply-adds where one will do.
# The dot product above it already had the shape the other way round, which
# is why the query block bought 1.5 times there and this did not notice.
#
# Swapped, the value is read once and multiplied into every query:
#
#   1419-token device prompt   2.544 s -> 2.235 s   1.14x
#   110-token device prompt    0.203 s -> 0.160 s   1.27x
#   64 generated               1.591 s -> 1.592 s   unchanged
#
# Generating does not move and its digest does not change: a batch of one
# takes the subgroup kernel, where QUERIES is one and the swap collapses to
# the same code.
#
# **The prompt's answers do change**, and this file said they would not.
# Each query still accumulates over the positions in the same order and the
# expressions are the same, so the arithmetic is identical as written; what
# differs is which multiply-adds the compiler fuses, and GLSL lets it choose
# unless a variable is declared precise. 1a26d24d33b8957b becomes
# b3d99fb4151edc6d. The conformance sweep is the check the tile's own
# half-precision operands are held to and it passes at 28344 sequences with
# none outside tolerance.
#
# The phase counter moved by 0.055 s where the run moved by 0.310. That is
# the fourth time today, and by now it is not a surprise but a property:
# these counters are host wall around asynchronous submissions and a change
# to a kernel shows up wherever the next fence happens to be waited on.
#
# What was re-measured, one sitting at a load of 0.52 to 0.82. The two-row
# table under "The device backend":
#
#   6-token prompt, 12 generated       cpu 0.444 s   device 0.343 s
#     -- evaluating the prompt             0.071         0.055
#     -- generating                        0.374         0.288
#     -- processor time                    1.61          0.03
#   110-token prompt, nothing generated    0.747         0.159
#     -- processor time                    3.84          0.17
#
# And the llama.cpp comparison, both programs in one sitting, llama.cpp at
# 95b8e33e1 build 10597, three rounds. The engine's rows are 110 tokens in
# 0.717 s and 64 generated in 2.056 s on the processor, 0.164 s and 1.551 s
# on the device.
#
#   model_runner, processor    153.4 t/s prompt, 31.1 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp, processor       341.6 t/s prompt, 39.9 t/s generating
#   model_runner, device       670.7 t/s prompt, 41.3 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp, device         1693.7 t/s prompt, 56.3 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp at -ngl 0        752.9 t/s, which is the device answering
#
# The gap is 2.2 and 1.3 on the processor, **2.5 and 1.4 on the device**.
# The device prompt row has gone 412.0, 424.7, 438.2, 464.1, 550.0 and now
# 670.7 across six sittings today, and the gap on it 4.0 to 2.5.
#
# The per-format ratios and the three-way table of where the weights are
# were not re-taken: neither benchmark makes an attention call.

# 2026-08-27, the batched product is short of arithmetic and not of memory.
# Six times the bytes costs a quarter more time.
#
# The entry above ended by saying this file had no instrument that separated
# what the tile does per flop from the memory it reads. It has one now. One
# shape, one batch, every format the tile decodes: the flops are identical
# and only the bytes differ, so what changes between rows is the traffic and
# nothing else.
#
# Sixteen thousand three hundred and eighty-four rows by two thousand and
# forty-eight, a hundred and twenty-eight vectors, best of three passes,
# two rounds averaged:
#
#   f16    2.00 bytes a weight   64 MiB   2737 Gflop/s
#   bf16   2.00                  64       2624
#   q8_0   1.06                  34       3066
#   q6_k   0.82                  26       3132
#   q5_k   0.69                  22       3176
#   q4_k   0.56                  18       3236
#   q4_0   0.56                  18       3225
#   q2_k   0.33                  10       3358
#
# **Six point one times the bytes for one point two five times the time.**
# If the tile were bound by memory the two-bit format would be six times the
# eight-bit one's speed; it is nine per cent.
#
# Fitting time = arithmetic + bytes across the two extremes:
#
#   the arithmetic         3.52 teraflops a second
#   the marginal byte rate 87 gigabytes a second
#
# and for the eight-bit format that puts the memory at fifteen per cent of
# the time and the arithmetic at eighty-five. Eighty-seven gigabytes a
# second is what row_product.comp's own note calls "about ninety" for this
# part: **the weight reads are already at the bus.** There is nothing to win
# by coalescing them better, by laying the weights out differently, or by
# making them smaller.
#
# What this retires, which is the point of having asked. A smaller weight
# format is not a faster one on this device -- q4_k and q8_0 are within six
# per cent. A half-precision anything on the weight side buys nothing. A
# better weight layout buys nothing. Every idea whose mechanism is "fewer
# bytes" or "better-shaped bytes" is answered.
#
# What is left is flops per clock out of the matrix instruction. Twelve
# compute units at about 2.7 GHz doing 256 half-precision flops a clock each
# would be 8.3 teraflops a second, so 3.52 is about forty-two per cent of
# it -- and that peak is arithmetic on a number this file has not measured,
# so it is quoted as an order and not as a figure.
#
# It also raises the prior on attention, which is measured separately and
# agrees: confining its reads to a cache-resident window bought 1.18 times
# after the query block went in. **Both device kernels are short of
# arithmetic and neither is short of memory**, which is a coherent thing to
# know about this part and was not knowable from any single measurement.
#
# One note on the instrument. Taken a pass apiece these eight read a spread
# of sixty per cent between rounds and disagreed about which format was
# fastest -- a first reading had f16 twenty-one per cent slower than bf16,
# which a second round showed was noise and which would have been published
# as a finding about unpackHalf2x16. Grains takes the best of three passes
# now, for the reason Measure_Device_Ratio already states.
#
# tests/src/device_bench.adb is the only source changed, and no figure group
# names it.

# 2026-08-27, the narrow projection was the instrument. The tile does 3.4
# teraflops a second and the shape table was measuring a round trip.
#
# The item said the grouped keys and values run at 455 gigaflops a second
# against 1900 to 3200 for every other shape, that workgroup starvation was
# confirmed but not the whole story, and that what else bound it was
# unestablished. What else bound it is that nothing did.
#
# `tests device-bench` times Products.Multiply, which uploads the
# activation, records a command buffer, submits it, waits on a fence and
# copies the answer back. That round trip costs the same whatever the matrix
# is. Holding the batch and the columns still and sweeping only the rows --
# one workgroup to sixty-four, so sixty-four times the arithmetic:
#
#   rows    workgroups   seconds     apparent Gflop/s
#     32        1        0.000252            67
#     64        2        0.000292           115
#    128        4        0.000330           203
#    256        8        0.000351           382
#    512       16        0.000374           719
#   1024       32        0.000456          1177
#   2048       64        0.000595          1805
#
# **Sixty-four times the work for two and a third times the time.** A least
# squares fit is 0.289 ms fixed plus 0.154 microseconds a row, and that
# marginal slope is **3.41 teraflops a second**. Differencing adjacent pairs
# from 128 rows up gives 3.19, 6.08, 3.25 and 3.87, so the fit is not an
# artefact of the fit.
#
# The narrow projection does 0.13 gigaflop of arithmetic. At 3.4 Tflop/s
# that is 0.038 ms, on top of 0.289 ms of round trip: **eighty-eight per
# cent of its measured time was the instrument**. There is nothing wrong
# with the shape, the sixfold spread across the table is the fixed cost
# dividing into different amounts of work, and the number to hold about the
# tile is 3.4 Tflop/s rather than anything in that table.
#
# Below four workgroups the device is genuinely starved -- the 32-to-64 and
# 64-to-128 steps give 0.42 and 0.87 Tflop/s -- but no shape a layer asks
# for is that small. The batch sweep that found 455 rising to 1103 as the
# workgroups went 8 to 64 was reading the fixed cost thinning, not
# starvation lifting.
#
# So the entry above is corrected: the narrow vector tile was measured and
# rejected for a problem that was not there, and the twenty-six per cent it
# bought on that shape was the fixed cost being divided among four times as
# many dispatches rather than a starvation being relieved.
#
# The fixed cost is not only the instrument's. The floor entry measures the
# whole surround of a 1419-token device prompt at 0.502 s, and that prompt
# makes about seventeen hundred product calls: 0.29 ms apiece is 0.50 s. The
# two measurements meet, which is the first time two of today's have.
#
# Whether a bigger batch would divide it was checked rather than assumed.
# --batch-size at 64, 128, 256, 512 and 1024 reads 3.138, 2.490, 2.459,
# 2.426 and 2.547 s on the long prompt, every one printing
# 1a26d24d33b8957b. The default is within three per cent of the best and the
# curve is flat between 128 and 512. It cannot help: the floor is host loops
# over positions, and a batch does not divide positions.
#
# What this leaves. The tile is at 3.4 Tflop/s at the margin, the surround
# is 0.5 s of 2.53, and llama.cpp would read the same prompt in about 0.86.
# Neither the shape table nor the batch size nor the submissions are where
# the remaining gap is. It is in what the tile does per flop, and this file
# has no instrument that separates that from the memory it reads.
#
# The row sweep is kept in tests/src/device_bench.adb, which no figure group
# names. No other source changed.

# 2026-08-27, what surrounds the products is a fifth, not a half. The phase
# counters said otherwise and the phase counters were wrong.
#
# The item said doubling put feeding's arithmetic at forty-three per cent of
# its phase, so the other fifty-seven -- three quarters of a second -- was
# submissions and fences, and that closing it was the next thing. That
# reading is wrong, and the way it is wrong is worth more than the item was.
#
# The measurement that settles it. Every Dispatch in the engine asks for
# zero workgroups: every command buffer is recorded, every descriptor set
# updated, every submission made and waited on, every activation uploaded
# and every kept answer copied back, and no arithmetic is done on the device
# at all. What is left is the floor -- everything a device prompt costs
# except the device.
#
#   1419-token device prompt   2.530 s   floor 0.502 s   80 per cent arithmetic
#   110-token device prompt    0.208 s   floor 0.045 s   78 per cent arithmetic
#
# Four rounds of the long pair and one of the short, and the two lengths
# agree to two points.
#
# So the surround is a fifth of a device prompt and most of that fifth is
# the host loops that remain: normalizing, joining and rotating are 0.526 s
# by the phase counters, which is the floor itself. Submissions, fences and
# transfers are what is left over from that, and it is not much.
#
# Why the phase counters said otherwise, which is the part to keep. A phase
# is host wall time around an asynchronous submission, so the "non-
# arithmetic" part of the feeding phase is the host **waiting for the device
# to finish the arithmetic**. It is not overhead; it is the work, seen from
# the side that is not doing it. Emptying feeding's dispatch takes the phase
# from 0.891 s to 0.351; doubling it adds only 0.405 rather than 0.540,
# because two dispatches in one command buffer pipeline better than one
# does. Neither number is the arithmetic and the difference between them is
# not overhead either.
#
# That is the third time today a phase counter has led somewhere false --
# feeding gaining time a change did not touch, projecting not moving under a
# doubling, and now this. They are useful for saying which part of a layer
# grew and useless for saying what a part is made of. What says that is a
# whole-run measurement with one thing removed.
#
# What it means for the gap. llama.cpp reads 110 tokens at 1657.8 a second,
# so this prompt would be about 0.86 s there against 2.53 here. Take the
# entire surround away -- every submission, every transfer, every host loop,
# all of it free -- and this is 2.03 s, still two and a half times behind.
# **The remaining gap is the arithmetic**, and the batched product profiled
# above at 455 to 3200 gigaflops a second against a part whose matrix
# instruction should reach far more is where it lives.
#
# No source changed. The dispatch counts were zeroed in a scratch build and
# put back.

# Measured for the 2026-09-04 sitting above, after the blocks a session
#   keeps; restamped twice since, after the scheduler and after the arrivals
#   that ride a round. Re-measured both times: every processor figure came
#   back where it was and so did the device's generating rows, and the
#   device's prompt rows moved under a binary that had not changed -- see
#   the control above.
# load: under 0.60, on a part settled at 61 C.

# 2026-08-15, phi2. The same four groups moved with llama.adb again. What
# was added to a loop this time is an addition: a bias is added to what a
# projection produced, at four places a layer and once at the end, and each
# addition is one pass over a vector the projection had just written
# hundreds of products into. Every architecture but this one has a null
# there and tests it once. Not re-measured, and this machine still has no
# model file to measure with, which is the same reason recorded under the
# engine group above rather than a second one.

# 2026-08-16, the byte cache. The four groups carrying llama.adb moved with

# packs a row on the way in and unpacks an element on the way out, both in
# the attention loop, and a session that does not ask for it tests one
# enumeration value more per position than it did. The published figures are
# taken at the default precision, which allocates and touches none of it.
# Not re-measured, and this machine still has no model file to measure with,
# which is the reason recorded under the engine group above.

# 2026-08-20, attention on the device from both evaluators. The four groups
# carry llama.adb, which gained two subprograms and three call sites: a
# position's keys and values are written to a cache a device holds, and a
# position attends to that cache there rather than here. This was already
# said while generating; saying it while a batch is evaluated is what this
# change adds, and the reason it had to be added is that a drafted run
# checks its proposals through the batched evaluator while it generates
# through the other, so a device wired into one and not the other made a
# drafted run disagree with an undrafted one.
#
# The device group is re-measured; this group is not, and the reason is
# that the code cannot run here. Put_Position returns on its first
# statement unless the session's backend is the device one, and
# Attend_There is reached only when Put_Position has come back saying the
# device took the position, which off the device it never does. What these
# figures pay for the change is one enumeration comparison per position per
# layer, on a path that reads a matrix per position per layer.

# One of the two single-vector kernels did not work at first, and the reason

# tells the compiler nothing and works only while it has no other use for
# that register. With eleven operands it stopped working: the six-bit kernel
# raised inside a worker, and *adding an exception handler and nothing else
# made it run*, which is the signature of code generation rather than
# arithmetic. Both single-vector kernels now keep their cursors in registers
# of their own, named in the clobber list, and advance no operand. The
# four-bit one had the same latent fault and had already passed a gate.
#

# across four sittings of unchanged code. It is a difference of two
# twelve-token runs and a twelve-token run is a third loading and warm-up;
# the README says so and says to read the conclusion rather than the number.

# 2026-08-26, and the figures above were NOT re-taken. This entry is the
# other thing this file allows -- saying why a change cannot have moved
# them -- and it is the first time that has been used rather than a sitting.
#
# What changed: every machine code insertion in the integer kernels stopped
# advancing the pointers it was handed. They did it with an addq on a
# register declared an input, which tells the compiler nothing and works
# only while it has no other use for that register; the six-bit kernel
# raised inside a worker when it stopped working, and an exception handler
# and nothing else made it run again. Four insertions still did it, all of
# them working and all of them having passed gates, including Rows_Singly,
# which every generated eight-bit token runs and which advanced four
# operands -- more than the one that failed. All six now keep cursors in
# registers of their own and copy the counter rather than decrementing it in
# place.
#
# Why it cannot have moved the figures, and the evidence rather than the
# argument. Three alternated rounds on both models, in one sitting:
#
#                                     before      after
#   eight-bit, sixty-four generated   2.085 s     2.086 s
#   eight-bit, 110-token prompt       0.767 s     0.765 s
#   four-bit, thirty-two generated    0.357 s     0.362 s

# and every digest identical -- 448c2ed68ec342ee, cbf29ce484222325,
# 4b6e8e99ae285b2a. That is a better instrument than a fresh sitting would
# be: alternated rounds in one sitting resolve four per cent, and the spread
# between sittings on this machine is larger than that. Re-measuring
# everything would have replaced numbers known not to have moved with
# numbers differing by more than the change does.
#
# Rows_Singly is one instruction a block shorter than it was, three pointer
# advances having become two, which is the only arithmetic difference and is
# below what any figure here resolves.
