# 2026-08-18, gpt2's feed-forward normalization. These groups carry
# llama.adb and moved with it. Re-measured rather than argued about, on this
# machine, with TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0 and a two-token prompt:
#
#   seven tokens, device      0.570 s wall, 0.468 s generating
#   seven tokens, processor   1.063 s wall, 0.895 s generating
#   twelve tokens, drafted    5.800 s wall, with the Q2_K of the same model
#                             proposing four at a time
#
# Both backends printed 5709b12b0517c8c5 on the seven-token run, which is the
# claim the two-row table rests on and the one worth re-checking after a
# change to the normalization every layer of every token goes through.

# 2026-08-18, the device sequence. products.adb gained a recording half --
# Open_Sequence, Add_Product, Run -- and the body that performs one product
# became One_Product, which both the single call and Run reach. Nothing about
# what reaches the device changed, and the seven-token run says so: the same
# digest 5709b12b0517c8c5, at 0.696 s wall against the 0.570 s taken earlier
# the same day on a quieter machine. Re-measured because the file is named by
# this group, not because anything in the loop moved.

# 2026-08-18, a descriptor set per sequence step. The pool the engine makes
# now holds thirty-three sets rather than one, and the extra thirty-two are
# allocated with it. Nothing dispatches differently and nothing new is
# recorded: what changes is that a command buffer could hold more than one
# product, which one set made impossible. The seven-token run still prints
# 5709b12b0517c8c5, which is the check that a driver accepted the larger pool
# -- an engine that failed to open would refuse the run rather than answer.

# 2026-08-18, the hoist. A sequence now records every product into one
# command buffer, binds a set per product, and waits once: acquiring the
# matrices and updating the descriptors happen before anything is recorded,
# because neither may happen between two dispatches already written down.
# The products of a sequence read the same activation and write disjoint
# parts of the result, so nothing between them waits and no barrier is
# needed -- what a second product costs is a dispatch rather than a
# submission and a fence.
#
# No published figure moves, and that is not an oversight. Nothing in the
# engine records a sequence yet: every product still arrives through the
# single call, which is why the seven-token run prints 5709b12b0517c8c5 as
# before. The saving is real and unclaimed until a layer asks for it.

# The other three groups carry llama.adb and moved with it. What changed for
# them is the shape of one call site, not the arithmetic: the processor and
# the reference still do three products one after another, through the same
# subprogram they did before. Re-measured anyway, on this machine, at a load
# under 1.50:
#
#   twelve tokens, processor   1.679 s wall, 1.491 s generating
#   twelve tokens, drafted     5.738 s wall
#
# Against 1.682 s and 5.800 s taken earlier the same day, and unchanged again
# when the feed-forward joined the grouping: the processor reads the group one
# matrix at a time, which is what it did when they were separate calls. The reference group
# is not separately re-timed: a reference run of a 1.1B model is minutes, its
# arithmetic is untouched here, and the conformance sweep compares it against
# the engine twenty-six thousand times a gate.

# 2026-08-19, the figures as they stand after the withdrawal above, taken on
# the tree that carries the combining kernel. Recorded as of their moment,
# which is the only way this file will quote a wall time from now on:
#
#   seven tokens, device      0.559 s wall, 0.461 s generating
#   seven tokens, processor   1.007 s wall
#   twelve tokens, processor  1.641 s wall, 1.476 s generating
#   twelve tokens, drafted    5.415 s wall
#
# Both backends printed 5709b12b0517c8c5 on the seven-token run, which is the
# claim the two-row table rests on and the one that has held through every
# change of the last two days. The ratio between the backends is what these
# lines are for; the absolute times are not comparable with yesterday's and
# are not offered as though they were.

# The four groups, re-taken on the tree that carries all of this, each crate
# verified to have built:
#
#   seven tokens, device      0.580 s wall, 0.467 s generating
#   seven tokens, processor   1.012 s wall
#   twelve tokens, processor  1.583 s wall, 1.409 s generating
#   twelve tokens, drafted    5.820 s wall
#
# Both backends printed 5709b12b0517c8c5 again. As of their moment, as every
# wall time in this file now is.

# 2026-08-20, the corruption behind every end-to-end figure taken today.
#
# Close released the vector and result buffers and left the cache alone:
# still allocated, still mapped, with Cache_At pointing into memory the
# device was about to take with it. Cache_Buffer appeared in four places in
# that file and none of them was Close.
#
# Nothing showed while nothing called Reserve, which is why it came through a
# gate. The moment the engine wired attention up, a suite that opens and
# closes a device once a test went from passing to "malloc(): unaligned
# tcache chunk detected" -- and, in another run, to a glibc thread-priority
# assertion, which is the same stale pointer surfacing wherever the allocator
# next happened to look. Two symptoms, one fault.
#
# What it costs is not the crash. Every end-to-end measurement of device
# attention taken today ran on a binary with it: the ones that said four
# times slower, the ones that said eighteen times slower, and the one that
# said eleven per cent faster. None of them mean anything. The isolated
# figures from `tests device-bench` are untouched -- that tool opens one
# engine and never closes it, which is exactly why it never saw this.
#
# The two-row device figure is not retaken: nothing the engine runs has
# moved, since nothing in the engine reserves a cache. The sources the group
# names have changed, which is the case this file's rules allow a written
# reason for.
#
# The benchmark not reproducing it was not luck. A tool that measures one
# call in one engine cannot see a fault in the second open, and the engine
# does nothing else all day.

# 2026-08-20, the call is not what is slow. Timed where it is made, inside a
# layer, an attention reads 0.537 ms -- the same as the benchmark says for it
# alone, and not the 2.55 ms that was inferred earlier by dividing an
# end-to-end difference by a call count. Reserving the cache costs 0.24 us a
# call after the one allocation of 88 MiB, and writing a position costs
# 1.18 us. Ninety-five milliseconds a run, all told, for the wiring.
#
# So the earlier inference was wrong, and it was wrong because an eight-token
# window is shorter than this machine's own variation. Lengthened to
# sixty-four it says something else entirely:
#
#   generating, unwired    0.282 s        4.4 ms a token
#   generating, wired      5.224 s       81.6 ms a token
#
# Fourteen hundred attention calls account for 0.76 s of that 5.2. And the
# cost per token grew from 4.4 to 81.6 while the calls per token did not:
# whatever this is, it is not a per-call cost.
#
# Five explanations have now been offered and measured away:
#
#   the machine was busy    no: interleaved, and the unwired prompt path
#                           read the same either side
#   the shape was wrong     no: the engine's own shape reads 0.35 ms
#   the cache went cold     no: walking every layer's region, 0.508 ms
#   the queue was busy      not settled by differencing -- four products
#                           cost thirty milliseconds and the attention one,
#                           and three tries gave -4.02, +0.76 and +1.09 ms
#   the cache took the      no: cycling twenty matrices with a cache
#   weights' room           reserved gives back none of them, and kept forty
#
# The last was worth asking because the weight budget is a share of the heap
# fixed when the device opens and knows nothing about a cache reserved after.
# It gives back nothing all the same.
#
# What is left is unknown, and the honest thing is to leave it that way rather
# than offer a sixth. The rows are in `tests device-bench`.

# 2026-08-20, hunting the difference between what an attention call costs on
# its own and what it costs a layer. Wired into the engine it read 0.709 s of
# generating against 0.261 s with the processor doing the attention -- taken
# interleaved in one session, with the prompt evaluation reading 53.99 s and
# 54.29 s either side of it, which is what says the machine was the same. It
# is 0.448 s across 176 calls: 2.55 ms apiece, against 0.35 to 0.54 ms for the
# same call measured on its own at the engine's own shape.
#
# Three explanations were offered for that and the benchmark refused two of
# them outright:
#
#   the machine was busy      no: the unwired prompt path read the same
#                             either side, and the pair was interleaved
#   the shape was wrong       no: measured at thirty-two heads sharing four
#                             groups, across ninety megabytes of cache,
#                             0.35 ms
#   the cache went cold       no: walking a different layer's region every
#                             call reads 0.508 ms against 0.537 for the same
#                             one twenty times over
#
# What is left is the fourth, and it is not settled. Beside one matrix product
# an attention's marginal cost read 0.703 ms in one run and 1.810 ms in the
# next -- above its cost alone, and varying by more than a factor of two. A
# layer submits four products around each attention, and 2.55 ms is inside
# what that suggests if the effect scales. It was called refuted here on the
# first of those two numbers, which was one reading and the smaller one.
#
# So: the call is cheap and the calling is not, and how much of the calling
# is the fence, the pipeline change, or the driver is not known. The rows are
# in `tests device-bench` for whoever asks next.

# 2026-08-20, the stage bounds held against the machine's time and not the
# wall's. What a stage costs the processor barely moves when something else
# is running; what it costs the clock moves by more than the growth a bound
# exists to catch. Conformance read 1741.85 s of wall in one run and 962 in
# the one before it, for the same twenty-six thousand sequences -- and 1757.39
# s of processor time, which is the figure that describes the stage.
#
# The five, measured on this host:
#
#   suite               3.94, 6.28 s              bound 20
#   repository checks   9.00, 11.81, 13.03, 19.39  bound 60
#   conformance         1757.39, 1945.01 s         bound 4000
#   fixtures            199.92, 211.37 s           bound 450
#   fuzzing             0.28, 0.30 s               bound 5
#   the whole gate      about 2180 s               bound 4800
#
# Twice the worst for the long stages and three times it for the short two.
# The short ones move: the repository checks read 9.00 and then 19.39, better
# than two to one, because a stage that reads five thousand files gets what
# the page cache and the scheduler give it that minute. The long ones are
# arithmetic and hold still -- conformance moved by a ninth across two runs
# whose wall times differed by a factor of two.
#
# This was set from a single reading each on the first pass and had to be
# widened twice. A bound taken from one reading is a bound taken from the
# best of them, and that is the third time this file has recorded the same
# lesson in two days.
#
# Where a host keeps no such number the bound is not held at all. It is said
# and not counted, rather than quietly reverting to the wall clock -- which is
# the measurement this change exists to stop relying on. Processor_Seconds
# reads /proc/self/stat, so that is every host but this family of them.
#
# Asking the system instead would answer everywhere: getrusage is POSIX and
# gives the same figure. It was written that way and taken out again, because
# a check in this repository refuses a host call bound by name in the tests
# crate -- one directory for every host -- and the check was right. It belongs
# in the platform layer with a body apiece, which is its own change and not
# this one.

# 2026-08-20, a bound that could not tell a busy machine from a stage that
# grew. Its own message said so -- "either the machine was busy or the stage
# grew" -- and it failed the gate either way. Conformance read 648, 992, 1505
# and 1794 s in one day against a bound of 1250, and the difference was the
# machine every time: this host was at a load of 3.6 for the last of them and
# none of it was this program's.
#
# The bound was not wrong. Twice the 648 s taken on a quiet machine is 1250,
# which is the rule written beside it. What was wrong is that a figure taken
# at a load of 3.6 is not a figure about the stage, and this repository
# already says so about every other number it prints: Host_Load.Publishable,
# whose own comment says the rule is about figures and not about any one
# measurement. A stage's time is a figure.
#
# So an overrun above that load is reported and not counted, and an overrun
# below it fails as before. Checked both ways with the bound set to a
# thousandth of a second: at a load of 3.06 it noted, and with the load
# forced to 1.0 it failed.
#
# A bound that fails half the time is a bound people learn to ignore, and
# that is how a suite of twenty-eight minutes went unnoticed for months.
#
# The rule alone was not enough, and the run that proved it is worth keeping.
# With the mechanism in, the gate's total was noted at a load of 1.57 and
# conformance failed at 1.40 -- correctly, by the rule, since 1.40 is quiet
# enough to publish from. It took 1479 s there against a bound of 1250 that
# came from 648 s the day before, also at a publishable load.
#
# So the quiet baseline itself moves by 2.3 times on this host, and a bound
# meant to catch a stage that doubles cannot be set tighter than the noise it
# is measuring against. 3000 s is twice the worst reading taken at a load the
# rule calls quiet, which is the most a wall clock can say here.
#
# The better instrument is processor time rather than wall time: what a stage
# costs the machine barely moves when something else is running, and what it
# costs the clock moves by more than the thing being watched for. That is a
# change to what Report_Stage measures rather than to any bound, and it is
# not made here.

# 2026-08-20, the attention kernel measured instead of guessed at. Two shapes
# of it had been written and each lost to the processor -- one by twelve
# times, the next by four -- and both were written before anything measured
# where their time went. `tests device-bench` asks that instead: one call
# timed at several shapes, with the arithmetic done put beside the seconds
# taken.
#
# What it said about the kernel as committed, one invocation a head:
#
#   1 head,   512 positions   6.204 ms     32 heads, 16 positions   0.582 ms
#   32 heads, 512 positions   9.980 ms     peak                     0.52 Gflop/s
#
# Rows two and three do identical arithmetic -- 131 thousand operations -- and
# differ fifteenfold. Thirty-two times the heads cost one and a half times;
# four times the positions cost three and a quarter. Positions were the serial
# axis, which is what one invocation a head means and what no amount of
# rewriting in the same shape would have fixed.
#
# Three changes, each from a number rather than a hunch:
#
#   a workgroup a head, positions dealt round its invocations
#   the cache left on the device instead of re-sent for every call
#   the cache mapped once instead of mapped and unmapped per write
#
#   attention, 32 heads 512 positions   9.98 ms  ->  0.395 ms
#   peak                                0.52     ->  13.0 Gflop/s
#   a cache write of 2048 values        1.6 ms   ->  0.26 us
#
# The processor does the same attention in 0.85 ms, so the kernel is now
# twice its speed where it was four times slower. The standing mapping is
# safe rather than lucky: the memory kind a device is chosen for must be
# host-coherent, which is required where that choice is made, so a write
# through it is seen without a flush. An earlier attempt at this was reverted
# for fear of exactly that, and the fear was unfounded.
#
# The device group's own two-row figure is not retaken: nothing the engine
# runs has moved, since nothing in the engine calls any of this. The sources
# the group names have changed, which is the case this file's rules allow a
# written reason for.
#
# It is not wired to the engine and does not pay yet. Generation at 522
# cached positions reads 0.654 s against the processor's 0.150 s, and 176
# attention calls account for seventy milliseconds of that. The rest is
# unexplained, so nothing is claimed for it. What there is now is an
# instrument that answers such a question in seconds rather than in
# forty-five-minute gate runs, which is what made the first two shapes cost
# what they did.

# 2026-08-19, and the answer to it: the suite takes eight seconds.
#
# The routine that was sixteen of its twenty-eight minutes called
# Conformance.Run -- the same sweep the gate runs as a stage of its own and
# `tests conformance` runs alone. So the gate compared the engine against the
# reference twice, 948 s inside the suite and 650 s again beside it, and the
# suite was the stage the gate did not time.
#
# Taken out of the suite:
#
#   before   1677 s, 259 tests
#   after        8 s, 258 tests
#
# Ninety-nine and a half per cent of a stage, and it was a duplicate. The
# gate loses nothing: the sweep still runs, once, where it was already
# running. What `tests test` alone loses is the comparison, and that is the
# right place to lose it -- a suite is what runs inside somebody's attention,
# and a sixteen-minute comparison belongs in the gate that already has one.
#
# The suite's bound comes down from 2900 s to 60, which says loudly if
# anything of that size is put back. And the README, which said a second and
# a half and was corrected yesterday to twenty-four minutes, now says eight
# seconds -- the first figure had been right before that test was added, and
# the correction was made to a number that was about to stop being true.

# 2026-08-19, where the suite's twenty-eight minutes go: one test.
#
# `tests slow` runs each of the nine cases on its own and times it, and will
# time one test named by the prefix AUnit's filter understands. What it says:
#
#   inference    1219.8 s        cli          3.2 s
#   template     1.0 s           gguf         0.4 s
#   backend, catalog, sampling, grammar, accounting, under a quarter-second
#
# Ninety-nine and a half per cent of the suite is one case, and of that case
# 948 s -- sixteen minutes -- is one routine: "logits match an independent
# reference implementation". Forty-eight of its forty-nine routines together
# come to under a second.
#
# That routine compares the engine against Reference_Transformer, which is
# what the conformance stage does for 650 s of its own. So the gate has been
# running the engine against the reference twice, once in the suite and once
# in the sweep, and nothing said so, because the suite stage was the one the
# gate did not time.
#
# The other thing this says is what the device tests cost, which two of
# yesterday's theories turned on: the cli case, holding all five of them and
# every device open they make, is 3.2 s. Neither device opens nor pipelines
# built eagerly were ever capable of the minutes they were blamed for, and
# this measurement would have said so in twenty minutes at any point.
#
# Getting it took three attempts and the failures are worth recording. AUnit
# times every routine and hands the numbers to the reporter, but this build
# selects its no-calendar variant, where the times are all zero -- a reporter
# that ranks them prints a column of noughts. perf writes an empty file here
# and gdb returns no backtrace. And driving AUnit twice over one suite object
# in a single process frees something twice, so the loop over routines has to
# be outside the program, a process each.

# 2026-08-19, what the suite costs, and why nobody knew. It reads 1443 s on
# this host with all 259 tests passing, and the README said a second and a
# half. That figure was true once and had not been re-measured; nothing
# checked it, because the gate ran the suite as its first stage and timed the
# four after it.
#
# The cost of not knowing was a day. AUnit prints nothing until it has
# finished, so a suite that outruns its timeout is indistinguishable from one
# that has hung -- and every timeout used against it was set from the
# published figure. Four explanations were offered for that silence in one
# afternoon: a barrier deadlock, a memory coherency fault, a wedged device,
# and pipelines built too eagerly. All four were wrong, and the last was
# tested by building the pipelines lazily, which made the suite slower and
# broke two tests.
#
# Nor was any of it new. Three days earlier the same suite ran past 2400 s
# without finishing, so it has cost tens of minutes since well before the
# work that was being blamed for it:
#
#   18871a3, three days back   over 2400 s, unfinished
#   a60e4e6, this morning      1443 s, 259 passing
#
# The gate now says `took: suite` beside the other four stages, with a bound
# of 2900 s on the same principle as theirs -- roughly twice what was
# measured, because a machine under load takes half again as long and that is
# not news. One gate run would have said all of this in one line.

# 2026-08-19, an attention kernel, checked and not yet wired.
#
# attention.comp does one position attending to everything a cache holds: the
# scores, the bound where an architecture states one, the softmax across them,
# and the values weighted by what comes out. It is the piece of a layer that
# stands between two matrix products and keeps them from being sent together,
# so it is what the rest of the resident chain waits on.
#
# Keys and values share a buffer, because attention wants four arrays and the
# pipeline layout carries three. Widening the layout would have cost every
# shader; laying the cache out as one region costs nothing, since this program
# writes the cache in the first place.
#
# One invocation a head, folding the softmax into the pass that computes the
# scores -- a running maximum, a running sum, a running weighted value -- so
# nothing is stored per position. The host does not do it that way because it
# has the scores in an array anyway; a device wants it because storing a score
# a position is storing a context length of them.
#
# Checked against the same arithmetic in binary64: worst disagreement under
# 1.0E-5 across four heads and sixteen positions. Checked by breaking it too
# -- swapping where the keys and the values begin disagrees by 1.6E-2.
#
# Not wired, and it cannot pay yet: Attend uploads the whole cache on every
# call, which is the round trip this exists to remove. What makes it worth
# having is the cache staying on the device between tokens, and that is a
# change to where the cache lives rather than to this kernel. The device
# figure is unchanged within the day's spread -- 0.596 s wall, 0.488 s
# generating, digest 5709b12b0517c8c5 -- because nothing in the engine calls
# it.

# 2026-08-19, the split explained, and a claim about it corrected.
#
# The experiment that seemed to find the speedup in neither half was recorded
# above as void because one half supposedly could not compile. That was wrong
# twice over: it compiles, and the split needs no explaining away at all.
#
#   cf13815 declares Open_Sequence and carries Run already -- a sequence
#   existed there; what it did not do was record several products into one
#   command buffer. Its Run calls One_Product a step, so a sequence of three
#   was three submissions.
#
#   cf13815's llama has no Product_Group, so nothing ever asked for a
#   sequence.
#
# So half A -- old llama, new products -- has the hoisted Run and no caller
# that groups: every product goes as its own Multiply. Slow, and correctly so.
# Half B -- new llama, old products -- asks for groups and reaches a Run that
# submits per product. Also slow, also correctly so. The whole is fast because
# the saving needs the caller and the callee together, which is what the
# change is rather than a puzzle about it.
#
# The lesson here is narrower than the two above and still worth keeping: a
# result that looks impossible was reached by asserting a compile failure
# instead of checking for one. `git show REV:path | grep` answers that in a
# second. Two of the three surprises of this day came from believing something
# about a build without asking the build.

# 2026-08-19, the gated block in the batched path too, and what it is worth:
# very little, for a reason worth writing down.
#
# Evaluate_Batch reads the prompt; Evaluate reads a token at a time while
# generating. Both now hand a device the whole gated block. The combining step
# was made to work over a batch -- it is elementwise, and both arms are laid
# out the same way by the same kernel, so what that layout is does not matter
# to it -- and the guard that restricted it to one position came out.
#
# On the prompt of the published figure, two tokens, the difference is inside
# the noise: 0.106 s against 0.093 s by median, with the two spreads almost
# entirely overlapping. On a 522-token prompt, where there is something to
# measure:
#
#   before  22.252  22.723  21.523
#   after   21.559  21.534  21.763
#
# Median 22.25 against 21.56, about three per cent, and the spreads overlap
# there too. Both were built with each crate verified.
#
# Three per cent where the token-at-a-time path gave twenty-three, and the
# reason is structural rather than disappointing: a batch of five hundred
# positions already amortizes a submission across five hundred positions'
# worth of arithmetic. Removing one submission a layer matters when a layer's
# work is one position wide and hardly at all when it is five hundred. The
# grouping was always going to pay where it paid, and this says where that is.
#
# It is kept because it is correct, tested, and takes real host arithmetic --
# an activation and a multiply over half a million values a layer -- off the
# processor. It is not kept for the three per cent, and the figures file
# should not later be read as claiming it was.

# 2026-08-19, later: the withdrawal below was itself wrong, and the reason is
# worth more than either claim.
#
# The probes that said Llama.Evaluate never runs were run against a stale
# binary. `tests/bin/tests` links the library statically, so rebuilding the
# library alone leaves the test tool carrying the old code. Several probes
# rebuilt only the library. They showed no change because nothing had
# changed -- not because the path was dead.
#
# Rebuilt properly, every probe reverses. Cutting Evaluate short fails the
# run. A deliberately wrong activation unit moves the digest from
# 5709b12b0517c8c5 to 8920270451e368f6. The path is live, the gated block does
# reach the device, and the grouping was running all along.
#
# The split that "found the difference in neither half" was explained later
# and not this way; see the entry above dated the same day. It compiles, and
# both halves are slow for good reasons. This paragraph was wrong and is kept
# only so the correction has something to point at.
#
# The figure, re-taken with both crates verified to have built, interleaved:
#
#   cf13815 (before grouping)  0.594  0.576  0.598
#   bef8293 (after)            0.451  0.461  0.456
#
# Median 0.594 against 0.456: twenty-three per cent, which is what the
# interleaved run said yesterday before any of this. The speedups stand.
#
# What does not stand is the method. A probe that leaves behaviour unchanged
# proves nothing until the binary under test is known to have been rebuilt,
# and "seven tokens generated" is not a check that a run succeeded. Both
# mistakes were made here in one day, and between them they produced a
# confident retraction of a true result. The rule that follows: build every
# crate that links what changed, verify the build said so, and prefer a probe
# that fails loudly to one that merely differs.

# 2026-08-19, a withdrawal -- WHICH IS ITSELF WITHDRAWN; see above. Kept
# because a wrong conclusion and how it was reached is worth as much as the
# right one. The device speedups this file reported yesterday
# -- eighteen per cent for grouping the queries, keys and values, three more
# for the gated arms, corrected to twenty-three by an interleaved run -- are
# not supported, and the attribution was wrong. What follows is how that was
# found, because the way it was found is the only part worth keeping.
#
# The wiring went into Llama.Evaluate, which takes one token. Generation does
# not call it. `Model_Runner.Generation` reads through Evaluate_Batch, even
# for a single token; Evaluate is reached by the draft model of a speculative
# run and by two other paths, and by nothing the seven-token figure exercises.
# Three probes say so and agree: an `exit` after the first normalization, a
# `return` in the same place, and a deliberately wrong activation unit. None
# of them moved the digest. Code that cannot change the answer when it is
# broken is code that did not run.
#
# So the grouping could not have caused the figure it was credited with. The
# figure itself reproduces -- cf13815 reads 0.59 s and 3c424a2 reads 0.46 s,
# interleaved, twice, on two occasions -- so something between those commits
# is worth twenty per cent and it is not what was claimed. Splitting the
# change in half finds neither half: cf13815 with the new products layer
# reads 0.594, and cf13815 with everything except the products layer reads
# 0.602, while the whole reads 0.50. Both builds were checked to have
# compiled, which the first attempt at this had not been.
#
# A difference that appears only when two halves are combined, neither of
# which contains a live code path, is not a difference anybody has explained.
# It may be code layout; it may be that these readings are worth less than
# they look. Either way the honest state is: the cause is unknown, and no
# device speedup is claimed here until one measurement isolates one change.
#
# What stands: the digest never moved through any of it, so nothing above
# computed anything different. And the machine drift recorded yesterday --
# a quarter, morning to evening, at the same load average -- is the same size
# as everything being argued about, which is the deeper reason this went
# wrong.

# 2026-08-18, how the device figures above were taken, and why the next ones
# are taken differently. The three lines under "the feed-forward joins in"
# were measured one after another over a working session -- apart, then with
# the queries, keys and values grouped, then with the gate too -- each at a
# load under 1.50. That is not good enough, and the way it showed was a
# regression that was not there: a change measured 0.46 s against a published
# 0.385 s, and the commit before it measured 0.46 s as well. The machine had
# moved, not the code.
#
# Taken again by building the two revisions alternately and running each three
# times, twice around:
#
#   ungrouped (cf13815)  0.559  0.594  0.590   and  0.605  0.602  0.604
#   grouped   (3c424a2)  0.472  0.471  0.459   and  0.463  0.449  0.459
#
# Median 0.598 s against 0.461 s: twenty-three per cent, where the sequential
# measurement said eighteen. The effect is real and was understated. What was
# also true, and unsaid, is that the same ungrouped build reads 0.468 s in the
# morning and 0.60 s in the evening at the same load average -- a quarter,
# which is larger than either effect this file has been reporting.
#
# So the absolute wall times in the two-row table are of their moment and the
# ratios between backends are what survive. A comparison between two revisions
# belongs in an interleaved run and nowhere else; a load average under 1.50 is
# not evidence that two figures taken hours apart may be subtracted.

# 2026-08-18, what device-side normalization would be worth: nothing this
# machine can measure. Written down because the question looks obviously
# worth a yes, and the answer is no for a reason that only a measurement
# gives.
#
# The instrument was to run the attention normalization eight times a layer
# instead of once, on every layer of every token, and see what it cost:
#
#   one normalization a layer      0.385 s generating
#   eight normalizations a layer   0.385 s and 0.388 s generating
#
# Seven extra normalizations of every layer of every token cost nothing that
# shows. So the most that moving them to a device could win is the host time
# they take, and that time is below the floor. TinyLlama is 2048 wide and
# twenty-two deep, so a token's normalizations are about 270 thousand
# operations against 55 milliseconds of generating -- half a per cent would
# be a generous ceiling and the instrument says it is smaller.
#
# There is no round trip to remove either, which is the part that is easy to
# get wrong from an armchair. The normalized vector is uploaded once today; a
# device-side normalization would upload the activation once instead. Same
# bytes, same submission. A normalization can only join a submission already
# happening if its output feeds the next step without coming back, and that
# is chaining machinery built for a gain measured at zero.
#
# Where the time actually is, from the figures above: removing three
# submissions a layer bought 0.083 s over seven tokens and twenty-two layers,
# which is about 0.18 ms a submission. A layer still costs four -- the
# queries, keys and values; the attention output; the gate and up; the down
# projection -- and the last three cannot be grouped because each waits for
# the one before. Collapsing those needs the dependent chain to stay on the
# device: the rotation, the attention, the softmax and the residual adds, not
# the normalizations by themselves. That is the shape of the next gain, and
# normalizations are a small component of it rather than a step toward it.

# 2026-08-18, the feed-forward joins in. The gate and the up projection of a
# gated block read the same normalized input with nothing between them, so
# they go to a device together as the queries, keys and values already did.
# The entry point was generalized to a group rather than gaining a second
# fixed shape.
#
#   seven tokens, device, three sent apart   0.468 s generating
#   seven tokens, device, q/k/v together     0.397 s generating
#   seven tokens, device, and the gate too   0.385 s generating
#
# The second saving is much the smaller, and the honest reason is arithmetic:
# grouping three products removes two submissions a layer, grouping two
# removes one. Three runs of the last figure at a load between 1.39 and 1.44
# read 0.393, 0.385 and 0.365 s, so three per cent is the middle of a spread
# that touches the measurement floor -- worth taking and not worth
# advertising. The digest is unchanged at 5709b12b0517c8c5, which is the part
# that is not a matter of degree.

# 2026-08-18, the layer asks for the sequence. A block's queries, keys and
# values are three matrices read against one normalized input with nothing
# between them, and they now go to a device together: one upload of that
# input, one command buffer, one submission, one fence. It is the first thing
# in the engine to record a sequence, and the first device figure in this
# line of work that moves.
#
#   seven tokens, device, before  0.570 s wall, 0.468 s generating
#   seven tokens, device, after   0.497 s wall, 0.399 s generating
#
# Both at a load under 1.50, same model, same prompt, median of three. Fifteen
# per cent off the generating time for removing two submissions and two fence
# waits a layer -- which is worth saying plainly, because the arithmetic did
# not change at all. The digest is the check that it did not: 5709b12b0517c8c5
# before and after, which is what three products sent together have to
# produce if they are the same three products.

# 2026-08-24, three ways of making the device's product faster, none of which
# does. Nothing here changed and no figure below moves; this entry exists so
# that the next attempt starts after these three rather than at them.
#
# The 110-token prompt on the device reads 1.937 s as the shader stands.
#
#   a row's blocks divided between the lanes, with a workgroup slot per row
#   and a tree reduction at the end -- every shape that fits:
#     64 lanes to a row   4.300 s        16 lanes   1.994 s
#      8 lanes            1.834-2.205 s   4 lanes   2.181 s
#
#   the quants read a word at a time rather than a byte at a time, alternated
#   against the byte-wide decode three rounds each, on generation because the
#   prompt did not move at all:
#     word   3.695, 3.622, 3.381 s        byte   3.744, 3.616, 4.595 s
#     the prompt: 2.018 s word against 1.937 byte
#
# The two bracket each other, which is this file's usual way of saying they
# are the same figure. Fourteen passes over 1.09 GiB in 1.95 s is 7.8 GB/s on
# a part with something near ninety, so the bus is not what the shader waits
# for either, and moving fewer bytes was never going to help.
#
# What is left is occupancy and latency: 2048 rows at one invocation each is
# thirty-two workgroups of sixty-four invocations, and each invocation walks a
# whole row of dependent scalar loads. The other runtime reaches 1657 tokens a
# second on this device, so the hardware has thirty times more in it.
#
# Two things found along the way that are worth writing down.
#
# A write-back that gives one vector of the batch to each lane drops vectors
# when there are fewer lanes than the batch is wide. It produced a fifth of a
# prompt with the rest at zero, and the device tests caught it within a
# minute -- the fixture sweep compares the device against the processor in
# every one of the fifteen formats, which is exactly the shape of check that
# catches a kernel doing four fifths of its work.
#
# And `tests shader` recompiling an unchanged source does not reproduce the
# committed words: the digest that guards them is over the source text, so a
# different build of the shader compiler passes the check while writing
# different SPIR-V. That is not a defect -- the digest is there to catch an
# edited source, and it does -- but it means the committed words are evidence
# about whoever last ran the compiler rather than about the source beside
# them. The words in the tree are the ones that were there before this
# attempt, restored, for that reason.

# 2026-08-24, two more ways of making the device's product faster, neither of
# which does, and one number that says where to look instead. Nothing here
# changed; the shader in the tree is the one that was in it.
#
# The 110-token prompt reads 1.054 s on the device and sixty-four generated
# tokens 4.541 s, at the batch the default now asks for.
#
#   vectors carried in one dispatch, which is how many times a prompt passes
#   over the whole model:
#     2   prompt 3.471 s   generating 4.741 s
#     4   prompt 2.451 s   generating 6.881 s
#     8   prompt 1.054 s   generating 4.541 s     <- what it is
#    16   prompt 1.219 s   generating 13.246 s
#
#   the accumulation bounded by the vectors a dispatch was given rather than
#   by the constant, which removes the eightfold waste generating pays:
#     prompt 9.470 s, generating 27.198 s
#
# The first table settles a question rather than failing to improve one. If
# the shader waited on the weights, sixteen vectors a dispatch would have won
# by halving the passes over them; it loses. Together with the 7.8 GB/s the
# entry above computes, that is two independent readings saying the same
# thing: this shader is not bandwidth bound, and no amount of moving fewer
# bytes will help it.
#
# The second is a comment in the shader turning out to be right. A constant
# bound lets the accumulators and the offsets stay in registers; a bound read
# from a push constant puts both arrays in memory, and what the spilling
# costs is nine times what the saved arithmetic is worth. The comment has
# said so since it was written and now has a number beside it.
#
# What is left is arithmetic anybody can do. Sixty-four tokens in 4.541 s is
# 71 ms a token; a token is 22 layers of three submissions; that is about
# 1.1 ms a submission, which lands inside the 0.6 to 2.3 ms a call the batch
# measurement bracketed. Device generation is very nearly all host
# round-trip, and the three submissions a layer are structural: the
# normalizations, the rotation and the cache writes happen on the host
# between the products. Making a layer one recording means moving those to
# the device, which is a larger change than any of the four tried so far.

# 2026-08-24, the device's open question, narrowed by a diagnostic that
# should have existed before the question was asked.
#
# The entry above left the device's 110-token prompt at 1.696 s against
# 0.608 s an hour earlier, and explained it as attention falling back off the
# device. That explanation was a guess about the one number nobody could see:
# this program could report how much of the model the device held and not how
# much of the context. It reports both now -- `--show-stats` says "bytes of
# context on the device" -- and the guess was wrong.
#
#   context on the device        92274688 bytes, at the default budget and
#                                at --device-memory 1200M alike
#   the processor fallback       compiled out entirely, so it cannot run:
#                                the same prompt still reads 1.692 s and
#                                still spends 1.05 s of processor
#   context sizes 256 to 2048    1.898, 1.876, 1.124 and 1.922 s, which is
#                                not a size effect
#   llama.cpp on the same device 1666 tokens a second in the same sitting,
#                                so the part is not throttled
#
# Attention is on the device throughout. What is left is inside the device
# path, and that path has not changed since the sitting that read 0.608 s.
#
# One attempt at a true A/B failed and is worth recording as a warning about
# this repository's own history rather than about the device: checking out
# the commit where 0.608 s was taken does not build, so the figure it
# produced was the current binary being measured twice. Several of the
# commits in that range do not build on their own -- the split that made them
# divided two files inconsistently -- so a bisect over it needs --skip, and
# the reading it gives without one is a reading of whatever was built last.

# 2026-08-25, a layer collapsed into one submission, measured, and taken
# back out. Nothing here moved, because the change did not land -- this is
# recorded so that the next reader does not build it a second time.
#
# The claim it tested was this file's own: that device generation is very
# nearly all host round-trip, arrived at by dividing 41 ms a token by the 66
# submissions a token makes and finding 0.63 ms, which sits inside the 0.6 to
# 2.3 ms a call the batch sweep had bracketed. That is arithmetic landing in
# a plausible range, not a measurement.
#
# What was built: a fourth shader doing the normalizations and the residual
# additions on the device, a step kind for each, steps naming which earlier
# step they read rather than only the one before them, and a barrier emitted
# only where a step reads something not yet visible. With it a layer's second
# half is one submission, and with the next layer's projections carried too a
# whole layer is one. Three configurations, three rounds, alternated, load
# under 1.20 before each, every digest 448c2ed68ec342ee:
#
#   three submissions a layer   2.623, 2.665, 2.650 s generating
#   two, the block fused        2.686, 2.811, 2.767 s
#   one, the whole layer        2.794, 2.824, 2.728 s
#
# Monotonically worse as the submissions fall. Fusing trades submissions for
# pipeline barriers -- three a layer against ten -- and a barrier drains the
# device where a submission does not. Against dispatches this small the drain
# costs more than the call it replaced.
#
# Two smaller things were measured out with it, both on the processor's
# integer kernel and both aimed at the same suspicion, that the kernel is
# bound by re-reading activations rather than by arithmetic:
#
#   keeping the eight lane sums a block product leaves, instead of reducing
#   them to a scalar each time, so that the reduction happens once a row.
#   The prompt goes 1.259 s to 3.321 s -- 2.6 times worse -- because the
#   lane form is no longer a reduction and the compiler stops emitting the
#   sixteen-bit multiply-add it was written around. Only a reduction shape
#   gets that instruction out of GCC.
#
#   storing the quantized activations sixteen bits wide so that the kernel
#   stops widening the same values again for every four rows of the matrix,
#   which is a quarter of its element operations. Three rounds alternated:
#   the prompt reads 1.480, 1.254, 1.313 s at eight bits against 1.369,
#   1.403, 1.414 at sixteen, and generation is level. Doubling the
#   activation array costs more in traffic than the widening costs in
#   arithmetic, which says the kernel is bound by reading activations and
#   not by widening them.
#
# 2026-08-25, a row is computed by eight invocations rather than one. The
# shader and the products body changed, so these two groups moved and were
# taken again.
#
#   six-token run                   cpu 0.541 s   device 0.445 s
#     evaluating the prompt         cpu 0.091 s   device 0.057 s
#     generating                    cpu 0.449 s   device 0.388 s
#     processor time                cpu 3.07 s    device 0.03 s
#   110-token prompt                cpu 1.273 s   device 0.577 s
#     processor time                cpu 7.14 s    device 0.12 s
#   sixty-four generated            cpu 2.527 s   device 2.098 s
#   llama.cpp, processor            381.8 t/s prompt, 40.0 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp, device              1684.8 t/s prompt, 57.4 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp at -ngl 0             774.8 t/s
#
# The change and why it nearly was not kept. One invocation a row has a
# wave's lanes reading one byte each from addresses a row apart, which is
# sixty-four transactions for what fits in one; eight lanes to a row read
# consecutive blocks instead. On the prompt that is a wash -- 0.531 s at one
# lane against 0.537 at eight, 0.545 at four, 0.564 at two, three rounds
# alternated -- and it was going to be reverted on that.
#
# Generating, the same three rounds: 2.973, 2.814, 3.013 s at one lane
# against 2.096, 2.099, 2.105 at eight. Twenty-nine per cent, better in
# every round, and steadier than the row it replaces by an order.
#
# What the coalescing argument missed is that the win is occupancy, not
# transactions. A 2048-row product at 256 invocations a group is eight
# workgroups and this part has twelve compute units, so a third of it idled
# for every token; a prompt is sixteen dispatches deep and hides that, a
# token is one dispatch and does not. Eight lanes makes it sixty-four
# workgroups. Which is why the prompt says nothing and generating says
# everything, and why measuring only the case the change was argued for
# would have thrown it away.
#
# One earlier shape of the same idea was measured and dropped: dividing
# inside a block, each lane taking every eighth element, has all eight lanes
# decode the same scale for an eighth of the multiplies each. 0.603 s
# against 0.520. Dividing by block keeps that work done once.
#
# Shared memory rather than a subgroup add, though this device offers
# clustered arithmetic: that needs SPIR-V 1.3 and so a Vulkan 1.1 instance,
# and this program asks for 1.0. One reduction a row is not worth narrowing
# what the program runs on.
#
# 2026-08-25, the shader decodes four weights out of a word. The device and
# outside groups moved with the shader and were taken again.
#
#   six-token run                   cpu 0.475 s   device 0.425 s
#     evaluating the prompt         cpu 0.079 s   device 0.052 s
#     generating                    cpu 0.396 s   device 0.376 s
#     processor time                cpu 2.70 s    device 0.03 s
#   110-token prompt                cpu 1.213 s   device 0.527 s
#     processor time                cpu 7.30 s    device 0.12 s
#   sixty-four generated            cpu 2.213 s   device 2.001 s
#   device ratios                   q8_0 0.36 one vector, 0.08 at eight,
#                                   0.036 at thirty-two; load 1.09 to 2.79
#   llama.cpp, processor            389.5 t/s prompt, 40.2 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp, device              1682.3 t/s prompt, 57.4 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp at -ngl 0             775.6 t/s
#
# What it bought, alternated three rounds against the byte-at-a-time decode:
# the prompt 0.507 s against 0.540 and generating 2.015 against 2.114, better
# in every round of both, and the digests unchanged -- it is the same
# arithmetic reached by fewer instructions, so an unchanged digest is the
# check that the sign extension is right.
#
# The ceiling this was aimed at was measured first: with the eight-bit
# branch's decode replaced by a constant the prompt reads 0.427 s against
# 0.573, so unpacking was about a quarter of the shader. Rather less than
# that quarter came back, which is the honest reading.
#
# And the thing worth remembering. The first version of this returned the
# four decoded bytes as an ivec4 from a helper, and measured SIXTEEN PER CENT
# WORSE than the byte-at-a-time decode it replaced -- 0.628 s against 0.543,
# consistently. The same four expressions written inline at the point of use
# are the win above. A vector returned across a call is a vector the compiler
# puts in memory, and no amount of counting instructions would have found
# that: the two versions have the same arithmetic and very nearly the same
# instruction count.
#
# Not done, and recorded so it is not reached for first: this device offers
# VK_KHR_8bit_storage, shaderInt8 and storageBuffer8BitAccess, and binding
# the weights as bytes would remove the extraction outright. It also means an
# instance extension for vkGetPhysicalDeviceFeatures2, a device extension,
# and a features struct threaded through the pNext chain at device creation.
# The shader-side fix took most of the same ground for none of that.
#
# covers: the two-row table under "The device backend" -- the six-token run
# and the 110-token prompt, on both backends -- the claim that the two print
# the same digest, the five per-format ratios in the table under it, which
# come from `tests benchmark` rather than from a model, and the three-way
# table of where the weights are, which is the same run with no option, with
# --device-memory 200M and with --device-memory 0. Re-measure with `tests speed --model PATH --backend
# cpu` and again with `--backend device`, which is the command printed beside
# the table. Both sides are listed here because the figure is a comparison:
# a change that makes the processor slower moves it as surely as one that
# makes the device faster.
#
# 2026-08-12, the diagnostics on the refusal paths. Re-measured rather than
# argued about, because the model is on this machine and the tool takes a
# --backend: the device figures moved by a hundredth of a second and the
# processor figures by half of one, which is the machine rather than the
# code and is now said in the README beside the table.
#
# The load reader moved out of the speed tool into a package of its own so
# that the benchmark could use it, which is a change to a file these groups
# name and no change to anything they measure: the reader is called once
# before a run and once after, outside every timed region, and the code
# inside those regions is the same code. Re-fingerprinted without
# re-measuring, for that reason.
#
# 2026-08-14, the benchmark reports the load too, and its five device ratios
# were taken again for it: one whole run, 2.34 rising to 3.11. The bias that
# names itself once the load is printed is now in the README beside them --
# a ratio of the device against the processor, taken while the measurement
# occupies the processor, flatters the device by however busy the machine
# was.
#
# The external-model runner does not report a load and now says why: it
# publishes counts and answers rather than timings, and a line carrying a
# load is a line that differs between two runs of the same check -- which is
# what the published transcripts are compared against, and what broke when
# the field was added there.
#
# 2026-08-14, everything re-measured so that the figures carry the load they
# were taken under, which is the first set that does. Started from a load of
# 0.4 and rose through the burst -- the tool is itself the work, so a run of
# measurements raises the load it reports, and the README says so beside the
# table. The pairs that are compared with each other were taken adjacently,
# and the drafting trio was taken after waiting for the load to fall back
# under one, because the arithmetic under it subtracts one run from another.
#
# 2026-08-13, the shift on the drafting path, and the tool now reads the
# load. Not re-measured, and the reason is the rule's own: both changes are
# off the path these figures walk. The drafting round gained a branch that
# fires when the context is exhausted, which none of these runs does -- they
# use a context of two thousand and generate twelve tokens -- and the load
# is read once before the first run and once after the last, outside every
# timed region.
#
# The machine is at a load of 2.8 as this is written, where the figures
# above were taken at 0.9, so re-measuring now would publish the difference
# between two machines-states as though it were a difference in the code.
# Which is the whole reason the tool now reports the load: from the next
# measurement onwards a figure carries the conditions it was taken under,
# and nobody has to take my word for which afternoon it came from.
#
# 2026-08-13, the rolling context. Apply_Rotary gained a way to turn a
# rotation back, which is a branch inside the innermost loop of the hot path,
# and the session gained a shift. Everything re-measured in one burst with
# the machine quiet -- load average under one when it started. The batch-size
# curve came out lower and now falls to the cap where before it flattened at
# sixteen; that is the same code measured on a quieter machine, and it is
# why the caveat about which machine a figure was taken on is worth keeping.
#
# 2026-08-13, the tool takes a repetition penalty. Added so the batch-size
# table could be taken with the penalty off, on the theory that the default
# one was why this model now answers the long prompt with its end-of-sequence
# token. It was not: the model stops either way, and what used to make it
# continue was the trailing newline the tool no longer passes. The option
# stays because it is one the command has, and the batch-size table is
# published without its digest column for the reason written beside it.
#
# The engine group's other three figures -- the worker-count and share-count
# comparisons and the batch-size table -- were re-measured at the same time.
# They had been left behind when the twelve-token figure was re-taken, so
# this group's fingerprint said re-measured while three of its four figures
# described the old prompt.
#
# 2026-08-13, the digest moved into a function the tests can call. The hash
# is the same hash -- it was inline in the sink and is now a procedure both
# the sink and a public Digest_Of use -- so nothing measured moved, and this
# is a re-fingerprint without a re-measure for the reason the rule allows.
# The figures below were all taken after the batch clamp and the failed-run
# check, which are the only behavioural changes in that file today.
#
# 2026-08-13, a third difference, and the tool joins the sources. Measuring
# with --backend reference produced "0 generated" in a millisecond and the
# tool called it a run: it passed the asked-for batch size to a backend that
# refuses batches, so every prefill failed, and nothing checked the outcome.
# The command clamps the batch to one for such a backend and warns. The tool
# does both now, and refuses to report a run that did not finish.
#
# Every figure the tool takes was re-measured after that, in one burst, with
# the machine otherwise quiet -- load average from other work under one when
# it started. And every group whose figures come from `tests speed` now lists
# the tool among its sources, because three differences between the tool and
# the command had gone unnoticed for as long as the tool existed and no
# fingerprint here was watching the thing doing the measuring.
#
# 2026-08-13, what the tool was measuring. Two differences between `tests
# speed` and the command it exists to reproduce, found by chasing a
# disagreement about how many proposals a drafted run makes.
#
# The tool read the prompt file raw; the command reads it line by line and
# puts the separators back between the lines, so the file's final newline
# never reaches the model. Every figure this tool has published described a
# seven-token prompt for a file the command tokenizes into six.
#
# And the tool sampled with the greedy configuration, which turns off the
# repetition penalty and the filters; the published command sets only
# --temperature 0 and keeps the defaults, a penalty of 1.1 over sixty-four
# tokens among them. That was harmless while penalties did nothing at
# temperature zero and stopped being harmless the day they started working.
#
# Both are fixed and everything the tool publishes was re-measured. The two
# harnesses now agree token for token: sixteen proposals and nine accepted,
# either way. Figures taken before this describe a slightly different run and
# are not comparable with these; where any survive in the README they say so.
#
# 2026-08-13, the draft's worker pool. The draft session was opened without
# one while the target had seven, so every proposal cost seven times what it
# should and the published figure measured the arrangement rather than the
# idea: twelve tokens went from 12.547 s to 3.880 s when the two shared a
# pool. Re-measured and republished, and the break-even arithmetic under
# "Drafting" is what came out of doing the subtraction properly.
#
# 2026-08-13, drafting. Evaluate_Batch grew a per-position logits parameter
# and the decode loop grew a branch; both are on the measured path even with
# no draft model, so all three groups were re-measured. The device's
# seven-token figure came out half a second above the six before it, on a run
# where the processor was slow as well; that is said beside the table rather
# than smoothed away.
#
# 2026-08-13, the sampling work and several sessions. Re-measured and
# republished, all three groups. The samplers are new branches on paths these
# figures do run, and all of them are off at the defaults these runs use, but
# the penalties changed shape -- what acts on a token is one function now,
# called from the greedy path as well -- and that is on the measured path.
# The numbers did not move beyond the spread the machine already shows.
#
# 2026-08-12, keeping the imported range inside memory this process owns.
# Re-measured, all of it, and republished. Worth recording what the numbers
# say about themselves: over five pairs taken this afternoon the device
# column varies by three per cent and the processor column by forty, on a
# machine with other work on it. The device is not competing for the cores
# the other work is using, and that -- rather than any change here -- is
# most of what moves the left column.
#
# 2026-08-12, reading the weights where they lie. Re-measured, all of it, and
# republished. The three-way table is new and was the point of the exercise:
# the import was written expecting it to be the answer to a model that does
# not fit, and it is three times worse than giving matrices back. What it is
# for is a machine that cannot hold the model twice.
#
# 2026-08-12, the memory budget and the eviction. Re-measured, both the two
# model figures and the five benchmark ratios, and all of them republished.
# The processor column moved more between measurements than the device
# column did, again, which is the second time that has happened and is why
# the caveat under the table now gives all three pairs rather than one.
# 2026-08-15, falcon. This group carries llama.adb and moved with it. Not
# re-measured, for the reasons written out under the engine group above.

# 2026-08-15, phi2. The same four groups moved with llama.adb again. What
# was added to a loop this time is an addition: a bias is added to what a
# projection produced, at four places a layer and once at the end, and each
# addition is one pass over a vector the projection had just written
# hundreds of products into. Every architecture but this one has a null
# there and tests it once. Not re-measured, and this machine still has no
# model file to measure with, which is the same reason recorded under the
# engine group above rather than a second one.

# 2026-08-16, --device N. The device group moved with
# model_runner-backend-device.adb, which gained a parameter naming which of
# the host's devices to open and a comparison keeping it beside the budget
# and the sharing policy. Both are in Open, which the figures quote
# separately from what they measure, and neither is in a loop: the device is
# opened once a run. Not re-measured, for the reason recorded under the
# engine group -- this machine has no model file to measure with -- and
# because a run on the same device gives the same figures whether the caller
# named it or took the default.

# 2026-08-16, the byte cache. The four groups carrying llama.adb moved with
# it. What was added to a loop is a third storage: a session that asks for it
# packs a row on the way in and unpacks an element on the way out, both in
# the attention loop, and a session that does not ask for it tests one
# enumeration value more per position than it did. The published figures are
# taken at the default precision, which allocates and touches none of it.
# Not re-measured, and this machine still has no model file to measure with,
# which is the reason recorded under the engine group above.

# 2026-08-16, --kv-cache in the timing tool. All four groups list
# speed_run.adb, which gained a parameter naming the storage a session keeps
# its context in. No engine source moved with it.
#
# And a model file exists on this machine at last, so the reason recorded
# three times above -- that there was nothing to measure with -- no longer
# holds. The engine group's twelve-token figure was taken again on
# TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0, the model every one of these figures describes:
# 1.871 s wall against the published 1.88, 10.76 s of processor time, at a
# load of 1.44 rising to 2.12, and the generated text is the same digest the
# README publishes -- 5abff916f9d83ca6. The figure holds.
#
# The other three groups are not re-measured. What moved is the tool's
# parameter list and nothing they time, and the run above is the evidence
# that the tool still measures what it measured.

# 2026-08-16, the device group, all four rows. Taken again on
# TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0, at the lengths the table publishes and with the
# prompt the repository checks in:
#
#   twelve tokens   device 1.248 s against 1.262 published
#                   processor 2.128 s against 1.777, at a load rising to 1.93
#   110-token prompt device 2.539 s against 2.504 published
#                   processor 6.366 s against 6.295 published
#
# Three of the four are within three per cent. The fourth is the processor's
# twelve-token row on a machine that was busy, and it is the machine rather
# than the code: every one of the eight runs printed the digest the table
# publishes -- 5abff916f9d83ca6 for the short pair, cbf29ce484222325 for the
# long one -- which is what says they answered the same question.
#
# Not republished, because nothing moved: the figures the table carries are
# the figures this machine takes, and rewriting 1.262 as 1.248 would be
# noise dressed as news.
#
# One thing worth recording about reproducing it. The long prompt is
# tests/fixtures/speed-prompt.txt and it is 110 tokens as the tool counts
# them; asking the command for the same file reports 130, because the
# command adds what a chat run adds and the tool measures what the figure
# describes. I chased the difference for a while before noticing which of
# the two was doing the counting.

# load: 1.70 rising to 2.37 for the fifteen ratios and the batched ones,
#       2026-08-15; 1.0 to 1.9 for the two-backend table, 2026-08-14
# 2026-08-17, what this host's device offers. One queue. The family the
# engine computes on has exactly one, so submitting to two is not something
# this machine could be made to do, and a figure comparing one queue against
# two cannot be taken here at all. The run says so itself now rather than
# leaving the number where only a test could see it.

# The device group moved with the backend gaining a way to report its queue
# count, which is read once when a device opens and never in a loop. Not
# re-measured for that: the pair was taken again at the right lengths a few
# entries above, on this model, and reads 1.248 s and 2.539 s against 1.262
# and 2.504 published.

# 2026-08-17, gpt2. The four groups carrying llama.adb moved with it. What
# changed in a loop is one test a token: a model with a table of positions
# adds a row to the embedding, and a model without one asks whether it has a
# table and moves on. Everything else gpt2 needed was already there. Not
# re-measured on that account, and the twelve-token figure was taken again
# on this model two days ago at 1.871 s against the published 1.88.

# 2026-08-18, gpt2's output bias. The four groups carry llama.adb, which
# stopped asking gpt2 for an output.bias -- one enumeration value removed
# from one test at load time, in a branch no published figure's model takes.
# Not re-measured on that account. The twelve-token figure was taken on this
# model two days ago at 1.871 s against the published 1.88.

# 2026-08-20, attention on the device from both evaluators. Re-measured, in
# one sitting, all four cells of the two-row table -- and the table now says
# that its two columns were not taken together, because they were not.
#
# device, 6 prompt tokens and 12 generated: 1.045 s wall, 0.196 s evaluating
# the prompt and 0.847 s generating, 0.36 s of processor time, digest
# 5abff916f9d83ca6, load 1.47 to 1.67. Published, against 1.262 s.
#
# device, 110 prompt tokens and nothing generated: 2.862 s wall, 1.90 s of
# processor time, digest cbf29ce484222325, load 1.50 to 1.80; and again an
# hour later, 2.894 s wall, 1.92 s of processor time, same digest, load 1.45
# to 1.54. The second is published, against 2.504 s, because it was taken at
# the quieter load and is the slower of the two: where a figure got worse,
# the reading that flatters the change least is the one to publish.
#
# That row got fifteen per cent slower and the reason is the change, not the
# machine -- the quieter of the two readings is the slower one, and the load
# it was taken at is below the 1.6 the published figure was taken at. The
# batched evaluator now asks the device for a blend once per position per
# layer where it blended all 110 positions here in one go. The cost is not
# optional: a drafted run checks proposals through the batched evaluator and
# generates through the other, and a device wired into one and not the other
# made those two disagree, which is the failure that prompted the change.
# Where the fifteen per cent actually goes has NOT been measured -- 110
# positions cost 1.90 s of processor time in total, which points at the count
# of submissions and their fences rather than at the arithmetic, but that is
# a suspicion and it is recorded as one.
#
# The processor column was taken beside these and is not published: 3.971 s
# for the short run at a load of 1.45 rising to 2.99, and 12.572 s for the
# long one at 1.50 rising to 3.06, against published figures of 1.777 and
# 6.295. The machine carried a virtual machine belonging to somebody else at
# two thirds of a processor for the whole evening; an earlier attempt at the
# short run waited 120 minutes and never saw the load fall below 1.5 at all.
# Those readings are here rather than published for the reason the kernels
# group gives above: a worse measurement is not a newer one.

# 2026-08-21, a batch of positions attending in one call. The kernel takes a
# workgroup a head of a position where it took a workgroup a head, and the
# batched evaluator submits its whole batch at once instead of a position at
# a time. Both device figures re-measured; the processor column untouched and
# still from its own earlier sitting, as the entry below says.
#
# First, what the previous entry left as a suspicion is now measured, and it
# was right. `tests device-bench` reproduces a whole prompt's attention --
# 110 positions over 22 layers against a 90 MB resident cache -- and reads
# 0.618 s in 2420 calls, 255 microseconds each. The floor of a call, taken at
# one head and sixteen positions where the arithmetic runs at 0.05 Gflop/s
# and is therefore nearly all overhead, is 82.7 microseconds. So between a
# third and a half of every call was submission and fence, which is what 2420
# of them cost. The same triangle as one call a layer reads 0.145 s in 22
# calls: 4.25 times less for arithmetic that did not change.
#
# device, 110 prompt tokens and nothing generated: two readings, 2.371 s at a
# load of 1.49 falling to 1.45, and 2.621 s at 1.45 to 1.46. The slower is
# published, against the 2.894 s of the previous entry and the 2.504 s before
# any of this. Both digests cbf29ce484222325.
#
# device, 6 prompt tokens and 12 generated: three readings, 1.019 s at a load
# of 1.04 to 1.12, 1.081 s at 1.44 to 1.56, and 1.098 s at 1.12 to 1.83. The
# slowest is published, against the 1.045 s of the previous entry. That row
# generates, so its batch is always one position and the kernel change should
# cost it nothing; three readings straddling the figure it replaces is what
# nothing looks like, and the slowest is published rather than the one that
# would let this be read as an improvement. Digest 5abff916f9d83ca6 for all
# three.
#
# One bug found by the suite on the way, worth recording because the figures
# above would have been meaningless with it: the readback copied one
# position's worth of blend whatever the batch size, so every position after
# the first kept what was already in the target. It failed the drafted-run
# agreement test and the device-memory test, and no figure was taken until it
# was fixed.

# 2026-08-21, attention named in the same command buffer as the matrix that
# reads its blend. Re-measured; the processor column untouched as before.
#
# On the pair alone, through `tests device-bench`: 0.456 ms, 0.553 ms and
# 0.848 ms saved across three runs, at two hundred rounds a side. Twenty
# rounds -- what every other row of that bench uses -- was useless here and
# it is worth saying why: the four readings taken at twenty were -0.097,
# +0.379, +0.878 and +0.216 ms, a spread five times the 82.7 microseconds
# being looked for, and the first of them said the change made things worse.
# A conclusion drawn there would have withdrawn something that pays.
#
# End to end it cannot be told from nothing, and is published as such.
# device, 6 prompt tokens and 12 generated: 1.067 s at a load of 1.50 to
# 1.86, 1.088 s at 1.44 to 1.57, and 0.976 s at 1.41 to 1.46. Against the
# three readings before the change -- 1.019 s, 1.081 s and 1.098 s -- every
# reading is lower and the ranges overlap almost entirely. The slowest,
# 1.088 s, is published. Digest 5abff916f9d83ca6 throughout.
#
# device, 110 prompt tokens and nothing generated: 2.628 s at a load of 1.46
# to 1.78, against 2.621 s. That run generates nothing, so it crosses this
# path once a layer rather than once a layer a token, and there was no
# reason to expect it to move. It did not. Digest cbf29ce484222325.
#
# What this leaves open, and it is not answered here: the pair saves half a
# millisecond and a generating run crosses it 264 times, which would be
# 130 ms of a 900 ms run and is not visible in the readings above. Either
# the saving does not survive the surrounding work or the readings are too
# coarse to hold it. Nothing here decides which.

# 2026-08-21, later the same day: the saving published for the joined pair
# was an artifact of how it was measured, and this replaces it. No figure in
# the table moves -- the end-to-end readings were already published as no
# movement, and they were right.
#
# What was published: 0.456, 0.553 and 0.848 ms saved by naming attention
# with the matrix that reads its blend, from three runs of `tests
# device-bench` at two hundred rounds a side. What three later runs of the
# same binary said: 1.036, 0.307 and 0.589 ms lost. The arms ran as two
# blocks -- every apart round, then every together round -- so anything that
# moved on this machine between the blocks landed on whichever arm ran
# second, and it moves: there is other people's work on it.
#
# Alternating the arms round by round puts the same drift through both.
# Four runs then agree. Idle device: 0.836, 0.695, 0.933 and 1.104 ms LOST
# by naming them together. With a layer's other three submissions around the
# pair: -0.005, +0.184, +0.254 and +0.390 ms, which is nothing.
#
# So the mechanism was wrong as well as the number. A submission is not a
# fixed toll: it costs waiting, and a device with work already queued is not
# idle to be waited on. The bench had said as much in a row taken days
# earlier and not connected to this -- four products cost 0.0217 s and the
# same four with an attention among them cost 0.0212 s -- and connecting
# those two would have cost nothing and saved a wrong claim.
#
# The pair stays named together: in a layer, which is the only place the
# engine crosses it, it is free either way, and the blend not returning to
# the host is less work even where the clock will not show it. It is no
# longer said to save time.

# 2026-08-21, both columns of the two-row table in one sitting, and the
# processor column replaced. The call that carries its own cache takes a
# batch now, which is why this group is re-measured at all; the figures moved
# for a different reason.
#
# All four cells back to back, each waiting for the machine to fall below
# 1.50 before starting:
