# that it is the third sitting to say so. The short run read 3.971 s on
# 2026-08-20, 4.016 s the same day, and 3.734 s here, in three separate
# sittings across two days. Twice this file recorded such a reading and kept
# the older figure on the ground that a worse measurement is not a newer
# one. That rule is for one reading against a quiet-machine figure; three
# agreeing readings are not a worse measurement, they are the machine, and
# continuing to publish 1.777 s would be publishing a figure this machine
# does not produce.
#
# Nothing in the engine's processor path changed between those figures and
# these -- the engine group's sources are fingerprinted and its entries above
# record what moved and why. What changed is that this machine now carries
# somebody else's virtual machine at about two thirds of a processor, all
# day, and the processor column competes for cores while the device column
# does not. That is also why the device column barely moved: 1.062 s here
# against 1.088 s published, and 2.631 s against 2.628 s.
#
# One thing worth writing down about the load figures, because it caught me
# out: a cpu run's load-after is mostly its own. Seven workers for eleven
# seconds is seventy-two seconds of processor time, and the load average
# follows. A cpu run can never end below 1.50 on this machine, so what says
# whether it started on a quiet machine is the load before it, which --wait
# is what enforces. The device runs are the ones whose load-after means
# anything about the surroundings.

# 2026-08-22, jina-bert-v2, the unigram road and the byte-pair cutting
# rules. All five groups moved and none of them was measured on a quiet
# machine, because the machine was not quiet at any point: somebody else's
# virtual machine at about two thirds of a processor all day, and two of
# their test suites beside it. `tests benchmark` and `tests speed` both
# refuse above a load of 1.50 and both were run with --anyway, which is why
# every reading below is quoted with its load.
#
# The device group is the one this change could really have moved, and it is
# re-measured and not republished. Both cells, at a load of 3.1 against the
# under-1.50 the published pair waited for:
#
#   device 6 prompt tokens, 12 generated:  1.017 s wall, 0.182 s prompt,
#          0.796 s generating, 0.39 s of processor time, load 3.17 to 3.24
#   device 110 prompt tokens, none:        2.625 s wall, 2.00 s of processor
#          time, load 3.13 to 3.33
#
# Against 0.885 s and 2.174 s published: fifteen and twenty per cent above,
# on a machine at twice the load. The device column is not immune to load
# here and this table's own text says why -- an integrated device shares a
# fifteen-watt budget with the processor it would otherwise be helping --
# so a busier machine is a worse measurement rather than a newer one, and
# the published pair stands.
#
# What does settle something is the digests: 5abff916f9d83ca6 and
# cbf29ce484222325, the same two the published figures carry, from a run
# through the changed kernel. The attention shader grew a fall-off with
# distance and a sixteenth push-constant word; for a model that states no
# bias it skips that branch, and an unchanged digest is the arithmetic
# saying so rather than an argument that it should be.
#
# The other two shaders are not merely equivalent but identical: their words
# in model_runner-shaders.ads are unchanged word for word from the committed
# ones. That was checked rather than assumed, and it had to be: a first
# regeneration passed --target-env vulkan1.1 and rewrote all three, moving
# the SPIR-V version header and eleven other words in each of the two that
# had not changed at all.
#
# The engine, reference and drafting groups are not re-measured, and this is
# the reason. What they carry of this change is llama.adb, and what changed
# there on a path a published figure's model takes is one call a head a
# position a layer -- Head_Slope, which returns on its first statement when
# the model states no bias, which every model in this file does. The rest is
# an enumeration value, membership tests at load time, and tensor names for
# an architecture none of these figures uses. The precedent for a per-score
# test is in this file and is why the fall-off is a guarded loop of its own
# rather than a test inside the score loop: folding the bound into that loop
# once cost twelve tokens 1.83 s to 2.07 s. This is a test per head, which
# is that cost divided by the span.
#
# Recorded rather than published, because a figure not taken is worth less
# than a figure taken badly and said to be bad: the twelve-token figure read
# 4.033 s here against the published 3.743, at a load of 2.73 rising to
# 4.17. Same digest, 5abff916f9d83ca6. It is in the direction and roughly
# the size a busy machine moves this figure, and the machine was busier than
# on the sitting that published 3.734.
#
# The tokenizer group is re-measured under the same conditions and its
# figures are not replaced. It read 0.0304 s for sixty thousand ordinary
# characters and 0.0436 s for sixty thousand brackets, at a load of 4.32,
# against 0.0139 s and 0.0156 s published at 1.11. What that pair is for is
# the ratio between its two halves -- the bound on the marker scan is what
# keeps hostile text from costing what it used to -- and the ratio is 1.44
# here against 1.12 published, both of them a world away from the 1600 the
# unbounded scan gave. The scan now looks at every position whose byte some
# piece of the vocabulary begins with rather than only where the text opens
# a bracket, which is a wider set of positions and a bounded one; that is
# what the second reading is watching, and it did not blow up. The figures
# stand from their own quiet run and are owed a quieter machine.

# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat v1.0 Q8_0, as TheBloke published it -- tinyllama-1.1b-chat-v1.0.Q8_0.gguf, 1170781568 bytes.
# 2026-08-24, the device table as taken, cell for cell, so that every figure
# the README publishes under "The device backend" is written down here with

# reads a position's values landed between the two sittings and generating
# is where attention is worth having. The device column of the long run has
# read 1.212, 2.043 and 1.993 s across three sittings on code that did not
# change it, which is a fifteen-watt part's own business.

# 2026-08-25, a result buffer is allocated out of memory the processor
# caches. The whole group re-measured, because it moves every device figure
# in it and none of the processor ones.
#
#   six-token run, 12 generated          cpu 0.509 s   device 0.563 s
#     evaluating the prompt              cpu 0.083 s   device 0.074 s
#     generating                         cpu 0.426 s   device 0.490 s
#     processor time                     cpu 3.05 s    device 0.04 s
#   110-token prompt, nothing generated  cpu 1.272 s   device 0.610 s
#     processor time                     cpu 7.84 s    device 0.12 s
#   where the weights are                23.19, 3.18 and 1.69 tokens/s
#   five ratios, from `tests benchmark`  q8_0 0.56 one vector, 0.14 at
#                                        eight, 0.043 at thirty-two; q4_0
#                                        0.69; f32 1.33; load 1.04 to 2.06
#
# What the engine asked the device for was one kind of memory: the first
# that is host-visible, coherent and the device's own. That is right for
# what the device reads and wrong for what the processor does -- reading it
# back is uncached, around a tenth of the bandwidth writing it gets -- and a
# 110-token prompt reads about ninety megabytes of results. It is why this
# group's device column read 1.70 s on 2026-08-24 and 0.608 s the day
# before on the same code: which of those a sitting got was the driver's
# choice and not the program's.
#
# So this group now names the file that makes the choice --
# src/platform/posix/model_runner-platform-device.adb -- which nothing here
# named while it decided every device figure in the file. That is the second
# time a figure has turned out to rest on a source no group listed, after
# the default batch, and the rule both of them teach is that provenance
# covers what decides a number rather than what computes it.
#
# The digest claim in this group changed meaning rather than value. The
# six-token run's two backends print different digests now -- 33f48397 on
# the processor and 5abff916 on the device -- because the processor's
# default arithmetic is int8 and the device computes in binary32; the
# processor at --arith f32 prints 5abff916 and takes 1.170 s doing it. The
# 110-token prompt agrees on cbf29ce4 either way.

# load: 1.30 to 1.30 and 1.30 to 1.19 for the six-token pair, 1.19 to 2.70 and 1.50 to 1.46 for the 110-token pair, and 0.49 for the per-format ratios, 2026-08-24
# load: 1.18 to 1.18 and 1.18 to 1.09 for the six-token pair, 1.09 to 1.08 and 1.08 to 1.08 for the 110-token pair, 1.04 to 2.06 for the benchmark, and 0.17, 0.17 and 0.24 before the three residency runs, 2026-08-25
# 2026-08-25, taken again after the processor's kernel changed. Six cells in
# one sitting, each waiting for the load to fall below 1.20:

#   6-token prompt, 12 generated       0.473 s        0.433 s

# nothing in the shader moved. The processor column is the change -- 1.207 s

# 2026-08-25, taken again after the processor's kernel changed. Six cells in
# one sitting, each waiting for the load to fall below 1.20:
#
#                                   cpu, 7 workers    device
#   6-token prompt, 12 generated       0.469 s        0.436 s
#     evaluating the prompt            0.073 s        0.053 s
#     generating                       0.395 s        0.382 s
#     processor time                   2.64 s         0.04 s
#   110-token prompt, nothing gen.     0.800 s        0.547 s
#     processor time                   4.09 s         0.13 s
#
# The device column is unchanged within noise -- nothing in the shader moved.
# The processor column is the change, and the 110-token row has come from
# 1.111 s to 0.800: the device still wins that run, by less than it did.
#
# Two shader changes were built for the device's prompt and neither is kept.
# Building the shader with its activation read replaced by a single address
# -- the wrong answer by the right instructions -- takes the 110-token device
# prompt from 0.516 s to 0.394 over three alternated rounds, so those reads
# are about a quarter of it. Staging a window of the activations in shared
# memory measured 1.399 s: two barriers a window is sixteen a row, and this
# shader's own notes already record a per-block barrier at 4.300 s against
# 1.937. Turning the batch column first, so the eight values a weight wants
# are consecutive, measured 0.746 s -- and needed a push constant telling the
# shader which layout it had, because a fused sequence stages the same buffer
# for an attention step that expects the other one. The turn is worse because
# a lane reads thirty-two consecutive columns of one vector per block, so it
# trades contiguity along thirty-two for contiguity along eight. The ablation
# measured removing seven eighths of the loads, not their scatter, and
# reading it as the scatter is the mistake this file keeps repeating.

# 2026-08-26, taken again after three changes to the processor side. Six
# cells in one sitting:
#
#                                   cpu, 7 workers    device
#   6-token prompt, 12 generated       0.461 s        0.432 s
#     evaluating the prompt            0.067 s        0.052 s
#     generating                       0.387 s        0.379 s
#     processor time                   1.59 s         0.03 s
#   110-token prompt, nothing gen.     0.784 s        0.529 s
#     processor time                   3.77 s         0.12 s
#
# The device column is unchanged within noise; nothing in the shader moved.
# The cpu column's processor time is the share count, and its 110-token row
# has come 1.111 to 0.800 to 0.784 across three sittings.

# 2026-08-26, taken again after a tile of answers was written back in the
# order the target keeps them rather than the order the tile does.
#
# Almost nothing here moves, and that is the finding worth recording. The
# change is worth about four per cent of a prompt -- 0.777 s against 0.744,
# three rounds alternated in one sitting, and a profile that puts the
# procedure it touches at 2.1 per cent where it was 5.1 -- and the spread
# between one sitting and the next on this machine is larger than that. The
# 110-token prompt reads 0.782 s here against 0.784 published. **Alternated
# rounds are the instrument; a table taken a day apart is not.**
#
# Unrolling the strip kernel's block loop was measured in the same sitting
# and is not kept: 54.03 thousand million instructions against 54.79, and
# 0.756 s against 0.738 over three alternated rounds, better in none of
# them. The twenty-one per cent of samples a profile charged to that loop's
# branch and cursor advances was skid from the loads they wait for.
#
# The matrix instruction, built and reverted, 2026-08-26.
#
# This device offers VK_KHR_cooperative_matrix revision 2: sixteen by
# sixteen by sixteen at subgroup scope, for float16 x float16 -> float32 and
# for signed byte x signed byte -> int32, with the unsigned and mixed
# combinations beside them. Fourteen shapes in all and every one of them
# sixteen cubed.
#
# The Vulkan 1.0 floor is not what stands in the way. vkEnumerateInstanceVersion
# is a 1.1 function, so a loader that does not export it is a 1.0 loader by
# definition; asked for through vkGetInstanceProcAddr with a null instance,
# with a fall back to 1.0 when vkCreateInstance refuses, the floor is kept
# and the instruction is still reached where it exists.
#
# Built: the negotiation, the device extension and its feature through the
# pNext chain of the create record, the shapes read back with
# vkGetPhysicalDeviceCooperativeMatrixPropertiesKHR and the usable one kept,
# a fourth shader module and pipeline, and a half-precision matrix-product
# shader for Q8_0 -- sixteen rows by sixteen vectors of the answer at a
# time, weights decoded into shared memory, one operation per sixteen-deep
# step. Compiled with `glslangValidator --target-env vulkan1.3`, which is
# SPIR-V 1.6 and a different invocation from the -V the other three take.
#
# Correct and slower. The whole suite passes with it enabled -- 280 tests,
# conformance 28344 sequences and none outside tolerance -- and forcing it
# on for every product, threshold and all, answers 5abff916f9d83ca6 on
# twelve tokens, which is what the processor and the row shader both answer.
# At its proper threshold the 110-token device prompt reads 1.210 s against
# 0.515 for the shader it would replace: 2.4 times slower.
#
# Then three things were measured, 2026-08-26, and two of them were nothing.
#
#   the row product it would replace                          0.515 s
#   the matrix product, converting the batch per workgroup    1.210 s
#   the same, with the batch converted once beforehand        1.037 s
#   and decoding four quants a word rather than one a byte    1.024 s
#   and with the operand read ablated to a single address     1.018 s
#
# The conversion was worth fourteen per cent and was worth writing a second
# shader for: vectors_half.comp turned the batch into half-precision once,
# into the half of the vector buffer the binary32 copy does not use, with
# the vector buffer made half again as large and a pipeline barrier between
# that dispatch and the products. The matrix shader then loaded its second
# operand straight out of memory as a column-major view -- no shared array
# for it, no conversion in the loop.
#
# The decode was worth nothing, which is the surprise: reading four quants
# out of a word rather than one out of a byte was sixteen per cent in
# row_product.comp and is a thousandth here. And the ablation is the one
# that settles it -- replacing the operand's address with a constant, so
# every workgroup reads the same sixteen values and the batch traffic is
# perfectly cached and perfectly wrong, changes nothing measurable. The
# hundred-and-twenty-eight-fold re-reading of the batch is not what costs.
#
# What is left is the shape rather than any part. A sixteen-row output tile
# is a hundred and twenty-eight workgroups of one subgroup each, which is a
# hundred and twenty-eight waves on a part that runs five hundred for the
# row product; the instruction's smallest tile is sixteen, so more waves
# means more of the batch per workgroup and more work per wave means a
# square tile with several accumulators to a side. That is a real
# matrix-multiply kernel and not this one.
#
# Everything is reverted. Anyone picking it up starts from a shader that was
# correct, from the knowledge that the extension and the 1.0 floor are not
# obstacles, and from three things that are not the answer.

# The six cells of the device table, taken in this sitting:
#
#                                   cpu, 7 workers    device
#   6-token prompt, 12 generated       0.450 s        0.427 s
#     evaluating the prompt            0.070 s        0.051 s
#     generating                       0.379 s        0.375 s
#     processor time                   1.61 s         0.04 s
#   110-token prompt, nothing gen.     0.782 s        0.521 s
#     processor time                   3.69 s         0.11 s


# 2026-08-26, taken again after the four-bit k-quant was given an integer
# kernel. Every figure in this file is Q8_0 and the Q8_0 path did not change,
# which is what these say: the 110-token prompt reads 0.806 s against 0.782,
# twelve tokens 0.458 s against 0.448, sixty-four generated 2.160 s against
# 2.165, and every digest is what it was. The spread between sittings is
# larger than any of those differences, as it has been all along.
#
# Q4_K, which is the change. Medians of three alternated rounds:
#
#   110-token prompt, floating-point path   4.598 s   31.67 s of processor
#   110-token prompt, byte dot product      1.317 s    8.19 s
#   thirty-two generated, either way        0.949 s / 0.930 s
#
# and the digest is 4b6e8e99ae285b2a in all six runs. The integer sums are
# exact either way and the rounding falls in the same places, which is not
# true of the eight-bit format: there the byte instruction groups a block's
# products differently and the README says so. Here it does not, because a
# nibble needs no bias and the minimum's term is taken out in binary64 at
# the end exactly as the floating-point path takes it.
#
# The same sitting, unalternated: 1.375 s for the prompt and 0.844 s
# generating, at a load rising through 1.7.
#
# The device figures are the noisiest rows here and both moved without
# anything touching them: the device's generating row reads 30.9 t/s against
# 27.3 last sitting, and llama.cpp at -ngl 0 reads 872.1 t/s against 774.6.
# Neither is a change to either program.
#

# 2026-08-26, taken again after the four-bit k-quant's generated token was
# given a kernel of its own. As with the batch kernel before it, every figure
# in this file is Q8_0 and the Q8_0 path did not change: 0.738 s for the
# 110-token prompt against 0.806 last sitting, 2.160 s for sixty-four
# generated against 2.160, and every digest what it was.
#
# Q4_K, which is the change. Medians of three alternated rounds:
#
#   thirty-two generated, floating-point path   0.938 s   6.33 s of processor
#   thirty-two generated, byte dot product      0.505 s   2.81 s
#   110-token prompt, either way                1.382 s / 1.442 s
#
# and 4b6e8e99ae285b2a in all six. Fifty milliseconds a token against
# eighty-four, where the eight-bit format takes thirty-three.
#
# Why it was worth doing, which was not obvious and was measured before a
# line was written. The eight-bit format's generated token is bound by the
# memory path and stops getting faster at four workers, so no kernel can
# help it. This one was still getting faster at seven -- 1.510 s at two
# shares, 1.130 at four, 0.993 at seven -- because the floating-point path
# it was taking spends its time unpacking and multiplying rather than
# waiting. The sweep is the reason the work was done and would have been the
# reason not to.
#
# The same sitting, unalternated: 1.468 s for the prompt and 0.505 s
# generating, at a load rising through 1.7.
#
# The drafting row moved a long way and none of it is the draft: the check
# reads 120 ms against 72, on the same three commands and the same models,
# because the eight-bit model it checks for is measured through a
# twelve-token run whose wall is a third loading and warm-up. The conclusion
# is the one it has always been and is now unconditional -- at this
# acceptance a round yields less than the check costs, so no draft price
# pays.
#

# 2026-08-26, taken again after the six-bit k-quant's generated token was
# given a kernel -- the last floating-point path in a "_M" file. Every
# figure here is Q8_0 and the Q8_0 path did not change: 0.763 s for the

# Q4_K_M, which is the change. Medians of three alternated rounds:
#

#   110-token prompt, either way                        1.017 / 0.983 s

# and 4b6e8e99ae285b2a in all six. Thirty-five milliseconds a token where
# the eight-bit file takes thirty-three: a file two thirds the size now
# generates at the same rate.
#
# One of the two single-vector kernels did not work at first, and the reason
# is worth keeping. Every insertion written before them advances the
# pointers it was handed -- an addq on a register declared an input -- which
# tells the compiler nothing and works only while it has no other use for
# that register. With eleven operands it stopped working: the six-bit kernel
# raised inside a worker, and *adding an exception handler and nothing else
# made it run*, which is the signature of code generation rather than
# arithmetic. Both single-vector kernels now keep their cursors in registers
# of their own, named in the clobber list, and advance no operand. The
# four-bit one had the same latent fault and had already passed a gate.
#
# The drafting threshold has now read 17, 10, 6 and 9 milliseconds a token
# across four sittings of unchanged code. It is a difference of two
# twelve-token runs and a twelve-token run is a third loading and warm-up;
# the README says so and says to read the conclusion rather than the number.
#
# The six cells of the device table, taken in this sitting:
#
#                                   cpu, 7 workers    device
#   6-token prompt, 12 generated       0.459 s        0.435 s
#     evaluating the prompt            0.071 s        0.052 s
#     generating                       0.386 s        0.383 s
#     processor time                   1.61 s         0.04 s
#   110-token prompt, nothing gen.     0.763 s        0.529 s
#     processor time                   3.65 s         0.11 s

# 2026-08-26, and the figures above were NOT re-taken. This entry is the
# other thing this file allows -- saying why a change cannot have moved
# them -- and it is the first time that has been used rather than a sitting.
#
# What changed: every machine code insertion in the integer kernels stopped
# advancing the pointers it was handed. They did it with an addq on a
# register declared an input, which tells the compiler nothing and works
# only while it has no other use for that register; the six-bit kernel
# raised inside a worker when it stopped working, and an exception handler
# and nothing else made it run again. Four insertions still did it, all of
# them working and all of them having passed gates, including Rows_Singly,
# which every generated eight-bit token runs and which advanced four
# operands -- more than the one that failed. All six now keep cursors in
# registers of their own and copy the counter rather than decrementing it in
# place.
#
# Why it cannot have moved the figures, and the evidence rather than the
# argument. Three alternated rounds on both models, in one sitting:
#
#                                     before      after
#   eight-bit, sixty-four generated   2.085 s     2.086 s
#   eight-bit, 110-token prompt       0.767 s     0.765 s
#   four-bit, thirty-two generated    0.357 s     0.362 s
#
# and every digest identical -- 448c2ed68ec342ee, cbf29ce484222325,
# 4b6e8e99ae285b2a. That is a better instrument than a fresh sitting would
# be: alternated rounds in one sitting resolve four per cent, and the spread
# between sittings on this machine is larger than that. Re-measuring
# everything would have replaced numbers known not to have moved with
# numbers differing by more than the change does.
#
# Rows_Singly is one instruction a block shorter than it was, three pointer
# advances having become two, which is the only arithmetic difference and is
# below what any figure here resolves.

# covers: the three-row table under "Drafting", the per-token costs derived
# from it and the 382 ms a check of five positions takes, which is derived
# from those. Re-measure with the three commands printed beside the table:
# `tests speed` on the model, on the draft, and on the model with the draft.
#
# Its own group, and generation.adb is in it. The drafting figures hung off
# the device group for a day -- a group whose source list does not include
# the file the drafting loop is written in, so the loop could have been
# rewritten without anything asking for a re-measure. A figure covered by a
# group that does not name its own source is a figure covered by nothing.
# 2026-08-15, falcon. This group carries llama.adb and moved with it. Not
# re-measured, for the reasons written out under the engine group above.

# 2026-08-15, phi2. The same four groups moved with llama.adb again. What
# was added to a loop this time is an addition: a bias is added to what a
# projection produced, at four places a layer and once at the end, and each
# addition is one pass over a vector the projection had just written
# hundreds of products into. Every architecture but this one has a null
# there and tests it once. Not re-measured, and this machine still has no
# model file to measure with, which is the same reason recorded under the
# engine group above rather than a second one.

# 2026-08-16, the byte cache. The four groups carrying llama.adb moved with
# it. What was added to a loop is a third storage: a session that asks for it
# packs a row on the way in and unpacks an element on the way out, both in
# the attention loop, and a session that does not ask for it tests one
# enumeration value more per position than it did. The published figures are
# taken at the default precision, which allocates and touches none of it.
# Not re-measured, and this machine still has no model file to measure with,
# which is the reason recorded under the engine group above.

# 2026-08-16, --kv-cache in the timing tool. All four groups list
# speed_run.adb, which gained a parameter naming the storage a session keeps
# its context in. No engine source moved with it.
#
# And a model file exists on this machine at last, so the reason recorded
# three times above -- that there was nothing to measure with -- no longer
# holds. The engine group's twelve-token figure was taken again on
# TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0, the model every one of these figures describes:
# 1.871 s wall against the published 1.88, 10.76 s of processor time, at a
# load of 1.44 rising to 2.12, and the generated text is the same digest the
# README publishes -- 5abff916f9d83ca6. The figure holds.
#
# The other three groups are not re-measured. What moved is the tool's
# parameter list and nothing they time, and the run above is the evidence
# that the tool still measures what it measured.

# 2026-08-16, the drafting group. Taken with both files present at last, and
# it does not reproduce -- which is the finding rather than a failure.
#
#   eight bits alone  2.040 s against 1.869 published
#   two bits alone    2.211 s against 2.660 published
#   drafted           7.286 s against 5.705, 24 proposed and 9 accepted
#                     against 16 proposed and 9 accepted
#
# The middle row is the tell: the two-bit file measured here is faster than
# the one the table describes, so it is not the same file. Two-bit
# quantizations of one model differ by who made them and how, and this
# repository never recorded which one it drafted with -- so the row cannot be
# reproduced by anyone, including me. The proposal count says the same thing
# from the other side: a different draft model proposes differently, and
# twenty-four proposals for nine acceptances is a worse draft than sixteen
# for nine.
#
# What did hold is the property the row exists to demonstrate: the drafted
# run printed 5abff916f9d83ca6, the same digest as the run without a draft.
# Drafting changed the cost and not the text.
#
# Not republished. A figure taken with a different draft model is not the
# published figure re-measured, and the honest fix is to record which file
# the table means -- which needs whoever took it, not another run.

# 2026-08-17, which two-bit file the drafting table means. History answers
# it as far as it can be answered: the external-model record in this
# repository names its own model as "tinyllama-1.1b-chat-v1.0.Q4_K_M.gguf
# (requantized locally)", so the practice here was to requantize rather than
# to download, and the two-bit draft was almost certainly made the same way.
# Nothing records which source it came from or which tool made it, and a
# locally requantized file is not a thing anyone else can obtain by name.
#
# So the row stands as unreproducible, and what is worth fixing is not this
# row but the rule that let it happen: a figure that describes a model should
# name the file it was taken with, precisely enough to get that file again.
# The external-model record does; these groups do not, and the difference is
# that one of them was written by somebody who had already been bitten.
#
# Every figure in this file now describes TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0 as
# TheBloke published it, which is nameable and obtainable, except the
# drafting row's second model, which is neither.

# load: 1.19 to 2.52 across the three runs, 2026-08-14
# 2026-08-17, gpt2. The four groups carrying llama.adb moved with it. What
# changed in a loop is one test a token: a model with a table of positions
# adds a row to the embedding, and a model without one asks whether it has a
# table and moves on. Everything else gpt2 needed was already there. Not
# re-measured on that account, and the twelve-token figure was taken again
# on this model two days ago at 1.871 s against the published 1.88.

# 2026-08-18, gpt2's output bias. The four groups carry llama.adb, which
# stopped asking gpt2 for an output.bias -- one enumeration value removed
# from one test at load time, in a branch no published figure's model takes.
# Not re-measured on that account. The twelve-token figure was taken on this
# model two days ago at 1.871 s against the published 1.88.

# 2026-08-20, attention on the device from both evaluators. The four groups
# carry llama.adb, which gained two subprograms and three call sites: a
# position's keys and values are written to a cache a device holds, and a
# position attends to that cache there rather than here. This was already
# said while generating; saying it while a batch is evaluated is what this
# change adds, and the reason it had to be added is that a drafted run
# checks its proposals through the batched evaluator while it generates
# through the other, so a device wired into one and not the other made a
# drafted run disagree with an undrafted one.
#
# The device group is re-measured; this group is not, and the reason is
# that the code cannot run here. Put_Position returns on its first
# statement unless the session's backend is the device one, and
# Attend_There is reached only when Put_Position has come back saying the
# device took the position, which off the device it never does. What these
# figures pay for the change is one enumeration comparison per position per
# layer, on a path that reads a matrix per position per layer.
#

# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat v1.0 Q8_0 as TheBloke published it, drafted by a two-bit file requantized locally whose source and tool nobody recorded. That second file is why this group cannot be reproduced, and saying so here is the point of this line.
# load: 1.46 to 1.99 for the model, 1.42 to 1.42 for the draft and 1.39 to 2.41 for the two together, 2026-08-24
# 2026-08-25, taken again, all three at a load of 1.0 to 1.7, medians of
# three: TinyLlama-1.1B Q8_0 twelve tokens in 0.471 s (39 ms a token), the
# same model at two bits in 1.728 s (144 ms), the first drafted by the second
# in 3.825 s with 24 proposed and 7 accepted.

# The arithmetic from those three: 24 x 144 = 3.46 s of draft passes leaves
# 0.37 s for six checks, 62 ms to check five positions against 39 ms for one
# token generated normally. A draft would have to cost under about 6 ms a
# token to pay here and this one costs 144. The threshold has tightened with
# every reading -- it was 24 ms -- because the model being drafted for keeps
# getting faster and the draft does not.

# generates at the same rate.
#
# One of the two single-vector kernels did not work at first, and the reason
# is worth keeping. Every insertion written before them advances the
# pointers it was handed -- an addq on a register declared an input -- which
# tells the compiler nothing and works only while it has no other use for
# that register. With eleven operands it stopped working: the six-bit kernel
# raised inside a worker, and *adding an exception handler and nothing else
# made it run*, which is the signature of code generation rather than
# arithmetic. Both single-vector kernels now keep their cursors in registers
# of their own, named in the clobber list, and advance no operand. The
# four-bit one had the same latent fault and had already passed a gate.
#
# The drafting threshold has now read 17, 10, 6 and 9 milliseconds a token
# across four sittings of unchanged code. It is a difference of two
# twelve-token runs and a twelve-token run is a third loading and warm-up;
# the README says so and says to read the conclusion rather than the number.

# 2026-08-26, and the figures above were NOT re-taken. This entry is the
# other thing this file allows -- saying why a change cannot have moved
# them -- and it is the first time that has been used rather than a sitting.
#
# What changed: every machine code insertion in the integer kernels stopped
# advancing the pointers it was handed. They did it with an addq on a
# register declared an input, which tells the compiler nothing and works
# only while it has no other use for that register; the six-bit kernel
# raised inside a worker when it stopped working, and an exception handler
# and nothing else made it run again. Four insertions still did it, all of
# them working and all of them having passed gates, including Rows_Singly,
# which every generated eight-bit token runs and which advanced four
# operands -- more than the one that failed. All six now keep cursors in
# registers of their own and copy the counter rather than decrementing it in
# place.
#
# Why it cannot have moved the figures, and the evidence rather than the
# argument. Three alternated rounds on both models, in one sitting:
#

# is the one it has always been and is now unconditional -- at this

# covers: the tokenizer's cost on hostile text -- 0.039 s for sixty thousand
# ordinary characters, 25.5 s for sixty thousand brackets before the marker
# scan was bounded and 0.045 s after -- re-measured against the sources this
# fingerprint names, which is why the figure moved from the 0.051 first
# reported: the same run on a quieter machine. Measured with
#   model_runner run tests/fixtures/tiny-model.gguf --raw --prompt-file F \
#     --max-tokens 1
# on a file of sixty thousand characters, timed by the shell, with F holding
# all 'a' and then all '<'. The prompt is refused for length in every case and
# the time is spent before that refusal.
#
# These went in as prose and not as a group, which made them the only measured
# figures in the README that nothing guarded -- and the ones most likely to
# move, because they are about a scan somebody may well touch again. 'tests
# fuzz' watches the same thing continuously as its worst case in milliseconds,
# but that is a limit and not a figure: it says the cost is not absurd, not
# what the cost is.
# load: 1.12, one `tests benchmark` run, 2026-08-14. The 25.5 s that says
#       why the bound exists is history and needs the commit before it; it
#       is not reproducible and is quoted as a reason rather than a figure.
# model: none. This figure times the tokenizer on text this tool builds itself.
# 2026-08-27, the tokenizer: twenty-one million lookups where seventeen
# thousand will do, and a user's wall clock halved.
#
# The entry above found it by accident. A device prompt reported 2.98 s of
# processor for 3.755 s of wall, and two thirds of that turned out to be
# tokenizing: 2.05 s for a 1419-token prompt, which is 1.45 milliseconds a
# token and more of the run than evaluating the prompt it produces. It is in
# no published figure because the speed tool times it apart from the
# evaluation, so nothing here had ever looked at it.
#
# What it was. The SentencePiece road merges the best-scoring adjacent pair
# until no pair names a vocabulary entry, and it found that pair by walking
# every surviving pair, building the two symbols into one string and looking
# that string up. A pass per merge, and the comment above it said so. For
# this prompt that is about six thousand seven hundred symbols falling to
# fourteen hundred, so five thousand two hundred and fifty merges over an
# average of four thousand pairs:
#
#   about 21 million concatenations and hash lookups
#
# What a merge actually changes is two pairs: the one the merged symbol now
# begins, and the one that ends where it begins. So the worth of every pair
# is worked out once and those two are worked out again:
#
#   6669 + 2 x 5250 = 17169 lookups, twelve hundred times fewer
#
# The pass that remains compares floats and is still linear in the surviving
# symbols, so this is the same O(n^2) it was -- what changed is that the
# constant is a float compare rather than a string built on the heap and
# hashed. A heap over the pairs would make it O(n log n) and is not needed
# to make the figure stop mattering.
#
#   tokenizing the 1419-token prompt   2.053 s -> 0.143 s   14.4x
#
# And on the run a user waits for, three alternated pairs, device backend:
#
#   wall              6.076 s -> 4.033 s   1.51x
#   processor time    3.13 s  -> 1.07 s
#   evaluating        3.52 s  -> 3.54 s    untouched, as it must be
#
# Bit for bit the same tokens. The scan runs over the same symbols in the
# same order -- a merge keeps the left symbol's index, so the living symbols
# stay in increasing index order -- and takes a pair only on a strictly
# greater score, so the leftmost of equals still wins. The token streams of
# all three speed fixtures hash the same before and after, and the device
# prompt still prints 1a26d24d33b8957b.
#
# Which vocabularies it reaches, 3000 characters of the same text:
#
#   TinyLlama, SentencePiece   0.53 s -> 0.11 s
#   Phi-3, SentencePiece       0.50 s -> 0.08 s
#   Gemma 3                    0.58 s -> 0.54 s
#   GPT-2, byte pair           0.15 s -> 0.15 s
#   Qwen 3, byte pair          0.46 s -> 0.47 s
#
# The byte-pair road is untouched and was never the problem: it merges
# within a word, where the counts are small and its own comment says a pass
# per merge is right. The Unigram road is a Viterbi walk and never had this
# shape.
#
# The covered figures were re-measured and did not move: sixty thousand
# ordinary characters read 0.040 s against the recorded 0.039 and sixty
# thousand brackets 0.045 against 0.045, timed over twenty runs apiece
# rather than one, because the shell's clock resolves to ten milliseconds
# and the figure is four of them. The fixture vocabulary is not
# SentencePiece and sixty thousand identical characters is not a shape this
# change touches, which is why.
#
# One thing worth saying about how this was found. Nothing was looking for
# it. It fell out of asking why a device prompt used 2.98 s of processor
# time, which was asked because the phase counters did not add up to the
# wall clock. The figure that mattered most to a user this whole session was
# the one no published figure contained.
#
# load: 0.55 to 0.86, 2026-08-27.

tokenizer 4CA182703733E5A2 src/library/model_runner-tokenizer.adb

# 2026-08-24, what the other runtime costs for the same file. The
# reference-runtime comparison has always been about agreement -- same
# tokens, same greedy continuation, same pooled vector -- and never about
# time, so the README could say how fast this engine is and not how fast
# that is a thing to be. Both sides, one sitting, one host:
#
#                             prompt 110    generating 64
#   model_runner, processor     21.5 t/s        11.0 t/s
#   llama.cpp, processor       387.6 t/s        40.0 t/s
#   model_runner, device        54.1 t/s        16.2 t/s
#   llama.cpp, device         1670.4 t/s        57.4 t/s
#
# The seconds behind the two model_runner rows, which is what `tests speed`
# reports: 5.128 s and 5.834 s on the processor, 2.033 s and 3.940 s on the
# device, medians of three. The prompt row generates nothing -- the file
# answers this prompt with its end token -- so `--max-tokens 0` is what it
# asks for and the generation row uses the short prompt.
#
# The other runtime was `llama-bench` at 95b8e33e1, build 10597, three
# repetitions each, `-t 8` for the processor rows. `--device none` rather
# than `-ngl 0`: with a Vulkan device present `-ngl 0` still evaluates the
# prompt on it and reads 880.6 t/s, which is the device measured and
# labelled as the processor.
#
# Generating is the bus and the ratio says so -- about 44 GB/s of this model
# against about 12. The prompt is not the same finding: that runtime writes
# a batch as a matrix-matrix product against hand-written kernels and this
# one shares a reading of the weights between the tokens of a batch and
# multiplies a row at a time.
#
# The device generating figure is the one to distrust: 16.2 t/s here, 13.3
# two hours earlier at a comparable load on unchanged code. Everything else
# in the table repeats to a few per cent.
#
# The fingerprint is the device group's, source for source, because these
# figures ran both backends through the same tool. It will go stale with
# that group and should be re-measured with it -- and re-measuring means
# having llama.cpp built, which nothing here checks and nobody should have
# to install to run the suite.

# covers: the four rows of the llama.cpp comparison table and the seconds quoted under it, and the 875.7 t/s that says why the command names --device none
# load: 0.16 to 1.69 and 0.87 to 2.70 for the processor rows, 0.75 to 0.69 and 0.69 to 0.68 for the device ones, and 0.68 and 0.97 at the start of the two llama-bench runs, 2026-08-24
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat v1.0 Q8_0, as TheBloke published it -- tinyllama-1.1b-chat-v1.0.Q8_0.gguf, 1170781568 bytes -- read by both runtimes.
# load: 1.39 to 1.89 and 1.47 to 2.92 for the processor rows, 1.50 to 1.46 and 1.46 to 1.39 for the device ones, and 1.39 and 2.40 at the start of the two llama-bench runs, 2026-08-24

# 2026-08-26, taken again after a tile of answers was written back in the
# order the target keeps them rather than the order the tile does.
#
# Almost nothing here moves, and that is the finding worth recording. The
# change is worth about four per cent of a prompt -- 0.777 s against 0.744,
# three rounds alternated in one sitting, and a profile that puts the
# procedure it touches at 2.1 per cent where it was 5.1 -- and the spread
# between one sitting and the next on this machine is larger than that. The
# 110-token prompt reads 0.782 s here against 0.784 published. **Alternated
# rounds are the instrument; a table taken a day apart is not.**

# and is not kept: 54.03 thousand million instructions against 54.79, and

# 2026-08-26, taken again after the four-bit k-quant was given an integer
# kernel. Every figure in this file is Q8_0 and the Q8_0 path did not change,
# which is what these say: the 110-token prompt reads 0.806 s against 0.782,
# twelve tokens 0.458 s against 0.448, sixty-four generated 2.160 s against
# 2.165, and every digest is what it was. The spread between sittings is
# larger than any of those differences, as it has been all along.
#
# Q4_K, which is the change. Medians of three alternated rounds:
#
#   110-token prompt, floating-point path   4.598 s   31.67 s of processor
#   110-token prompt, byte dot product      1.317 s    8.19 s
#   thirty-two generated, either way        0.949 s / 0.930 s
#
# and the digest is 4b6e8e99ae285b2a in all six runs. The integer sums are
# exact either way and the rounding falls in the same places, which is not
# true of the eight-bit format: there the byte instruction groups a block's
# products differently and the README says so. Here it does not, because a
# nibble needs no bias and the minimum's term is taken out in binary64 at
# the end exactly as the floating-point path takes it.
#
# The same sitting, unalternated: 1.375 s for the prompt and 0.844 s
# generating, at a load rising through 1.7.
#
# The device figures are the noisiest rows here and both moved without
# anything touching them: the device's generating row reads 30.9 t/s against
# 27.3 last sitting, and llama.cpp at -ngl 0 reads 872.1 t/s against 774.6.
# Neither is a change to either program.

# 2026-08-26, taken again after the four-bit k-quant's generated token was
# given a kernel of its own. As with the batch kernel before it, every figure
# in this file is Q8_0 and the Q8_0 path did not change: 0.738 s for the
# 110-token prompt against 0.806 last sitting, 2.160 s for sixty-four
# generated against 2.160, and every digest what it was.
#
# Q4_K, which is the change. Medians of three alternated rounds:
#
#   thirty-two generated, floating-point path   0.938 s   6.33 s of processor
#   thirty-two generated, byte dot product      0.505 s   2.81 s
#   110-token prompt, either way                1.382 s / 1.442 s
#
# and 4b6e8e99ae285b2a in all six. Fifty milliseconds a token against
# eighty-four, where the eight-bit format takes thirty-three.
#
# Why it was worth doing, which was not obvious and was measured before a
# line was written. The eight-bit format's generated token is bound by the
# memory path and stops getting faster at four workers, so no kernel can
# help it. This one was still getting faster at seven -- 1.510 s at two
# shares, 1.130 at four, 0.993 at seven -- because the floating-point path
# it was taking spends its time unpacking and multiplying rather than
# waiting. The sweep is the reason the work was done and would have been the
# reason not to.
#
# The same sitting, unalternated: 1.468 s for the prompt and 0.505 s
# generating, at a load rising through 1.7.
#
# The drafting row moved a long way and none of it is the draft: the check
# reads 120 ms against 72, on the same three commands and the same models,
# because the eight-bit model it checks for is measured through a
# twelve-token run whose wall is a third loading and warm-up. The conclusion
# is the one it has always been and is now unconditional -- at this
# acceptance a round yields less than the check costs, so no draft price
# pays.

# by element, 83.1 per cent Q4_K and 16.9 per cent Q6_K. A profile of the
# prompt before this change put accumulate_dot -- the floating-point row
# product, which by then meant Q6_K and nothing else -- at 44.7 per cent,
# beside the Q4_K kernel's 43.6. One sixth of the weights was costing half
# the time.

#   110-token prompt, Q6_K on the floating-point path   1.355 s   8.29 s cpu

# The same sitting, unalternated: 0.994 s for the prompt and 0.508 s
# generating.

# The drafting check reads 53 ms here against 72 and 120 in the two sittings

# 2026-08-26, taken again after the six-bit k-quant's generated token was
# given a kernel -- the last floating-point path in a "_M" file. Every
# figure here is Q8_0 and the Q8_0 path did not change: 0.763 s for the
# 110-token prompt against 0.719, 2.159 s for sixty-four generated against
# 2.187, and every digest what it was.
#
# Q4_K_M, which is the change. Medians of three alternated rounds:
#
#   thirty-two generated, six-bit on the floating-point path   0.505 s
#   thirty-two generated, six-bit on the byte product          0.356 s
#   110-token prompt, either way                        1.017 / 0.983 s
#
# and 4b6e8e99ae285b2a in all six. Thirty-five milliseconds a token where
# the eight-bit file takes thirty-three: a file two thirds the size now
# generates at the same rate.
#
# One of the two single-vector kernels did not work at first, and the reason
# is worth keeping. Every insertion written before them advances the
# pointers it was handed -- an addq on a register declared an input -- which
# tells the compiler nothing and works only while it has no other use for
# that register. With eleven operands it stopped working: the six-bit kernel
# raised inside a worker, and *adding an exception handler and nothing else
# made it run*, which is the signature of code generation rather than
# arithmetic. Both single-vector kernels now keep their cursors in registers
# of their own, named in the clobber list, and advance no operand. The
# four-bit one had the same latent fault and had already passed a gate.
#
# The drafting threshold has now read 17, 10, 6 and 9 milliseconds a token
# across four sittings of unchanged code. It is a difference of two
# twelve-token runs and a twelve-token run is a third loading and warm-up;
# the README says so and says to read the conclusion rather than the number.

# 2026-08-26, and the figures above were NOT re-taken. This entry is the
# other thing this file allows -- saying why a change cannot have moved
# them -- and it is the first time that has been used rather than a sitting.
#
# What changed: every machine code insertion in the integer kernels stopped
# advancing the pointers it was handed. They did it with an addq on a
# register declared an input, which tells the compiler nothing and works
# only while it has no other use for that register; the six-bit kernel
# raised inside a worker when it stopped working, and an exception handler
# and nothing else made it run again. Four insertions still did it, all of
# them working and all of them having passed gates, including Rows_Singly,
# which every generated eight-bit token runs and which advanced four
# operands -- more than the one that failed. All six now keep cursors in
# registers of their own and copy the counter rather than decrementing it in
# place.
#
# Why it cannot have moved the figures, and the evidence rather than the
# argument. Three alternated rounds on both models, in one sitting:
#
#                                     before      after
#   eight-bit, sixty-four generated   2.085 s     2.086 s
#   eight-bit, 110-token prompt       0.767 s     0.765 s
#   four-bit, thirty-two generated    0.357 s     0.362 s
#
# and every digest identical -- 448c2ed68ec342ee, cbf29ce484222325,
# 4b6e8e99ae285b2a. That is a better instrument than a fresh sitting would
# be: alternated rounds in one sitting resolve four per cent, and the spread
# between sittings on this machine is larger than that. Re-measuring
# everything would have replaced numbers known not to have moved with
# numbers differing by more than the change does.
#
# Rows_Singly is one instruction a block shorter than it was, three pointer
# advances having become two, which is the only arithmetic difference and is
# below what any figure here resolves.

# 2026-08-26, taken after the five-bit k-quant was given both kernels.
#
# Every figure in this file is Q8_0 or Q4_K, and neither path changed except
# for one comparison in a dispatch, so the first table here is a control
# rather than a new number. Medians of three alternated rounds, one sitting:
#
#                                       before      after
#   eight-bit, 110-token prompt         0.763 s     0.791 s
#   eight-bit, sixty-four generated     2.163 s     2.173 s
#   four-bit, 110-token prompt          0.977 s     1.015 s
#   four-bit, its ten generated         0.435 s     0.435 s
#
# and every digest what it was -- cbf29ce484222325, 448c2ed68ec342ee,
# 4b6e8e99ae285b2a. The two prompts read about four per cent slower, which
# is inside this instrument's floor and is not in the same direction in
# every round: the eight-bit prompt is slower in two of three rounds and
# faster in the third, and the four-bit one slower in two and faster in one.
#
# A note about the four-bit generating row, because the README's figures for
# it say "thirty-two generated" and this run reports ten. The model answers
# fixtures/speed-prompt-short.txt with its end token after ten, so
# --max-tokens 32 has always been measuring ten tokens on that file. Both
# sides of every alternated pair measured the same ten, so the comparison
# holds; the label does not, and it is the label that wants reading with
# care.
#
# Q5_K, which is the change. Medians of three alternated rounds, better in
# every one:
#
#   110-token prompt, floating-point path   4.193 s   29.09 s of processor
#   110-token prompt, byte dot product      1.061 s    5.73 s
#   thirty-two generated, floating-point    3.330 s   19.45 s
#   thirty-two generated, byte product      1.241 s    4.32 s
#
# and the digests are cbf29ce484222325 and 0a1a63f0305d35d6 in all twelve
# runs. The integer sums are exact either way and the rounding falls in the
# same places, as for the four-bit format and unlike the eight-bit one.
#
# The model was made here rather than downloaded, and that is worth stating
# because it is a fixture nobody else has: llama-quantize at 95b8e33e1,
# build 10597, --allow-requantize, from tinyllama-1.1b-chat-q8_0.gguf to
# Q5_K_M -- 783 MB, and a genuine "_M" mixture of Q5_K, Q6_K and Q8_0
# tensors. Requantizing costs quality, and nothing in this file measures
# quality: the conformance sweep does that, on synthetic fixtures, and reads
# 28344 sequences with none outside tolerance. For timing, a requantized
# file is a Q5_K_M file.
#
# The device, drafting, scaling, reference, kernels and engine figures were
# not re-taken beyond the control above. Their fingerprints moved because
# every one of those source lists names the integer kernels, and what
# changed there is two procedures nothing but a Q5_K product enters and one
# comparison in the dispatch that chooses between them. No device source, no
# generation source and no reference source changed at all, and none of
# those figures is taken on a five-bit model.

# 2026-08-26, the device's matrix instruction -- built again, and kept.
#
# What changed. A batch of eight-bit weights on a device that offers
# VK_KHR_cooperative_matrix is computed as a matrix product: thirty-two rows
# by a hundred and twenty-eight vectors of the answer to a workgroup, one
# subgroup of sixty-four, two weight matrices against eight vector ones, and
# the weights decoded into two kilobytes of shared memory a block at a time.
# It replaces a row product repeated once per eight vectors, which read the
# whole matrix sixteen times for a batch of a hundred and twenty-eight.
#
# The same instruction was built and reverted twice before, at a sixteen-row
# output tile, where it was correct and 2.4 times slower. The entries above
# record that and the three ablations that said what was not to blame. This
# is the shape those entries said was left to try.
#
# The A/B, which is what the change is about. Three alternated rounds in one
# sitting, one binary with the capability turned off against the same binary
# with it on:
#
#   110-token device prompt        0.527 s  ->  0.280 s
#   sixty-four generated, device   2.363 s  ->  2.383 s
#
# better in every round on the prompt, and inside the floor on generating,
# which it has to be: a generated token is one vector, the narrowest matrix
# the instruction has is sixteen, and the host refuses the kernel a batch
# shorter than thirty-two. Every digest unchanged -- cbf29ce484222325 on the
# prompt and 448c2ed68ec342ee generating.
#
# The device table under "The device backend", re-measured whole in one
# sitting, each cell waiting for the load to fall below 1.20:
#
#                                        cpu, 7 workers    device
#   6-token prompt, 12 generated             0.450 s      0.431 s
#     evaluating the prompt                  0.069 s      0.053 s
#     generating                             0.381 s      0.378 s
#     processor time                         1.61 s       0.04 s
#   110-token prompt, nothing generated      0.763 s      0.280 s
#     processor time                         3.66 s       0.10 s
#
# with 5abff916f9d83ca6 on the twelve-token run and cbf29ce484222325 on the
# prompt, which is what every other path in the program answers.
#
# The llama.cpp comparison, same sitting, llama.cpp at 95b8e33e1 build 10597,
# three repetitions each, -t 8 for the processor rows:
#
#                              prompt 110    generating 64
#   model_runner, processor      146.9 t/s       30.5 t/s
#   llama.cpp, processor         355.1 t/s       39.8 t/s
#   model_runner, device         413.5 t/s       28.1 t/s
#   llama.cpp, device           1672.9 t/s       57.5 t/s
#
# The seconds behind the two model_runner rows, which is what tests speed
# reports: 0.749 s and 2.095 s on the processor, 0.266 s and 2.279 s on the
# device, medians of three. The device's prompt row is the one that moved,
# from 207.9 t/s; the gap to the other runtime on it went from eight times to
# four. `--device none` rather than `-ngl 0`: with a Vulkan device present
# -ngl 0 reads 883.8 t/s, which is the device measured and labelled as the
# processor.
#
# The shader's own rates, taken in a scratch harness that does nothing else
# and counts the half-precision copy of the batch against itself, on the
# shapes this model is made of, a hundred and twenty-eight vectors each:
#
#   2048 x 2048    1845 GFLOP/s
#   5632 x 2048    2249 GFLOP/s
#   2048 x 5632    2039 GFLOP/s
#   32000 x 2048   3842 GFLOP/s
#
# and the four measurements that chose the tile, three of which went the
# other way from the guess:
#
#   four vector matrices against one weight matrix   957 GFLOP/s
#   one vector matrix against four weight ones       328
#   one subgroup to a workgroup                     2005
#   two subgroups                                   1709
#   four subgroups                                  1512
#   a step of thirty-two columns                    2012
#   a step of sixty-four                            1634
#   loops bounded by a constant                     2031
#   loops bounded by what the batch really holds    1647
#
# The last of those is why the host rounds the batch up to a whole tile and
# the copying shader zeroes what the rounding invents: a loop whose length is
# not known when it is compiled is a loop whose accumulators cannot stay in
# registers, and it costs a fifth of the speed.
#
# The processor figures were not re-taken beyond the two rows above, and no
# processor source changed. The kernels, engine, reference, drafting and
# scaling groups did not go stale, which is the fingerprints saying the same
# thing: nothing outside the device path moved.

# 2026-08-26, the four-bit k-quant through the device's matrix instruction.
#
# What changed: matrix_product.comp decodes Q4_K as well as Q8_0. Only the
# decode differs -- a value is the scale times a nibble less a minimum,
# worked out in binary32 and rounded once into the tile -- so the two share
# a shader and the branch is on a push constant.
#
# Three alternated rounds in one sitting, one binary with Q4_K refused by
# the kernel against the same binary with it allowed:
#
#   Q4_K_M, 110-token device prompt   0.891 s  ->  0.402 s
#   Q8_0,   110-token device prompt   0.287 s  ->  0.286 s
#   Q4_K_M, its ten generated         0.731 s  ->  0.725 s
#
# better in every round on the four-bit prompt, and inside the floor on the
# other two, which they have to be: the eight-bit path is the same
# instructions it was, and a generated token is one vector and the kernel
# refuses a batch shorter than thirty-two. Digests cbf29ce484222325 and
# 4b6e8e99ae285b2a throughout.
#
# The shader's own rate, from the same scratch harness as the entry above,
# 2048 x 2048 against a hundred and twenty-eight vectors and the
# half-precision copy counted against it: 1868 GFLOP/s for Q4_K and 1840 for
# Q8_0 in the same run.
#
# The device table under "The device backend" and the llama.cpp comparison
# were NOT re-taken. Both are Q8_0, the eight-bit path did not change, and
# the control above says so with a number: 0.287 s against 0.286 on the very
# figure those tables are built from, which is inside a floor of five per
# cent. Their fingerprints moved because the shader and the dispatch they
# name did.
#
# One thing this entry exists to record as much as the figures. The matrix
# product was committed in the entry above with a gate that could not enter
# it: the conformance sweep's longest sequence is eight tokens and the
# device format test used twelve rows against a batch of ten, and the kernel
# refuses a row count its thirty-two-row tile does not divide and a batch
# shorter than thirty-two. Every check it passed was a check of the shader
# beside it. The device format test now runs its fifteen formats a second
# time at sixty-four rows and a batch of forty, and the bounds the two paths
# are held to are measured rather than guessed:
#
#   row product against the processor, every format, both shapes   2.1e-5
#   the eight-bit tile against the processor                       7.1e-3
#   the four-bit tile against the processor                        7.4e-3
#
# Three hundred times as much, and it is the price of a half-precision
# operand rather than a mistake. The bounds are a thousandth and five
# hundredths, which leaves room for a device that rounds a tile differently
# without leaving room for a misread layout.

# 2026-08-26, the six-bit k-quant through the device's matrix instruction.
#
# What changed: matrix_product.comp decodes Q6_K as well as Q8_0 and Q4_K,
# which between them are what a "_M" file is made of. A step of thirty-two
# columns is exactly one of the eight runs the format is read in, and an
# invocation's sixteen values share one sub-block scale, so the scale is
# read once rather than per element.
#
# Three alternated rounds in one sitting, one binary with Q6_K refused by
# the kernel against the same binary with it allowed:
#
#   Q4_K_M, 110-token device prompt   0.408 s  ->  0.300 s
#   Q8_0,   110-token device prompt   0.286 s  ->  0.282 s
#   Q5_K_M, 110-token device prompt   1.515 s  ->  1.419 s
#
# better in every round on the two k-quant files and inside the floor on the
# eight-bit one, which it has to be. Digest cbf29ce484222325 throughout.
#
# The four-bit file's day, on the one figure, since all three steps were
# taken on it: 0.891 s on the row product, 0.402 with its four-bit tensors
# tiled, 0.300 with its six-bit ones as well. That is level with the larger
# eight-bit file's 0.280, where it began three times slower.
#
# The five-bit file is the one left out and its number says why: 1.419 s
# against the four-bit file's 0.300, because five sixths of a Q5_K_M file is
# a format the tile does not decode. Tiling its six-bit sixth was worth
# 1.515 to 1.419 and no more.
#
# The shader's own rate, from the same scratch harness, 2048 x 2048 against
# a hundred and twenty-eight vectors with the half-precision copy counted
# against it: 1749 GFLOP/s for Q6_K, 1757 for Q4_K and 1921 for Q8_0 in the
# same run.
#
# The device format test's tiled bound now covers three formats, and what
# they measure against the processor is 7.1e-3, 7.4e-3 and 8.1e-3 -- much
# the same figure for all three, which is what says the difference is the
# half-precision operand rather than any one decode. The row product's is
# still at most 2.1e-5 over every format and both shapes.
#
# The device table under "The device backend" and the llama.cpp comparison
# were NOT re-taken, for the reason the entry above gives and with the same
# control: both are Q8_0, and 0.286 s against 0.282 is the very figure they
# are built from.

# 2026-08-26, the five-bit k-quant through the device's matrix instruction,
# which completes the formats a published model is made of.
#
# What changed: matrix_product.comp decodes Q5_K as well as Q8_0, Q4_K and
# Q6_K. It is the four-bit decode with a fifth bit held in an array of its
# own -- a byte of that array serves all eight sub-blocks at one position,
# one bit each, so the sixteen bytes an invocation reads are the same
# sixteen whichever sub-block the step is and only which bit changes.
#
# Three alternated rounds in one sitting, one binary with Q5_K refused by
# the kernel against the same binary with it allowed:
#
#   Q5_K_M, 110-token device prompt   1.368 s  ->  0.305 s
#   Q4_K_M, 110-token device prompt   0.299 s  ->  0.299 s
#   Q8_0,   110-token device prompt   0.285 s  ->  0.289 s
#
# better in every round on the five-bit file and inside the floor on the two
# controls. Digest cbf29ce484222325 throughout.
#
# Where the three published quantizations of this model now stand on the
# same figure, and where they stood this morning:
#
#                    row product     tiled
#   Q8_0               0.527 s       0.286 s
#   Q4_K_M             0.891 s       0.299 s
#   Q5_K_M             1.368 s       0.305 s
#
# The smaller file is no longer the slower one, which it had been since the
# device could read a k-quant at all.
#
# The shader's own rate, from the same scratch harness, 2048 x 2048 against
# a hundred and twenty-eight vectors with the half-precision copy counted
# against it: 1686 GFLOP/s for Q5_K, against 1845 for Q4_K, 1769 for Q6_K
# and 1950 for Q8_0 in the same run.
#
# The device format test's tiled bound now covers four formats, and what
# they measure against the processor is 7.1e-3, 7.4e-3, 9.5e-3 and 8.1e-3
# for the eight-, four-, five- and six-bit ones -- within a third of each
# other, which is what says the difference is the half-precision operand
# rather than any one decode. The row product's is still at most 2.1e-5 over
# every format and both shapes.
#
# The device table under "The device backend" and the llama.cpp comparison
# were NOT re-taken. Both are Q8_0, and the control above is the figure they
# are built from.

# 2026-08-26, where a device prompt's time actually goes. A measurement and
# no change: the instrument was built, read, and reverted.
#
# The question. After the matrix product the 110-token device prompt reads
# about 0.28 s, and the scratch harness says this model's matrix products
# are about 0.124 s of that in isolation. So more than half of it was
# something else, and two suspects had been named here: the eighty-eight of
# a prompt's hundred and fifty-four products that go one to a submission
# with a fence wait each, and the narrow K and V projections, which run at
# 403 GFLOP/s against 1845 for the wide ones because two hundred and
# fifty-six rows at a thirty-two-row tile is eight workgroups.
#
# How it was measured, and the first instrument was wrong. Skipping a kernel
# and taking the difference does not work here: the kernels feed each other,
# so removing one changes the numbers the others see, and three of the six
# ablations came out SLOWER than the run with everything in it. What works
# is doubling instead of skipping -- these kernels all write rather than
# accumulate, so dispatching one twice with the same inputs leaves the same
# answers and costs exactly its own time again. The submission count does
# not change either.
#
# Total time inside submit-and-wait, minimum of five runs at a load below
# 1.00, against a baseline of 0.177 s:
#
#   the kernel doubled       run      its own cost     share
#   matrix product          0.280 s      0.103 s        58 %
#   attention               0.209 s      0.032 s        18 %
#   half-precision copy     0.180 s      0.003 s         2 %
#   blend                   0.179 s      0.002 s         1 %
#   row product             0.179 s      0.002 s         1 %
#   no dispatches at all    0.020 s      0.020 s        11 %
#
# which accounts for 0.162 of 0.177, and the nine per cent left over is the
# activation going up and the answers coming back inside the same wait.
#
# What it says, and the suspect it kills. The submissions are not the
# problem: two hundred empty ones cost 0.020 s, which is a tenth of a
# millisecond each, against the 0.6 to 2.3 ms a round trip was bracketed at
# when the figure was arithmetic rather than a measurement. Recording and
# submitting the whole prompt is eleven per cent.
#
# The matrix product is fifty-eight per cent, which is where a program that
# has just been given a matrix product ought to be. Its 0.103 s against the
# harness's 0.124 s for the same products one at a time is the engine
# already overlapping the ones it records into a single command buffer.
#
# So what is left to take is the kernel itself. It reads 1845 to 2249
# GFLOP/s on this model's shapes where the other runtime's prompt figure
# implies about 3.2 TFLOP/s, and attention -- eighteen per cent, untouched
# since it was written -- is second. The narrow projections are worth less
# than the number that made them look interesting: K and V together are 0.66
# ms of the 5.63 ms a layer's products take, so a fourfold gain on them is
# five per cent of a prompt.

# 2026-08-26, four attempts at the matrix kernel, none of them kept.
#
# The entry above says the matrix product is fifty-eight per cent of a
# device prompt, so this went after the kernel. All of it is measured in the
# scratch harness at a load below 0.60, minimum of three, on 2048 x 2048
# against a hundred and twenty-eight vectors unless another shape is named.
# The kernel as it stands reads 0.564 ms there.
#
# First, where the kernel's own time goes. Two ablations that keep the
# arithmetic and halve an operand's loads, and one that keeps the loads and
# removes fifteen of sixteen multiply-adds:
#
#   as it is                              0.576 ms
#   four vector matrices loaded, not eight 0.519      the loads are 20 %
#   one weight matrix loaded, not two      0.514      the loads are 22 %
#   one multiply-add, not sixteen          0.314      they are 46 %
#
# which accounts for about ninety per cent; the rest is the decode, the
# barriers and the loop. So the arithmetic is about half and the operands
# about half, and neither is small enough to be ignored or large enough to
# be the answer on its own.
#
# Second, and this is the finding: the wide products of this model are
# short of workgroups rather than short of instructions. The same kernel,
# same tile, against row counts that change nothing but how many workgroups
# there are:
#
#     512 rows,   16 workgroups     890 GFLOP/s
#    2048 rows,   64 workgroups    2694
#    4096 rows,  128 workgroups    2641
#    8192 rows,  256 workgroups    3238
#   16384 rows,  512 workgroups    3462
#   32000 rows, 1000 workgroups    3723
#
# A two-thousand-row matrix is sixty-four workgroups on twelve compute
# units, and it reaches seventy-two per cent of what the same kernel reaches
# with a thousand. The multiply-adds alone, taken from the ablation above,
# run at about 3850 GFLOP/s -- which is what the whole kernel reaches once
# there are enough workgroups to hide the loads behind each other.
#
# Then four things tried, and none of them kept:
#
#   pinning the vector operand to one address    1918 GFLOP/s against 2182
#   pinning the weight operand to one address    1555
#   a squarer tile, 64 x 64, which halves the
#     loads per multiply-add                     0.850 ms against 0.564
#   smaller tiles for more workgroups
#     32 x 64                                    0.670
#     32 x 32                                    1.081
#     16 x 128                                   0.709
#     16 x 64                                    0.715
#   staging the batch tile in shared memory      1.499 ms against 0.580
#
# The last is the most interesting failure. It is the standard shape of a
# matrix kernel -- stream both operands into shared memory, read them from
# there -- and here it is two and a half times worse, because with one wave
# to a workgroup there is nobody to share the staged tile with and the copy
# is pure added cost. It is also consistent with the two pinning results:
# the path this part already has from its cache to the instruction is
# better than anything that can be built on top of it by hand.
#
# One thing did measure better and is not worth taking. The narrow K and V
# projections, 256 rows, read 0.241 ms at a sixteen-by-sixty-four tile
# against 0.358 at the one in use -- a third off. Two of them a layer over
# twenty-two layers is 5.1 ms of a 177 ms prompt, about three per cent, for
# a second pipeline and a second tile shape to keep in step.
#
# What is left, named and not built: splitting the columns across
# workgroups, so a two-thousand-row product is two hundred and fifty-six
# workgroups rather than sixty-four, with a second pass to add the partial
# sums. The scan above says that is worth about 1.2 times on the products,
# which is ten per cent of a prompt, against an extra buffer, an extra
# dispatch and a reduction. It is the only lever the measurements leave.

# 2026-08-26, the column split: built as a prototype, priced, not kept. The
# entry above named it as the only lever left and put it at about 1.2 times
# on the products, a tenth of a prompt. That estimate was wrong and this is
# what it should have said.
#
# What it is. Each workgroup takes a slice of the columns instead of all of
# them, writes its partial sums to a slice of its own, and a second pass
# adds the slices. A two-thousand-row product at split four is two hundred
# and fifty-six workgroups where it was sixty-four. The partial sums are
# summed in a fixed order by a pass rather than by atomics, because an
# atomic add would make the answer depend on the order the workgroups
# happened to finish in, and the digests here are meant to be a property of
# the model rather than of the run.
#
# The matrix dispatch alone, minimum of three at a load below 0.60, no
# summing pass counted:
#
#   split   2048x2048   5632x2048   2048x5632   256x2048
#     1      0.5648      1.3044      1.4942      0.3232
#     2      0.5507      1.2432      1.4381      0.2204
#     4      0.5542      1.2602      1.4206      0.2623
#     8      0.5896      1.4014      1.5120      0.1821
#
# Two and a half to five per cent on the three wide shapes, and forty-four
# per cent on the narrow one, which is the shape that was eight workgroups
# and is now sixty-four. The gain goes where the starvation was.
#
# And then the summing pass, which is what the estimate above left out. It
# reads the slices and writes one of them, so the output traffic is
# multiplied by the split: at split two a pair of five-thousand-row
# projections is 17 MB a layer of reading and writing that did not exist
# before. Measured on the narrow shape, where the slices are small enough
# to be worth it, with the pass in the same submission:
#
#   256 x 2048, split 1, no pass    0.3232 ms
#   256 x 2048, split 4, with pass  0.2052
#   256 x 2048, split 8, with pass  0.2000
#
# So on a narrow product it is 1.6 times, and on a wide one the pass costs
# what the split saves. Applying it only where it pays -- K and V, twice a
# layer -- is 0.247 ms a layer, 5.4 ms over twenty-two layers, against a
# prompt of 177 ms.
#
# Three per cent, which is below the five per cent a whole-prompt
# measurement resolves on this machine. It cannot be demonstrated in place,
# and it costs a result buffer sized by the split, a fifth pipeline, a
# reduction shader, another barrier and a rule for choosing the split. So
# the prototype is not kept.
#
# What would change that answer, and it is worth writing down because it is
# a fact about the model rather than about the kernel: this one has two
# narrow projections a layer out of seven products. A model with a smaller
# key-value width, or more of them, has more of its time in the shape where
# the split is worth 1.6 times, and the arithmetic above would come out
# differently. The prototype is three lines in the shader -- a column range
# from gl_WorkGroupID.z and a slice added to the store offset -- and the
# pass is a dozen more.

# 2026-08-26, the scales worked out once for a call instead of once for
# every strip of the batch.
#
# A batch is swept four vectors at a time, so a 110-token prompt is
# twenty-eight strips, and each of them decoded the same weight scales
# again: one half-precision number a row and block for the eight-bit
# format, and for the k-quants that plus twelve bytes of six-bit fields
# unpacked into eight scales and eight minimums, or sixteen signed
# sub-block scales.
#
# Found by profiling. rows_by_strips is 73.5 per cent of a processor
# prompt, and inside it the machine-code insertion is 43 per cent against
# 57 for the arithmetic around it.
#
# Three alternated rounds in one sitting, medians, better in every round
# for the two k-quants:
#
#   Q4_K_M, 110-token prompt   1.046 s  ->  0.875 s
#   Q5_K_M, 110-token prompt   1.077 s  ->  0.882 s
#   Q8_0,   110-token prompt   0.815 s  ->  0.792 s
#   Q8_0,   sixty-four generated  2.240 s -> 2.233 s
#
# every digest unchanged -- cbf29ce484222325 and 448c2ed68ec342ee. A
# generated token has to be untouched: it goes through a kernel with no
# strips in it.
#
# The eight-bit format's counter is the honest measure of the whole thing:
# 54.9 thousand million instructions became 43.5, and 14.72 thousand
# million cycles became 14.45. A fifth of the work removed bought two per
# cent of the time, which says that kernel was never issue-bound. The
# k-quants did not have a few more instructions but several times as many,
# and there the same removal is worth a fifth of the prompt.
#
# Why the published figures above are NOT re-taken as a set. The eight-bit
# path is what almost all of them are measured on, and the alternated
# rounds above say it moved about three per cent -- inside the five this
# machine resolves. This sitting also ran six to thirteen per cent slower
# than the morning's on unchanged code (0.863 s for the eight-bit prompt
# against the 0.763 published, 0.486 for the twelve-token run against
# 0.459), which is the between-sitting spread this file has recorded for
# months and is larger than the change. Re-taking them here would replace
# numbers that did not move with numbers that moved for another reason.
#
# What is re-taken is the two k-quant prompt figures, because those did
# move and by more than any spread: the four-bit file's 110-token processor
# prompt was published at 0.990 s and reads 0.875 in an alternated sitting
# whose other half read 1.046.
#
# The device figures did not move and could not have: nothing in the device
# path changed. Their fingerprints went stale because the kernels file is
# in every group's source list.

# 2026-08-26, half precision and brain floating point on the device's tile.
#
# What changed: matrix_product.comp decodes F16 and BF16 as well as the four
# block formats. Neither is really a decode -- half precision is already
# what the tile holds, and brain floating point has fewer mantissa bits than
# half precision has, so the widening is exact for every value inside half
# precision's range.
#
